Bramble · Stage 2 · 2 November 2026

Mechanics library

75 mechanics: the 65 from the first round, rewritten with example units, a game where each one shines, and variations, plus 10 new ones added this round, one of them yours. Nothing here is adopted, and your directions are marked on each card with the designer's response. Review notes are folded under each card.

Costs are rough guesses until simulated. Budget notes use only the tier curve and your tolerance bands.

Group A

How much damage a hit does

No. 1

Hunker

NEW
STICKYMEDIUM

When an enemy attacks this badger, it takes only half the damage.

Rules textHunker. When an enemy attacks this, the damage this takes from that attack is halved, rounded up. Damage it takes while it is attacking, splash damage aimed at another unit, reflected damage and damage from effects are taken in full.
Sett DiggerTier 1
Hunters
Hunker.
1 Attack4 Health
Sett WardenTier 3
Hunters
Hunker. Whenever this survives being attacked, your back-row units gain +1 Attack this combat.
2 Attack8 Health
Old BrockTier 5
Hunters
Hunker. Units adjacent to this have Hunker.
4 Attack9 Health
How they play together

Sett Warden goes in F2 or F3, in front of your back-row carries. Every big hit it soaks at half value makes the units behind it hit harder when their turn comes. Old Brock in the middle of the front row gives Hunker to both neighbours, so a row of three badgers halves every attack the enemy makes until one of them falls. Hunker pairs with Shoulder (a bear that takes hits for its neighbours) and with back-row units that gain from waiting, such as Bide.

Why run it

It makes your front row last against one or two huge attackers, which buys time for whatever you are growing behind it.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, you buy Sett Warden and put it in F2, in front of your dormouse and heron. Turn 7, you face a TALL board with a 22/18 Great beast. In the fight, the beast hits your Warden (now 2/14) for 11, not 22, and the Warden lives. Your heron in B1 gains +1 Attack. The beast's next hit would kill it, but your front units have used those turns to kill the beast's feeders, and your heron comes out of Bide at 8 Attack.

Good against

TALL boards that rely on one or two very large hits.

Weak against

WIDE swarms and BURST pings, which lose little or nothing to halving; and the badger takes full damage when it attacks.

Similar to

Like 'defend' stances in turn-based RPGs that halve incoming damage.

Variations
  • Dig In
    Hunker until this attacks for the first time in a combat. After that it takes full damage.
  • Badger Sett (slot effect)
    When you play this, the slot it is in gains 'The unit in this slot has Hunker' for the rest of the game.
  • Thick Hide
    Damage this takes from attacks is reduced by 2, to a minimum of 1. This flips the matchup: good against WIDE, poor against TALL.
Your direction

'Hunker. This is one of the ones I mentioned. This seems cool.' (02h). Direction: example cards.

Designer's response

Followed. Three badgers that build on each other, plus a slot-effect version that uses fixed slots and a flat-reduction version that has the opposite matchup, for comparison.

Open questions
  • Against Peck (X), is each strike halved on its own? Proposed: yes. Rounding up then favours the woodpecker slightly, which is fine counterplay.
  • The order of Hunker, Shell and other reductions should be set once in the rulebook, not on each card.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a Halve handler on a damage query at the unit layer (R1), and the text already limits it to exchange damage as the defender, so splash, thorns and effects land in full. Two small gaps: say Hunker from two sources (Old Brock's aura on a unit that already has Hunker) halves once, and define Sett Warden's 'survives being attacked' as still on the board after that attack's death resolution (9.6.1 step 5). No loop; rounding up means it never stalls alone; one badge, readable.

Budget. Hunker is cheap to price, but all three badgers carry big bodies on top of it; shrink the bodies and keep the text.

  • Sett Digger: edge. T1 1/4: body 5 + Hunker (E89, halve while defending) 0.3 x 4 = 1.2 [guess] = 6.2 vs V(1) 5: +24% (Tier-1 floor +-1 SP = +-20%). Rounding up makes halving weak against Tier-1 hits of 1-2, so it may be nearer 6.0. Change: If the sim agrees: 1/3 (4 + 0.9 = 4.9, -2%).
  • Sett Warden: flag over. T3 2/8: body 10 (+25% before text) + Hunker 0.3 x 8 = 2.4 + about 1.5 survived attacks x +1 Attack to about 2.5 back units x 0.75 (back Attack) x 1.4 (combat buff on back units, T04) = 3.9 [guess] = 16.3 vs V(3) 8: +104% (+-15%). Change: 1/4, and the payoff goes to 'the units behind this' (it stands in F2 or F3 in front of the carries, as in the scene): 5 + 1.2 + about 1.2 survived attacks x 1.5 units x 0.75 x 1.4 = 1.9 = 8.1 (+1%).
  • Old Brock: flag over. T5 4/9: body 13 + own Hunker 0.3 x 9 = 2.7 + Hunker for about 1.43 neighbours (about 5 Health each at turn 8) x 0.3 x 0.7 while Brock lives = 1.5 [guess] = 17.2 vs V(5) 12: +43% (+-15%). Change: 3/6 (9 + 1.8 + 1.5 = 12.3, +3%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3: the damage exchange is no longer equal when this is the defender.

From combat 1. Creatures: Badger. A badger in its sett is the classic animal you can't dig out. Folio: the faded gentry and the Bathhouse Veterans..

No. 2

Antlers

VARIANT of Galaxy's Ranged
TALL

While this stag is attacking, it takes only half the damage.

Rules textAntlers. While this is attacking, damage it takes is halved, rounded up. 'While attacking' means from the start of its attack to the end of the damage exchange (9.6.1 steps 1 to 4), for each of its attacks, extra attacks included. It includes damage reflected at it during the attack. Damage that lands later, such as from an enemy's death effect, is taken in full.
Yearling StagTier 2
Great beasts
Antlers.
3 Attack4 Health
Rutting StagTier 4
Great beasts
Antlers. After this kills a unit, it gains +2 Attack this combat.
6 Attack5 Health
Monarch of the GlenTier 6
Great beasts
Antlers. This attacks twice each cycle.
8 Attack8 Health
How they play together

Rutting Stag is a front carry that wants many trades: each kill makes it bigger, and Antlers means it only pays half for each one. The Monarch's second attack in every cycle doubles how often the halving helps. Antlers likes Bristle defenders less than most attackers do, because the reflected damage is halved too.

Why run it

Your carry can trade into several units in one fight instead of dying to the first big defender.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, you buy Rutting Stag and buff it to 12/14. In the fight it attacks a 10/10 boar: it takes 5, not 10, kills the boar and goes to 14 Attack. Next it hits a 6/8 badger and takes 3. Three trades later it is still standing, where a plain 12/14 would have died on the second.

Good against

Boards of medium units that each hit back hard.

Weak against

Hunker defenders (both sides halve, so trades are slow), random damage effects, and anything that hits it while it isn't attacking.

Similar to

Like a charging knight's lance-and-shield in tactics games, where the attacker takes less counter-damage.

Variations
  • Lowered Antlers
    The first attack this makes each combat, it takes no damage.
  • Charge
    While this is attacking, it takes 3 less damage from each hit, to a minimum of 1.
  • Antler Clash
    Antlers. When this attacks a unit with Antlers or Hunker, neither unit halves damage, and both deal double damage.
Your direction

No reaction to this entry yet. It is the owner's own example (02f): 'You could have something that takes 50% less damage when it's attacking (either it rounds up or down, I don't know).'

Designer's response

Rounded up, so a stag never becomes immune and Antlers against Hunker always finishes.

Open questions
  • Rounding up versus down: up keeps every hit worth at least 1, down lets 1-damage hits do nothing. Proposed: up.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works under 9.6.1 with the time window it now states (steps 1 to 4 of each of its own attacks, extra attacks included), and Bristle's prick lands inside that window, so it is halved as the text says. Monarch's two attacks use 9.4.11 and Rutting Stag's kill trigger is 9.7.2 step 1. Antlers against Hunker is slow but finite because both round up; readable.

Budget. The stags are priced like their bodies plus a small damage cut; the Tier-6 stag is far over because double attack on a big body is expensive.

  • Yearling Stag: edge. T2 3/4: body 7 + Antlers (E89, halve while attacking) 0.3 x 4 = 1.2 [guess] = 8.2 vs V(2) 7: +17% (+-15%). Change: If the sim agrees: 3/3 (6 + 0.9 = 6.9, -1%).
  • Rutting Stag: flag over. T4 6/5: body 11 + Antlers 0.3 x 5 = 1.5 + '+2 Attack this combat after a kill' (about 0.6 kills x about 0.6 later attacks x 2) = 0.8 [guess] = 13.3 vs V(4) 10: +33% (+-15%). The old line priced the same stag without the kill text at +25%. Change: 5/4 (9 + 1.2 + 0.8 = 11.0, +10%).
  • Monarch of the Glen: flag over. T6 8/8: body 16 (+14% before text) + Antlers 0.3 x 8 = 2.4 + attacks twice (Windfury, 0.8 x own Attack = 6.4; Antlers makes the second attack likelier) [guess] = 24.8 vs V(6) 14: +77% (+-15%). Change: 5/5 (10 + 1.5 + 4.0 = 15.5, +11%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3: the exchange is no longer equal when this is the attacker.

From combat 2 (marked variant by the designer). Creatures: Red deer stag. It leads with its antlers and takes the blow on bone. Great beasts front carry..

No. 3

Shrug

NEW
STICKYTALL

Small hits bounce off this bison and do nothing.

Rules textShrug (N). Hits of N damage or less deal no damage to this. A hit ignored this way counts as no hit: it fires no 'when damaged' or 'when hit' effects on either side. Golden: N stays the same (7.3.5, thresholds keep their value).
Bison CalfTier 2
Great beasts
Shrug 1.
2 Attack5 Health
Bison ElderTier 5
Great beasts
Shrug 2.
4 Attack10 Health
Herd MatriarchTier 6
Great beasts
Shrug 2. Start of Combat: give your other Great beasts Shrug 1 this combat.
5 Attack9 Health
How they play together

The Calf is an early wall that 1-Attack tokens can't touch. The Elder ignores tokens and pings outright. The Matriarch spreads Shrug 1 to the rest of the herd, so a whole Great beasts board stops caring about chip damage and BURST.

Why run it

It is a built-in answer to WIDE token boards and Start of Combat pings, the comps that usually beat big slow units.

A game where it shines

Turn 8, your scouting says the lobby has a strong WIDE player with dozens of 1/1 and 2/2 tokens. You buy Bison Elder and put it in F2. In the fight, every token that attacks it bounces off and dies to its 4 Attack hitting back. They have to find one big hitter, and they don't have one.

Good against

WIDE swarms, BURST pings and Peck.

Weak against

One big hitter, SCAM kills, and anything that lowers N.

Similar to

Like damage thresholds in tabletop games (a vehicle that ignores small-arms fire).

Variations
  • Thick Fleece
    The first 3 hits each combat that deal 2 or less to this are ignored.
  • Stubborn
    Shrug 1. Whenever this ignores a hit, it gains +1 Attack this combat.
  • Winter Coat
    Shrug 3 until this takes damage for the first time; then Shrug 0.
Open questions
  • A board with no hit above N can never kill it, so the fight runs to the 200-attack tie (9.10.1). That is the intended counter, but a no-progress rule could end such fights sooner.
  • Should golden double N? The entry assumed yes (Shrug 4); 7.3.5 as written keeps it at 2.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as an Ignore handler (R1), and the text now keeps N on a golden, which matches 7.3.5 (R12) but contradicts the old entry, so the owner should confirm. Needs a ruling on stacking: the Herd Matriarch gives Shrug 1 to a Bison Elder that has Shrug 2, so say the highest N applies, and keep Ignore before Halve in the pipeline or Shrug plus Hunker ignores hits it shouldn't. Stalemate: a board with no hit above N can't kill it and the fight runs to the 200-attack tie (9.10.1); R3's no-progress rule would end it sooner.

Budget. Shrug is worth little because few hits are small; only the Bison Elder needs a smaller body.

  • Bison Calf: in band. T2 2/5 Shrug 1: body 7 + Shrug 1 (E89, ignore hits of N or less: about 0.08 of Tier-2 damage arrives in 1-damage hits) x 5 = 0.4 [guess] = 7.4 vs V(2) 7: +6% (+-15%).
  • Bison Elder: flag over. T5 4/10 Shrug 2: body 14 (+17% before text) + Shrug 2 at about 0.15 x 10 = 1.5 [guess] = 15.5 vs V(5) 12: +29% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: 3/8 Shrug 2 (11 + 1.2 = 12.2, +2%).
  • Herd Matriarch: in band. T6 5/9 Shrug 2: body 14 + own Shrug 2 at about 0.1 x 9 (Tier-6 hits are bigger) = 0.9 + Shrug 1 to about 3 other Great beasts of about 6 Health at 0.05 = 0.9 [guess] = 15.8 vs V(6) 14: +13% (+-15%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3 and 9.6.3: some damage events do nothing at all.

From combat 3. Creatures: Bison. Thick fleece on a huge frame. Folio: the Bison Elders, immovable tanks..

No. 4

Shell

NEW
STICKY

The first hit on this snail each combat deals at most N damage, then the shell breaks.

Rules textShell (N). The first time this takes damage in a combat, that damage is capped at N (N is at least 1). Then the shell breaks for the rest of combat. Any damage breaks it, including a 1-damage ping. A Shell unit with N Health or less still dies to a hit of N, so its Health must be raised above N for the shell to save it.
Garden SnailTier 1
Neutral
Shell 4.
1 Attack3 Health
Snail-Shell TraderTier 2
Neutral
When you play this, give another friendly unit Shell 5.
2 Attack3 Health
Roman SnailTier 4
Neutral
Shell 7. End of Turn: give your other units with Shell +2 Health permanently.
3 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The shell number is a Health target. Garden Snail at 3 Health dies to a capped 4 hit, so it is a trap until you give it 2 more Health; after that it shrugs off the first hit of any size. Roman Snail feeds Health to your other shelled units every turn, so they climb past their N. The Trader puts Shell 5 on a unit that already has more than 5 Health, such as your carry, so the enemy's biggest first swing does at most 5.

Why run it

It is a one-hit wall that rewards you for buffing Health, and it stops the enemy carry's first big swing cold.

A game where it shines

Turn 2, you buy Garden Snail (1/3, Shell 4) and a Health buff, and it becomes 1/5. Turn 4, a 9/9 boar hits it: the hit is capped at 4 and the snail lives at 1 Health, so the boar is held up for a full attack. Turn 6, you play Snail-Shell Trader and give your 6/14 carry Shell 5; the enemy's 20-Attack stag hits it for 5, not 20.

Good against

TALL boards and big single hits, for one hit per unit.

Weak against

Pings and tokens that break the shell cheaply, Peck (the first strike breaks it), and anything when the unit's Health is below N.

Similar to

Like Hearthstone's Divine Shield, but it caps a hit instead of blocking it, so small hits still get through.

Variations
  • Hard Shell (the original)
    No single hit deals more than N damage to this, all combat. Best on a dedicated low-Health tech unit, such as Grove Snail, Tier 3, 1/9, Shell 3: a TALL carry does 3 a hit, while Peck, WIDE and BURST break through.
  • Thick Shell
    Shell (N), but it breaks after the second hit instead of the first.
  • Regrowing Shell
    Shell (N). When this side's attack order starts a new cycle, a broken shell regrows.
Your direction

'Shell seems potentially cool. We could iterate. Maybe a flat number is problematic. Or maybe it operates like Divine Shield in Hearthstone, and the shell breaks after a couple of hits, or maybe just one hit, but it limits the damage to a certain number for the first hit ... If you have a shell unit that has 5 Health but a 7-strength shell, you know you need to buff it to get the power from the shell.' (02h)

Designer's response

Followed: the main text is now the owner's breakable shell, and the units are built around buffing Health past N. Light pushback: the flat cap still works as a narrow tech card (Hard Shell variation), because Peck, WIDE and BURST all break it; it is kept as a variation, not the keyword.

Open questions
  • Golden: with a cap, a bigger N is weaker. Proposed: N stays the same on golden (7.3.5); the golden gets its doubled stats only.
  • Does a hit that Shrug ignores break the shell? Proposed: no, because it counts as no hit.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. The owner's breakable version works as a one-time Cap handler (R1), and keeping N on golden is consistent with 7.3.5. Needs rulings: a second Shell on one unit (Snail-Shell Trader onto a Roman Snail) should keep the lower N or not stack; 'the first time this takes damage' needs an answer for simultaneous hits (Swarm joiners, Close Ranks, Cleave), proposed cap the largest and break; and say whether a Knot share breaks it (proposed no, shares aren't hits). Readability: Garden Snail's printed Shell 4 on a 3-Health body does nothing until buffed, which the owner asked for, but the card should say 'needs more than 4 Health' or new players will read it as broken.

Budget. The owner's first-hit Shell is cheap and fair on the low snails; the Tier-4 Roman Snail is +79% over its tier (band ±25%), and +1 Health a turn instead of +2 brings it back.

  • Garden Snail: in band. T1 1/3 Shell 4: body 4 + Shell 4 is blank until buffed (3 Health is under N, and Tier-1 hits are about 2), about 0.3 for later buffs [guess] = 4.3 vs V(1) 5: -14% (floor +-20%).
  • Snail-Shell Trader: in band. T2 2/3: body 5 + 'give another unit Shell 5' (a one-off keyword grant, E20 at 0.9: blank on turns 2-4, about 1.5-2 on a late carry facing big first hits, about 1.2 averaged) [guess] = 6.2 vs V(2) 7: -11% (+-15%). It grows with the game, so sim it at turns 8-12 too.
  • Roman Snail: flag over. T4 3/6 Shell 7: body 9 + own Shell 7 about 0.5 (6 Health is under N until buffed) + End of Turn +2 Health to about 1.5 other Shell units = s about 3 SP a turn x 0.9 (Health only) = 2.7, over the Tier-4 growth cap of 2.5; c(4) 3.1 x 2.7 = 8.4 [guess] = 17.9 vs V(4) 10: +79% (+-25%, an R03 trigger). Change: 2/4, and End of Turn gives +1 Health (s about 1.5 x 0.9 = 1.35, under the cap; 3.1 x 1.35 = 4.2): 6 + 0.3 + 4.2 = 10.5 (+5%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3: for one hit, the attacker's Attack stops mattering above N.

From combat 4. Creatures: Snail. A shell doesn't care how hard you hit it, only how often..

No. 5

Peck (X)

was PeckNEW
MEDIUMBURST

This woodpecker strikes its target 2 or 3 times in one attack, splitting its Attack between the strikes.

Rules textPeck (X), where X is 2 or 3. When this attacks, it strikes its target X times instead of once, in one turn of the attack order. Its Attack is split among the strikes as evenly as possible, larger parts first. Each strike counts as an attack: this unit's 'whenever this attacks' effects fire before each strike, and each strike is a separate hit. The target deals its damage back once, with the last strike. If the target dies, the remaining strikes are lost.
Lesser Spotted WoodpeckerTier 1
Birds
Peck 2.
2 Attack2 Health
Drumming WoodpeckerTier 3
Birds
Peck 2. Whenever this attacks, give your other Birds +1 Attack this combat.
4 Attack4 Health
Yaffle, the Green WoodpeckerTier 5
Birds
Peck 3. Whenever a friendly unit attacks, deal 1 damage to a random enemy.
6 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Drumming Woodpecker's buff fires once per strike, so each of its attacks gives your other Birds +2 Attack. Yaffle pings once per friendly attack, three times on its own attack, and again for each Drumming strike. Against Shell, the first strike breaks the shell and the rest land in full; against Hunker, each strike is halved separately and rounds up.

Why run it

It doubles or triples your on-attack effects, and it opens one-hit walls such as Shell.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, you buy Drumming Woodpecker and put it in F1 with two Birds behind. Turn 6, you add Yaffle. In the fight, Drumming attacks a snail with Shell 5: strike one (2 damage) breaks the shell, strike two lands 2. Your Birds gain +2 Attack, and Yaffle pings twice. When Yaffle attacks, it pings three more times before its own strikes land.

Good against

Shell, Divine-Shield-style blocking layers, and Hunker.

Weak against

Shrug (small strikes do nothing) and Bristle (it pricks back once per strike).

Similar to

Like multi-hit moves in Pokemon (Double Kick, Fury Attack), which split one action into several hits.

Variations
  • Peck (original)
    Its damage is dealt as that many 1-damage hits, capped at 5 hits, with the rest added to the last hit.
  • Flurry
    This unit's first attack each combat has Peck 3. Later attacks are normal.
  • Scatter Peck
    Peck 2, but each strike picks its own random target among the candidates.
Your direction

'I think 10 is probably far too much, because we'd have balance issues. I think Peck would lend itself to on-attack trigger effects: it takes damage normally, but its attacks count as multiple attacks, maybe 2 or 3 ... which is good against things like Shell ... So we can have a look at Peck X, where X is the number of attacks it splits the damage into.' (02h)

Designer's response

Followed: Peck is now Peck (X) with X of 2 or 3, each strike counts as an attack for on-attack effects, and it takes damage back once, normally. The small X also fixes the trigger-limit problem the rules review found. The uncapped original is kept only as a capped variation.

Open questions
  • Does the target's 'when attacked' effect fire once or X times? Proposed: once, so only the woodpecker's own effects multiply.
  • Should the damage back land with the first strike (the woodpecker may die mid-attack) or the last (proposed)?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a split on the dealing side (R1, E90) inside one turn of the attack order. Needs a ruling: deaths are checked after each strike (9.6.3), so if an early strike kills the target the return blow 'with the last strike' never lands, which makes Peck a partial first strike like Quick; say whether that is intended or move the return blow to the first strike. Also say that 'each strike counts as an attack' is for this unit's own effects only: not for enemy 'when attacked', Bystander or Whistle triggers, and not toward the 200-attack limit (9.10.1).

Budget. Peck X itself is cheap; the two payoff woodpeckers are over because Peck multiplies their 'whenever attacks' text, so their bodies must shrink.

  • Lesser Spotted Woodpecker: in band. T1 2/2 Peck 2: body 4 + Peck about 0.5 (E90 split: gains against Divine Shield, Shell and Hunker, loses against Shrug and Bristle; same as the old line) [guess] = 4.5 vs V(1) 5: -10% (floor +-20%).
  • Drumming Woodpecker: flag over. T3 4/4 Peck 2: body 8 + Peck 0.5 + '+1 Attack to your other Birds this combat' on each strike: about 1.25 attacks x 2 strikes x about 1.8 other Birds x 0.7 realised = 3.2 [guess] = 11.7 vs V(3) 8: +46% (+-15%). Change: 2/3, text unchanged (5 + 0.5 + 3.2 = 8.7, +9%).
  • Yaffle, the Green Woodpecker: flag over. T5 6/6 Peck 3: body 12 + Peck about 0.7 + 1 damage to a random enemy on each friendly attack (about 6 friendly attacks, Peck strikes included, while Yaffle lives x 0.85 x 0.7 for wasted pings at Tier 5 = 4.2) [guess] = 16.9 vs V(5) 12: +41% (+-15%). Change: 4/4 (8 + 0.7 + 4.2 = 12.9, +8%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3 (one damage event per attack) and 9.4.3 (one attack per turn).

From combat 5. Creatures: Great spotted woodpecker. It drums; it doesn't punch..

No. 6

Knot

NEW
WIDEMEDIUM

Damage to one Knotted rat is shared out evenly among all your Knotted rats.

Rules textKnot. When a Knotted unit takes damage, after its own reductions, the damage is split as evenly as possible among all your Knotted units, in either row. Any leftover points go to the unit that was hit. The shares are not new hits: the units that receive them apply no reductions and fire no 'when damaged' effects. This breaks back-row protection on purpose. Units that die because a Knot aura is lost die in the same death check.
Gutter RatTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Knot. When this dies, your other Knotted units gain +1 Attack this combat.
1 Attack2 Health
Knotted RatTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Knot.
2 Attack3 Health
Knot ElderTier 6
Hedgerow folk
Knot. Your Knotted units have +1 Health for each Knotted unit you have.
5 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Five Knotted units with the Elder each have +5 Health, and a 20-damage hit costs each of them 4. When the knot finally fails, many rats die at once, and each Gutter Rat that dies makes the survivors hit harder. The Elder dying is the big moment: everyone loses Health together.

Why run it

A wide board that big hitters can't pick apart one unit at a time.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you have three Knotted Rats and a Gutter Rat in the front row. An enemy 12/12 boar hits one rat: the 12 damage is split 3 each, and nobody dies. Turn 8, you find Knot Elder. Your six rats now have +6 Health each, and the enemy TALL carry needs several swings before anything dies.

Good against

TALL boards that win by killing one unit per big hit.

Weak against

Peck and WIDE boards (every small hit still counts), and destroy effects that skip damage.

Similar to

Like linked enemies that share one health bar in action games.

Variations
  • Linked (Oozes)
    Damage to this is shared only with the friendly units in front of it and behind it.
  • Tied Tails
    When you play this, choose an adjacent unit. Damage to either is split evenly between the two.
  • Frayed Knot
    Shares go only to Knotted units in the same row, so back-row protection holds.
Open questions
  • Overlaps the Ooze 'Linked' idea in archetypes.md 4.1. Pick one, or keep Knot for rats and Linked for Oozes.
  • Riding or underground units: are they in the knot?
  • The old example name, The Rat King, clashes with a Galaxy card; renamed Knot Elder.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag under

Rules. Works as a Share handler at the end of the damage query (R1): shares are not hits, fire no 'when damaged' effects, deliberately pass back-row protection (card text wins, 1.2.1), and aura-loss deaths join the same check. Small gaps: say a share doesn't break a Shell and doesn't feed Scar, and that two Knotted units hit at the same moment (Cleave) split one after the other. Off-slot rats (riding, underground) need R7's occupant table; readable with a drawn rope.

Budget. Plain Knot is priced at about zero, so the vanilla rat needs a bigger body, and the Elder's N-squared aura needs to become linear.

  • Gutter Rat: edge. T1 1/2: body 3 + Knot about 0 net (E91 sharing) + on death, about 2 other Knotted units gain +1 Attack this combat x 0.8 realised x 0.6 chance they are there = 1.0 [guess] = 4.0 vs V(1) 5: -20% (on the Tier-1 floor of +-1 SP). Change: If the sim agrees: 1/3 (5.0, 0%).

Same as the old Rat King. Change: 3/3, 'This has +2 Health for each Knotted unit you have' (linear; Knot spreads that Health to every rat): 6 + 8 x 0.9 = 13.2 at N = 4 (-6%), about 15 at N = 5 (+7%).

Bends. 9.6.3: a unit's Health is no longer only its own, and Knotted units tend to die together.

From combat 13. Creatures: Rats. Folio: the Rat King, 'tails have knotted into one gossiping hive-mind'..

Group B

Damage back to the attacker

No. 7

Bristle

VARIANT of Galaxy's Stinky
STICKYWIDE

Whenever an enemy hurts this hedgehog in a fight, its spines deal damage back.

Rules textBristle (N). Whenever an enemy unit deals damage to this during an attack (when this attacks or is attacked), deal N damage to that unit after the damage exchange and before deaths are checked. So a hedgehog that dies still pricks. Thorn damage (from Bristle, Curl or any other reflect) never triggers thorn damage.
Hedgerow HedgehogTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Bristle 1.
1 Attack3 Health
Hoglet MotherTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Bristle 1. When this dies, summon a 1/1 Hoglet with Bristle 1.
1 Attack3 Health
Hedgehog ApothecaryTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Bristle 3. Your other units with Bristle have +1 Bristle.
2 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Tokens with 1 Health die just by attacking a hedgehog. Hoglet Mother leaves a pricking baby behind, so the wall takes two kills. The Apothecary raises every hedgehog's Bristle, so a front row of them makes each enemy attack cost its attacker 2 or more Health on top of the exchange.

Why run it

It punishes many small attacks, so it is the one wall where swarming is wrong.

A game where it shines

Turn 2, you play Hedgerow Hedgehog and Hoglet Mother in the front row. The enemy has four 1/1 tokens. In the fight, each token attacks a hedgehog, deals 1, and dies to the prick before it can do anything else. Turn 6, with the Apothecary, a Peck 2 woodpecker that strikes your hedgehog takes 2 pricks of 2.

Good against

WIDE token boards, Peck and Swarm.

Weak against

One big hit that kills the hedgehog at once, and Antlers (pricks are halved).

Similar to

Thorns in Diablo and World of Warcraft.

Variations
  • Spines
    Bristle, but the prick goes to a random enemy instead of the attacker (closer to Galaxy's Stinky).
  • Quill Shed
    When this dies, deal N damage to the unit that killed it.
  • Bristle (any damage)
    Also fires when an enemy effect damages this; the unit responsible takes N, or a random enemy if no unit is.
Your direction

No reaction to this entry yet. It is the owner's own idea (02f): 'Something that deals damage back to the attacker whenever it's dealt damage. Maybe the hedgehogs do that: the thorn effect.'

Designer's response

Kept on hedgehogs. The no-thorns-from-thorns rule covers Curl too, which closes the Bristle and Curl loop the rules review found.

Open questions
  • Should Bristle fire on effect damage? It is a dial (the 'any damage' variation).

No. 8

Curl

NEW
STICKY

This hedgehog rolls into a ball: it doesn't attack, it takes half damage, and anything that hits it gets pricked.

Rules textCurl. While another friendly unit can attack, this doesn't attack and damage it takes is halved, rounded down. Whenever an enemy unit or enemy effect hits this, even for 0 damage, the enemy unit responsible takes 1 thorn damage; if no unit is responsible, a random enemy takes it. When no other friendly unit can attack, it uncurls for the rest of combat: it attacks normally and takes full damage.
Pincushion HedgehogTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Curl.
2 Attack6 Health
Hedge DoctorTier 3
Hedgerow folk
Whenever a friendly unit with Curl pricks an enemy, your Hedgerow folk gain +1 Attack this combat.
2 Attack4 Health
Tightly Wound HedgehogTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Curl. Its thorn damage is 3.
4 Attack10 Health
How they play together

Pincushion soaks hits in the front row while the Hedge Doctor turns every prick into Attack for the board. When everything else has died, the curled hedgehogs uncurl with all that Attack and finish the fight. Pings and tokens are the worst attackers into it: they deal 0 and still get pricked.

Why run it

A wall that wins by making the enemy hurt itself, and a late finisher once it uncurls.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you have two Pincushions in F2 and F3 and the Hedge Doctor in B2. The enemy BURST board opens with five 1-damage pings: each deals 0, each pricks a random enemy, and your board gains +5 Attack. Their tokens attack the balls, deal 0 and die to the pricks. When your other units have fallen, the Pincushions uncurl with 7 Attack.

Good against

BURST pings and WIDE tokens.

Weak against

One big hitter: half of 20 is still 10.

Variations
  • Curl (simple)
    This doesn't attack while another friendly unit can. Bristle 1. (Built from existing parts, as the entry suggested.)
  • Brief Curl
    Curl for the first cycle of combat only; then it uncurls.
  • Prickle Ball
    Curl. When this uncurls, it deals damage equal to the pricks it made this combat to a random enemy.
Open questions
  • Overlaps Bristle, Hunker and Bide; it may be better as one unit's text than as its own keyword.
  • Uncurling needs a clear visual cue.
Review notesRules: broken as writtenBudget: flag over

Rules. Broken for two Curl units with no other attackers, which is the designed Pincushion plus Tightly Wound pair: each one waits only while 'another friendly unit can attack', and whether the other can attack depends on the first, so no consistent answer exists (if both can attack neither does, if neither can both do). Fix: 'While another friendly unit without Curl can attack', and give Curl, Bide and Nocturnal one shared 'waiting' status so they don't keep each other asleep (R3). Also note that pricking 'even for 0 damage' is a deliberate exception to R1's rule that a 0 hit fires nothing, and rounding down here conflicts with the round-up halvings elsewhere (question 1).

Budget. Halving damage while never attacking roughly doubles a hedgehog's Health, so every Curl body needs to be about half as big, and the Doctor's team-wide payoff should hit one unit per prick.

  • Pincushion Hedgehog: flag over. T2 2/6: Attack only once uncurled (about 0.4) + halving rounded down about doubles its Health against attacks, 12 effective Health at 0.7 on a unit that doesn't attack = 8.4 (E89, E83) + pricks about 3 hits x 0.85 = 2.5 [guess] = 11.3 vs V(2) 7: +61% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: 1/4 (0.2 + 5.6 + 2 = 7.8, +11%).
  • Hedge Doctor: flag over. T3 2/4: body 6 + '+1 Attack to your Hedgerow folk this combat' on every Curl prick. In the build it serves (2 Curl units, about 6 pricks a fight, 4 Hedgerow folk) that is 6 x 4 x 0.5 realised = 12 [guess] = 18 vs V(3) 8: +125% (+-15%). With one Curl unit about 8.3 (+4%); the price is the committed build's. Change: Each prick gives +1 Attack to one random other Hedgerow folk: 6 + 6 pricks x 0.85 x 0.5 = 8.6 (+7%) in a committed build.
  • Tightly Wound Hedgehog: flag over. T5 4/10, thorns 3: Attack only once uncurled (about 0.8) + about 20 effective Health at 0.7 = 14 + pricks about 3.5 hits x 3 x 0.7 = 7.4 [guess] = 22.2 vs V(5) 12: +85% (+-15%). Change: 3/5 with thorns 3 (0.6 + 7 + about 2.5 hits x 3 x 0.7 = 5.3 = 12.9, +7%).

Bends. 9.4.5: a unit with Attack sits out the attack order. 9.6.1 step 3: the exchange stops being equal.

From creatures 1. Creatures: Hedgehog rolled into a ball. Folio: hedge-apothecaries who carry cures on their spines..

Group C

Not attacking, and stopping attacks

No. 9

Bide

NEW
TALL

This unit skips its attacks and grows each time, until it wakes up and strikes.

Rules textBide. While this waits, it skips its attacks. Each time its side's attack order passes over it, it grows. It stops waiting, for the rest of combat, when its wake condition is met or when no friendly unit that isn't waiting can attack. Each Bide card names one wake condition: - Screen: it waits while a friendly unit stands in a slot in front of it (8.2.1), and gains +2 Attack this combat each time it waits. Only back-row slots have slots in front, so in the front row it never waits. - Slumber: it waits until it takes damage, and gains +1/+1 this combat each time it waits.
Hazel DormouseTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Bide (Slumber).
1 Attack3 Health
Grey HeronTier 3
Birds
Bide (Screen). When this stops waiting, it attacks immediately.
1 Attack5 Health
Seven Sleepers' DormouseTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Bide (Slumber). It gains +2/+2 instead each time it waits.
3 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The heron sits in the back row behind two tough front units, such as Hunker badgers or Shell snails. The longer they hold, the bigger it gets, and when its screen falls it strikes at once. Dormice sleep in the back row, where attacks can't reach them while the front lives, and wake only when the fight finally comes to them.

Why run it

A back-row carry fed by time instead of deaths, which makes a sturdy front row pay twice.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, you buy Grey Heron and put it in B2, behind Sett Warden in F2 and a Bison Calf in F3. In the fight, the heron waits through four cycles while the badger and bison hold, reaching 9 Attack. When the Calf dies, its screen breaks, and the heron attacks immediately for 9.

Good against

Slow boards that take many cycles to break your front row.

Weak against

Boards that break the screen fast (it wakes small) and early pings (one ping wakes a dormouse at once, since random damage ignores rows).

Similar to

Like Bide in Pokemon (wait two turns, then strike back), and charge-up attacks in many games.

Variations
  • Bide (N)
    This skips its first N turns, gaining +3 Attack this combat each time. Then it attacks normally. A fixed countdown.
  • Hibernate
    When you play this, it sleeps for 2 turns: it can't attack in those combats. Then it wakes with +6/+6 permanently. (A time-bound Recruit Phase version, per pillar 13.)
  • Patient Strike
    Bide (Screen). When this stops waiting, its next attack deals double damage.
Your direction

'The Bide stuff's interesting. I like that. Kind of cool. We could explore that.' (02h)

Designer's response

Followed with example cards. The wake rule now reads 'no friendly unit that isn't waiting can attack', so two Bide units can't wait for ever. The waiting is inside one fight and ends by itself, so it stays on the right side of pillar 13; the Hibernate variation tries the Recruit Phase version the owner called cool ('Don't move for 3 turns, then X', 02i).

Open questions
  • Grey Heron's 'attacks immediately' can fire in the middle of death resolution when its screen dies. Proposed: it attacks after death resolution ends.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a declined turn: the unit is skipped and flagged (shared rule 2), and the fallback 'no friendly unit that isn't waiting can attack' prevents a lone heron from stalling. Needs a ruling that Curl and Nocturnal count as waiting under that fallback, or a Bide heron and a Curl hedgehog can each wait on the other (R3). Grey Heron's 'attacks immediately' on waking can fire inside death resolution, so queue it after the death pass and before the 9.9.1 check (R5).

Budget. Bide fits the budget once the heron's body is trimmed; the Tier-1 dormouse sits just over the line.

  • Hazel Dormouse: edge. T1 1/3 Slumber, back: body 3.75 + about 1.5 waits at Tier 1 (short fights) x +1/+1 on a back unit, net of skipped attacks = 2.3 (R32) [guess] = 6.05 vs V(1) 5: +21% (floor +-20%). The old line priced it at Tier 2 (-2%); it moved down a tier. Change: If the sim agrees: 1/2 (about 5.0, 0%).
  • Grey Heron: flag over. T3 1/5 Screen, back: body 5.75 + about 2 waits x +2 Attack as a combat buff on a back unit (x 1.4) = 4.2, + waking attack 0.5, - back-row attacks it gives up 0.7 = 4.0 [guess] = 9.75 vs V(3) 8: +22% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: 1/3 (3.75 + 4.0 = 7.75, -3%).
  • Seven Sleepers' Dormouse: edge. T5 3/6 Slumber +2/+2, back: body 8.25 + 2 waits x +2/+2 = 7.2, - 1.5 for skipped attacks [guess] = 14 vs V(5) 12: +16% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Assumes the growth lasts this combat only. Change: If the sim agrees: 3/5 (about 13, +8%).

Bends. 9.4.5 and 9.4.6: a unit able to attack declines to, and the pointer moves past it. 9.4.2: the back row's reserve role becomes a wind-up.

From rows 12, combat 8. Creatures: Grey heron, which stands still, then stabs (Folio: first-strike lancer). Hazel dormouse, which sleeps over half the year; the Marmot Cantons' hibernators who wake stronger..

No. 10

Whistle

NEW
STICKYMEDIUM

This marmot never attacks; on its turn it whistles a warning, and the biggest enemy's next attack deals half damage.

Rules textWhistle. This never attacks, even with 0 Attack. When its turn in the attack order comes, it whistles instead, and that uses its side's turn. A whistle warns of the highest-Attack enemy unit that isn't already warned of (ties at random): that unit's next attack deals half damage, rounded up, to everything it hits, splash included. Then the warning is used up. Whistles don't count as attacks for ending combat: a side whose only units have Whistle can't attack (9.9.1).
Marmot LookoutTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Whistle.
0 Attack4 Health
Whistle-Watch MarmotTier 3
Hedgerow folk
Whistle. When an attack you warned of doesn't kill the unit it hit, give that unit +2 Health this combat.
1 Attack5 Health
Alpine ElderTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Whistle. Each whistle warns of the two highest-Attack enemies instead of one.
2 Attack7 Health
How they play together

Put the marmot in the back row, where it can't be attacked while your front lives. Every time its turn comes, instead of a weak attack, it takes the edge off the enemy's best hitter. Whistle-Watch adds Health to whoever survives the softened hit, so your wall gets tougher each time. It stacks with Hunker: a 24 hit halved by the whistle and again by Hunker is 6.

Why run it

Your back row can protect your front row instead of sitting idle.

A game where it shines

Turn 6. Your opponent is TALL: a 24/20 Rutting Stag and a few small feeders. You have Sett Warden (Hunker, 3/14) in F2, a Bison Calf in F3 and Marmot Lookout in B2. In the fight, your front units attack first; then your marmot's turn comes, and it whistles at the stag. The stag attacks your Warden: 24, halved by the whistle to 12, halved by Hunker to 6. The Warden lives. Next cycle the marmot whistles again. Without the marmot the Warden takes 12 a hit and dies on the second; with it, the Warden survives two hits and your front row has time to kill the feeders.

Good against

TALL boards with one huge hitter.

Weak against

WIDE boards with many similar attackers (each whistle softens only one attack), and any board that kills your front row fast.

Similar to

Like a support character's 'defend ally' or 'weaken' action in turn-based RPGs: it spends its turn reducing an enemy hit instead of attacking.

Variations
  • Alarm Call (original)
    The next enemy attack, whoever makes it, deals half damage. Simpler, but often wasted on a token.
  • Lookout Post
    This never attacks. Enemy attacks against the units in front of this deal 2 less damage, to a minimum of 1. (No timing to follow: a plain aura.)
  • Sentry Call
    Whistle, but instead of halving, the warned enemy's next attack must target your friendly unit with the most Health.
Your direction

'Whistle. I don't quite understand it. I'd need a bit more of an example.' (02h). Direction: example cards and a play scene.

Designer's response

In one line: it is a back-row unit that spends its turns protecting instead of hitting. To make that easy to picture, the whistle now aims at the highest-Attack enemy (the predator the marmot spots), rather than whichever enemy happens to attack next, which was often a token. The original is kept as a variation, and Lookout Post is a version with no timing at all.

Open questions
  • Does whistling use the side's turn (proposed, so it is a real trade) or come free, as shared rule 2 suggests?
  • Should a warning still pending at the end of a cycle carry over? Proposed: yes, until that enemy attacks.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works with its explicit override of 9.4.6 (it takes a turn even at 0 Attack) and its own end-of-combat line, so two Whistle-only boards end as a tie (9.9.1). Needs rulings: whether a whistle counts toward the 200-attack limit (9.10.1), whether a warned Peck unit halves only its first strike or the whole attack (proposed whole attack), and that a lone marmot still whistles while the enemy keeps attacking. The card reads as one sentence ('On its turn: the strongest enemy's next attack deals half'), which should answer the owner's 'need more of an example'.

Budget. A whistle saves half of the enemy's biggest hit, which grows with the tier, so the Tier-3 and Tier-5 marmots need smaller bodies.

  • Marmot Lookout: in band. T2 0/4: body 4 + about 3 whistles a fight x about 1 damage saved x 0.8 = 2.4 (E89 on the enemy's next hit, R32) [guess] = 6.4 vs V(2) 7: -9% (+-15%). Same as the old line.
  • Whistle-Watch Marmot: flag over. T3 1/5: Attack only on retaliation (about 0.5) + 5 + 3 whistles x about 2.5 saved (half of a Tier-3 top hitter's hit) x 0.5 used well x 0.8 = 3.0 + '+2 Health this combat' after about 2 survived warned attacks x 2 x 0.9 x 0.6 = 2.2 [guess] = 10.7 vs V(3) 8: +34% (+-15%). Change: 1/3 (0.5 + 3 + 3.0 + 2.2 = 8.7, +9%).
  • Alpine Elder: flag over. T5 2/7: retaliation about 1 + 7 + about 2.5 whistles x 2 enemies x about 4 saved (half of a Tier-5 top hitter) x 0.45 used well x 0.8 = 7 [guess] = 15 vs V(5) 12: +25% (+-15%). The saving grows with the size of the enemy's biggest hit. Change: 1/5 (0.5 + 5 + 7 = 12.5, +4%).

Bends. 9.4.5 and 9.4.6: a unit takes a turn in the attack order to do something other than attack. 9.6.1: one enemy attack's damage is halved.

From combat 9, creatures 13. Creatures: Alpine marmot. Sentries whistle an alarm from the rocks. Folio: whistle-sentries who raise the alarm at everything, including clouds..

No. 11

Mire

NEW
MEDIUMSTICKY

An enemy that this toad damages gets stuck in the mud and skips its next attack.

Rules textMire. An enemy unit that takes damage from this, whether this attacks it or it attacks this, skips its next turn in the attack order; the pointer passes to its side's next unit. Mire doesn't stack: a unit already stuck stays stuck once. Extra attacks out of turn ('attacks immediately') are not skipped.
Mudwallow ToadTier 2
Bog folk
Mire.
2 Attack4 Health
Fen NewtTier 3
Bog folk
Ambush (whenever an enemy back-row unit attacks this, this deals its Attack to the attacker first).
2 Attack5 Health
Bogland MatronTier 5
Bog folk
Mire. Your other Bog folk have Mire.
3 Attack7 Health
How they play together

Every enemy front-row unit that trades with a toad loses its next turn, and that turn passes to its side's next unit. With the front row stuck, more of the enemy's attacks come from its back row: support units and carries that would rather not trade. Fen Newt is waiting for exactly those attackers and hits them first. The Matron spreads Mire across your whole Bog folk board.

Why run it

It shuts down a carry for a turn, and with the right partners it drags the enemy's fragile back row into bad trades.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, you buy Mudwallow Toad and put it in F2. Turn 5, the enemy's 10/10 bear attacks the toad and gets stuck: next cycle it skips, and the enemy's B1 owl (4/3) takes that turn instead. It attacks your Fen Newt, takes 2 first, then trades and dies. Your toad mired the bear and your newt ate the back row.

Good against

TALL carries (one stuck turn costs them a lot) and boards with attacking back-row units.

Weak against

Quick attackers that kill the toad first, and WIDE boards, where one skipped attack matters little.

Similar to

Like Freeze in constructed Hearthstone (the frozen unit loses its next attack).

Variations
  • Sink
    An enemy damaged by this has -3 Attack until after its next attack.
  • Deep Mire
    The first enemy this damages each combat skips its next two turns.
  • Mud Slot (slot effect)
    When you play this, its slot gains: 'Start of Combat: the enemy across from this slot skips its first turn.'
Your direction

'Mire's interesting. That's cool, because it makes their backline attack into you. I like that as a tech-card effect. And you could have effects for when the enemy's backline attacks, so you have some counterattack damage or something; you could build into that.' (02h). Then: 'I was just kind of exploring the idea ... If it seems like I'm not asking you to do anything with it, I'm not.' (02i)

Designer's response

No change to Mire. The owner's picture partly holds as written: Mire moves nobody, but each skipped front-row turn passes to the next unit, so more of the enemy's attacks come from its back row. Fen Newt is one 'effect for when the enemy's backline attacks', and Ambush is pitched as its own new mechanic below.

Open questions
  • Is it intended that attacking a toad mires the attacker (the damage back counts)? Proposed: yes; that is what makes it anti-TALL.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a skip on the enemy's next turn: when chosen to attack, a stuck unit is flagged, loses the mark, and its side picks its next unit or passes (9.4.10), so it can't stall a fight. The text already covers no stacking and out-of-turn attacks; add that Mire still applies when the toad dies in the same exchange. Fen Newt depends on the new Ambush mechanic's ruling; readable.

Budget. Each skipped enemy attack is worth most of an enemy unit's Attack, so the Tier-5 toad that spreads Mire is well over and needs a small body.

  • Mudwallow Toad: edge. T2 2/4: body 6 + about 1.1 enemies a fight survive its damage and lose an attack x A(2) 2.5 x 0.8 = 2.2 (E93) [guess] = 8.2 vs V(2) 7: +17% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: If the sim agrees: 2/3 (7.2, +3%).
  • Fen Newt: in band. T3 2/5 Ambush (no Mire): body 7 + about 0.6 enemy back-row attacks on it x first strike (Divine Shield's 3.0 x about 0.4 chance its 2 damage kills the attacker) + a little for blunting back-row hitters = 1.0 [guess] = 8.0 vs V(3) 8: 0% (+-15%).
  • Bogland Matron: flag over. T5 3/7: body 10 + own Mire about 1.1 x A(5) 4.9 x 0.8 = 4.3 + Mire for about 2 other Bog folk, about 0.8 skipped attacks each x 4.9 x 0.8 x 0.6 while she lives = 3.8 [guess] = 18.1 vs V(5) 12: +51% (+-15%). Change: 2/4 (6 + about 3.0 on fewer exchanges + about 3.0 as she dies sooner = 12, 0%).

Bends. 9.4.5: the enemy's attack order is changed from outside.

From combat 10. Creatures: Common toad and the Bog folk. Mud and bog water slow anything that wades in. Folio: the Fen Tigers, who sabotage and flood..

No. 12

War Dance

NEW
SCAM

Instead of its first attack, this stoat dances, freezing the enemy's strongest unit, then strikes it for double damage.

Rules textWar Dance. Instead of its first attack each combat, this dances, which uses its turn. The enemy unit with the highest Attack (ties at random) is entranced: it can't attack until this unit attacks again or dies. This unit's next attack must target the entranced unit, in either row, and deals double damage. If the entranced unit can't be targeted when that attack comes, this attacks normally and the trance ends.
Weasel FeintTier 1
Hunters
Instead of its first attack each combat, the highest-Attack enemy is entranced until this attacks again or dies. (The dance without the strike.)
1 Attack2 Health
Ermine DancerTier 4
Hunters
War Dance.
3 Attack4 Health
Prima of the Ermine CourtTier 6
Hunters
War Dance. Your other units also deal double damage to the entranced unit.
5 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The dance holds the enemy carry still for about a cycle. Your other units use that time to clear its feeders and guards, and then the stoat's strike lands on the carry wherever it stands. The Prima turns the whole board into the strike. Weasel Feint is the cheap early version that just buys a cycle.

Why run it

It switches off the enemy's best unit for a cycle and then hits it hard, even in the back row.

A game where it shines

Turn 7, you fight a 30/30 boar carry. You have Ermine Dancer, now 9/8, in F3. In the fight, the stoat dances and the boar stands entranced while your front row kills two of its feeders. On the stoat's next turn it strikes the boar for 18. Your next two attackers finish it.

Good against

TALL boards with one carry.

Weak against

Pings and fast front units that kill the stoat before its second turn, and WIDE boards with no single big unit.

Similar to

Like stun or sleep effects in RPGs.

Variations
  • Hypnotic Dance
    The trance lasts until this dies, but there is no double damage.
  • Pair Dance
    Entrances the two highest-Attack enemies; no double damage.
  • Courtship Display
    Instead of its first attack, the highest-Attack enemy must attack this unit on its next turn.
Open questions
  • Double damage applies before Peck splits the damage (a Peck stoat doubles first).
  • If the stoat is Mired, the trance lasts longer; both still end.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works under 9.4.5 if an entranced unit counts as unable to attack, so a side whose only unit is entranced passes and 9.9.1 can end a fight where neither side can attack (R3). Needs a ruling for two dancers: the second dance picks the same highest-Attack enemy, so say each dance picks the highest enemy not already entranced, and that each trance ends with its own dancer. Long for a card; the Weasel Feint half is the readable core.

Budget. Taking the enemy's best attack off the board is worth a lot at high tiers, so the Tier-6 dancer needs a much smaller body; the lower two sit near the line.

  • Weasel Feint: edge. T1 1/2: body 3 + the highest-Attack enemy (about 3 at Tier 1) loses about one attack x 0.8 x 0.8 (it would sometimes attack after the weasel died anyway) = 1.6 (E93) - its own skipped first attack 0.7 [guess] = 3.9 vs V(1) 5: -22% (floor +-20%). Change: If the sim agrees: 1/3 (4.9, -2%); more Health also keeps the trance up longer.
  • Ermine Dancer: edge. T4 3/4: body 7 + the entranced carry loses about one attack (about 5) + double-damage strike about 2.5 - its own skipped first attack about 2.5 = 5 (E93, E90) [guess] = 12 vs V(4) 10: +20% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: If the sim agrees: 3/3 (about 11, +10%).
  • Prima of the Ermine Court: flag over. Group D

When something else is attacked

No. 13

Bystander

was AlibiVARIANT of Battlegrounds' Champion of Y'Shaarj and Arm of the Empire
WIDEMEDIUM

Whenever an enemy attacks one of your other units, this fox grows.

Rules textBystander. Whenever an enemy unit attacks one of your other units, this gains +1/+1 this combat.
Fox NotaryTier 1
Hunters
Bystander.
1 Attack3 Health
Fox WitnessTier 3
Hunters
Bystander. Your other Foxes also gain the +1/+1.
3 Attack3 Health
Reynard, EsquireTier 5
Hunters
Bystander. At the end of combat, if this was never attacked, it keeps half the stats Bystander gave it this combat (rounded down), permanently.
4 Attack5 Health
How they play together

In the back row, a fox is never attacked while your front lives, so it grows from every enemy attack. Tough front units (Hunker badgers, Shoulder bears, Shell snails) mean more enemy attacks before the front falls. Reynard turns a good fight into permanent stats, but only if nothing ever reached him.

Why run it

A back-row carry that grows from the enemy's attacks, so your sturdy front row feeds it.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, you put Reynard, Esquire in B2 behind two Hunker badgers and a bear. In the fight, the enemy attacks your front row nine times before it falls, and Reynard reaches 13/14. He finishes the last two enemies and is never touched. After the fight he keeps +4/+4 permanently.

Good against

Slow boards that need many attacks to get through your front.

Weak against

BURST and random damage that reach the back row, and anything that attacks the fox directly (Swoop, Grudge, Rival).

Variations
  • Alibi (original)
    Whenever an enemy attacks and this was one of the units it could have targeted, but it chose another, this gains +1/+1. Rewards a front-row fox that was lucky; harder to see.
  • Hideout
    Whenever a friendly unit is attacked while this can't be targeted, this gains +1/+1. Back row only.
  • Onlooker
    Whenever any unit, friendly or enemy, is attacked, this gains +1 Attack this combat.
Your direction

'Alibi is interesting. I'm not sure it's the right name for it, but that's cool. Maybe have Alibi just be "whenever an enemy unit attacks a different unit", and rename it to something else, rather than having it be that complicated.' (02h)

Designer's response

Followed: the trigger is now the owner's simpler one, and the keyword is renamed Bystander. Other names: Onlooker, Lie Low, Wallflower, Not Me. The original candidate-list version is kept as a variation. The simpler trigger also fixes the rules review's problem that a back-row fox could never be a candidate. The Clerk is renamed Fox Notary to avoid the name clash with the Play Dead example.

Open questions
  • When Shoulder redirects an attack aimed at a fox onto a bear, does the fox count as 'one of your other units' being attacked? Proposed: the trigger reads the final defender, so yes for every fox except one that ends up the defender.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Simple and readable: one trigger on enemy attack declarations, reading the final defender after any Shoulder redirect, as the designer proposes. Peck's strikes should count once here (see Peck). Reynard, Esquire should say 'if this survived and was never attacked', since a fox killed by an effect was never attacked; the permanent write to its own recruit board is allowed (9.1.1, R9).

Budget. The renamed trigger fires on almost every enemy attack, so +1/+1 per trigger is expensive; +1 Attack per trigger and smaller bodies keep the fox idea in band.

  • Fox Notary: edge. T1 1/3: body 4 + about 1.5 triggers in Tier-1 fights (1-3 units, often none beside it) x +1/+1 this combat x 0.65 realised = 2.0 (R33) [guess] = 6.0 vs V(1) 5: +20% (on the floor of +-1 SP). Change: If the sim agrees: 1/2 (5.0, 0%).
  • Fox Witness: flag over. T3 3/3: body 6 + own Bystander about 3.5 triggers x +1/+1 = 4.5 + other Foxes (about 1.5 x 3 triggers x 2 SP x 0.7) = 6.3 [guess] = 16.8 vs V(3) 8: +110% (+-15%). The simpler trigger fires more than Alibi did, so this is if anything low. Change: 1/2 in the back row, where it lives longest, and Bystander plus the shared gain give +1 Attack instead of +1/+1: 0.75 + 2 + 2.5 + 3.2 = 8.5 (+6%).
  • Reynard, Esquire: flag over. No. 14

Shoulder

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Elistra the Immortal and Taunt
STICKYTALL

Once per enemy cycle, this bear steps in and takes an attack aimed at a unit beside it.

Rules textShoulder. Once per enemy cycle, when an enemy attacks a unit adjacent to this, this becomes the defender instead, before any 'when attacked' effects. The enemy cycle resets when the attacking side's attack order starts again. If two Shoulder units could take the attack, the first in reading order does. A gap next to this (8.2.2) means no neighbour to guard.
Bear CubTier 2
Great beasts
Shoulder.
2 Attack5 Health
Bear WardenTier 4
Great beasts
Shoulder. Whenever this takes an attack for another unit, it gains +1/+2 this combat.
3 Attack9 Health

Great beasts
Shoulder, with no once-per-cycle limit.
6 Attack12 Health
How they play together

Put the bear between two units you want kept alive, such as a front-row carry and a Hunker badger. Bear Warden grows each time it steps in. Old Bruin takes every attack aimed at its neighbours, so a TALL player can put a carry in F2 next to it and the carry is almost never hit until Bruin falls.

Why run it

It guards your front-row carry without moving it to the back row.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your carry is a 15/10 Rutting Stag in F3. You put Bear Warden in F2, next to it. In the fight, the first enemy attack each cycle that targets the stag hits the bear instead, and the bear grows to 5/13. The stag keeps attacking at full strength.

Good against

Boards whose attacks land randomly on your carry.

Weak against

Many attackers per cycle (only one is redirected), and gaps: a bear in F1 guards only F2.

Similar to

Like bodyguard or 'cover' abilities in tactics games (Final Fantasy's Cover).

Variations
  • Shield Bash
    Shoulder. When this takes an attack for another unit, deal 3 damage to the attacker first.
  • Interpose
    Once per combat, when an enemy effect would damage an adjacent unit, this takes the damage instead.
  • Den Mother
    Shoulder, but it guards the units behind it once the back row can be attacked.
Open questions
  • A bear that takes the attack and then dodges it (Jink) makes the attack miss: intended?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. The redirect works if it sits in a separate defender step after must-be-attacked (R6), before 'when attacked' effects as the text says. Needs a ruling that a redirected attack is never redirected again: if a defender change counts as a new 'attacks a unit adjacent to this', two adjacent Old Bruins (no once-per-cycle limit) pass one attack back and forth until the 10,000-trigger tie (9.10.2), and even two Bear Cubs bounce it once each. The 'enemy cycle' needs R4's per-side cycle counter; readable as 'takes hits for its neighbours'.

Budget. Shoulder itself is cheap; the two bigger bears are over mostly on body size.

  • Bear Cub: in band. T2 2/5: body 7 + Shoulder (E91: Taunt's 0 plus the neighbour it guards) about 0.5 [guess] = 7.5 vs V(2) 7: +7% (+-15%).
  • Bear Warden: flag over. T4 3/9: body 12 (+20% before text) + Shoulder 0.5 + '+1/+2 this combat' on about 2 redirected attacks x 3 SP x 0.6 realised = 3.6 [guess] = 16.1 vs V(4) 10: +61% (+-15%). The old line priced the bear without the growth at +25%. Change: 2/5 (7 + 0.5 + about 2.9 as it falls sooner = 10.4, +4%).
  • Old Bruin: flag over. T6 6/12: body 18 (+29% before text) + Shoulder with no limit, a full Taunt for both neighbours, about 2 (top of E91) [guess] = 20 vs V(6) 14: +43% (+-15%). Change: 4/8 (12 + 2 = 14, 0%).

Group E

Choosing targets and reaching the back row

Grudge

NEW
SCAM

This rook remembers whoever hurt it, attacks them first, even in the back row, and hits them harder.

Rules textGrudge. When an enemy unit damages this, by attack or effect, that enemy is marked for the rest of combat. When this attacks, it must target a marked enemy if one can be targeted, in either row (at random among several), and it deals +2 damage to marked enemies. Marks stay on units that move. A unit that dies and comes back is a fresh copy with no mark.
Jackdaw WitnessTier 1
Birds
Grudge.
1 Attack3 Health
Rook MagistrateTier 3
Birds
Grudge.
3 Attack4 Health
Speaker of the ParliamentTier 5
Birds
Grudge. Your other Birds also attack marked enemies first and deal +2 damage to them.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

A rook in the front row gets marked attackers: whatever hits it, it hits back on its turn with +2. If an enemy back-row unit pings the rook or attacks it, the rook flies straight to it. The Speaker shares the marks with every Bird, so one hit on any rook points your whole flock at that enemy.

Why run it

It punishes the enemy for hitting it, and it lets your Birds reach back-row units that touch you.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, the enemy has a back-row owl that pings at Start of Combat. It pings your Rook Magistrate. The owl is marked, and on the rook's turn it attacks the owl in the back row for 5 and kills it. Turn 8, with the Speaker, every Bird follows the mark.

Good against

BURST pings from the back row, and back-row attackers.

Weak against

Being hit only by tokens, which wastes the grudge, and boards that don't hit the rook first.

Similar to

Like threat or aggro in MMOs, turned around: the one who hurt you is the one you go after.

Variations
  • Vendetta
    When a friendly unit dies, the enemy that killed it is marked.
  • Hold a Grudge
    Marked enemies take +2 damage from all your units; no change to targeting.
  • Remember Faces
    Start of Combat: mark the enemy with the highest Attack.
Open questions
  • A marked unit that can't be targeted falls back to normal targeting (R6).
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: in band

Rules. Works as reach plus bonus damage, with R6's fallback stated for marked units that can't be targeted. Needs a ruling on whose marks they are: the text says 'a marked enemy', so a mark made by one rook already steers every rook, which makes the Speaker's 'your other Birds also attack marked enemies' only useful for non-Grudge Birds; say marks belong to the marking side. Retaliation damage marks the defender too, which is probably intended; readable with a mark badge.

Budget. Grudge is in band at every tier as written.

  • Jackdaw Witness: in band. T1 1/3: body 4 + 2 bonus damage x about 1 attack x 0.5 chance a marked enemy lives x 0.85 = 0.85 + reach 0.3 (E90, E83) [guess] = 5.15 vs V(1) 5: +3%.
  • Rook Magistrate: in band. T3 3/4: body 7 + bonus damage about 1.3 + back-row reach about 0.5 [guess] = 8.8 vs V(3) 8: +10% (+-15%). Same as the old line.
  • Speaker of the Parliament: in band. T5 4/6: body 10 + own Grudge about 1.8 + about 2 other Birds attacking marked enemies for +2 (about 1 attack x 0.6 x 2 x 0.85, x 0.6 while it lives = 1.2, plus reach 0.4) [guess] = 13.4 vs V(5) 12: +12% (+-15%).

Bends. 9.5.1 (back-row protection) and 9.5.5 (random target).

How they play together

Where you place a robin is where you aim it. In B1 or B2 it duels the enemy back-row carry that sits across from it, ignoring the front row. Double damage both ways means the robin usually dies, but it often takes something much bigger with it. The Redbreast grows each time it wins a duel.

Why run it

A cheap piece that reaches into the enemy's back row and trades up.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you notice the lobby's strong players keep their carry in B2. You put Robin Redbreast in B2. In the fight, it duels an 8/8 back-row owl: it hits for 6 and the owl hits it for 16. Good against

Boards with one carry in a predictable slot.

Weak against

Gaps across from it (no Rival), and big Rivals with high Attack that kill it first.

Similar to

Like duel cards in Magic (Fight effects), aimed by board position.

Variations
  • Turf
    Start of Combat: the enemy across from this must attack only this until one of them dies.
  • Fair Duel
    Rival without double damage. If this kills its Rival, it gains the Rival's Attack this combat.
  • Song Duel
    Start of Combat: this and the enemy across from it each lose Attack equal to the other's, this combat.

  • In async snapshot play you can't see where the enemy carry is; the duel is a guess. Keep numbers small.

Rules. Works as a Start of Combat bond with forced reach (R6), and the fallback for a Rival that can't be targeted is stated. Needs rulings: two robins across from each other become mutual Rivals, so say the double damage applies once, not four times; and the bond reads the board when it resolves, so random Start of Combat side order decides it against Flush or Changeling (R8). The duel line is readable one at a time; 'Turf' as a variation name clashes with the new Turf mechanic in batch 3.

Budget. Rival fights fit, but Robin Redbreast's permanent +2/+2 per kill is +104% over Tier 2 (band ±25%); +1 Attack per kill keeps the snowball slower.

No. 17

Nocturnal

VARIANT of Galaxy's Sneak
SCAMTALL

This unit waits out the first round of attacks, then hunts the weakest enemy anywhere on the board.

Rules textNocturnal. This doesn't attack in your side's first attack cycle. From your second cycle on (“night falls”), it attacks the enemy unit with the lowest Health, in either row. Ties are random. If that unit can't be attacked (for example it is behind a Shelter), it attacks the next lowest. If every unit on your side that can attack is Nocturnal, your first cycle ends at once and night falls.
Little Owl UsherTier 1
Birds
Nocturnal.
2 Attack3 Health
Tawny MagistrateTier 3
Birds
Nocturnal. After this kills a unit, it gains +1/+1 permanently.
5 Attack2 Health
Eagle-Owl JusticeTier 5
Birds
Nocturnal. When night falls, your other Nocturnal units gain +3 Attack for this combat.
6 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The owls sit in the back row during the first cycle, while the front rows trade and leave units wounded. When night falls they each pick off the lowest-Health enemy, often a damaged front unit or a fragile back-row buffer. The Magistrate grows from each kill it lands, so pairing it with Start of Combat pings (BURST) or splash makes its targets easier. The Eagle-Owl turns the moment night falls into a spike for every owl.

Why run it

It kills the enemy's support pieces that normally hide safe in the back row, and it finishes wounded units instead of bouncing off a fresh tank. It also stays out of harm for a cycle, so a low-Health carry like the Magistrate survives long enough to act.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your opponent has been winning with a 1/3 Nest Robin in their back row that buffs their front line every turn. You buy a Tawny Magistrate and put it in B2. In the fight, cycle 1 goes by and your owl does nothing while the front rows trade. Then night falls: the Magistrate ignores their 4/7 front tank and drops on the Robin in B1, the lowest-Health enemy, and kills it. It survives the 1-damage return blow and grows to 6/3 for good.

Good against

Boards that hide fragile engines or buffers in the back row, and boards whose front row is already wounded.

Weak against

Fast fights that end in one cycle, and high-Health boards where nothing is ever low; Shelter blocks its reach.

Similar to

Galaxy's Sneak reaches the back row; Nocturnal waits first and then targets the weakest anywhere.

Variations
  • Crepuscular
    This attacks only in your first and last cycle: it skips every cycle after the first until it is the last unit on your side that can attack.
  • Night Watch
    This doesn't attack in your first cycle. When night falls, it gains +X/+X for this combat. (Waits, but keeps normal targeting.)
  • Early Bird
    In your first cycle only, this attacks the enemy with the lowest Health in either row. After that it attacks normally.
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I kept the idea and fixed the review's gap: a side of only Nocturnal units skips straight to night, so its cycle can't stall.

Open questions
  • Does night fall per side (each side's own second cycle) or once for the whole fight? Proposed: per side, since 9.4.5 keeps a pointer per side.
  • The UI needs to show cycles for this to be readable. Is a visible “night falls” moment acceptable in the fight presentation?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works with R4's per-side cycle counter, which the designer proposes, and the text solves R4's stall itself: a side of only Nocturnal units ends its first cycle at once. Lowest-Health reach with a 'next lowest' fallback fits R6 (protection beats reach). Readable if the fight shows a 'night falls' moment, which Nightfall needs too.

Budget. Nocturnal's wait and its reach roughly cancel, so the owls are near budget; only the Tier-5 owl needs a smaller body.

No. 18

Shiny

NEW
SCAMTALL

This thief goes straight for enemy golden units, wherever they stand, and steals stats from them.

Rules textShiny. If the enemy has any golden units, this attacks one of them (chosen at random), in either row.
