Jackdaw LookoutTier 1
Birds
Start of Combat: if the enemy has a golden unit, your Shiny units gain +2 Attack for this combat.
1 Attack3 Health
Fence MagpieTier 3
Birds
Shiny.
3 Attack3 Health
Magpie HoarderTier 4
Birds
After a friendly unit steals in combat, you get 1 extra gold next turn (at most 2 gold per combat).
2 Attack5 Health
How they play together

The Lookout spots goldens at Start of Combat and sharpens the magpies, so the Fence Magpie's first hit on a golden lands harder. Every time a magpie connects it grows for good, and the Hoarder turns each theft into gold for next turn. Against a lobby with no goldens the magpies are plain 3/3s, so they are a tech package you pick up when someone is ahead.

Why run it

It is a tax on the player who is far ahead: the bigger their golden pile, the more you take. Your magpie grows permanently from fights with the leaders, and the Hoarder pays you for it.

A game where it shines

Turn 8, the lobby leader has a golden 12/12 Bison in their back row behind three front units. You pick up a Fence Magpie and a Jackdaw Lookout. In the fight, the Lookout gives the magpie +2 Attack; it flies over the front row, hits the golden Bison for 5, steals +1/+1 and dies to the return blow. Next turn your magpie is 4/4 for good, and the Bison's owner lost that fight by one unit.

Good against

Players who are triple-rich and stack stats on golden units.

Weak against

Lobbies without goldens, and golden units big enough to kill the magpie in one return blow before it steals again.

Similar to

Like a catch-up tool in racing games (the blue shell in Mario Kart): it aims at whoever is ahead.

Variations
  • Covet
    This attacks the enemy with the most Attack above its card's printed Attack (the most buffed unit). Whenever it damages that unit, steal +1/+1 as Shiny does. Works without goldens.
  • Snatch
    Start of Combat: steal +2/+2 from a random enemy golden unit. (A BURST version: no attack needed, but no growth beyond the steal.)
  • Thieves' Honour
    Shiny, and this never attacks enemy units that are Shiny. (The old golden rule: thieves don't rob each other.)
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I took the review's fix: the victim loses the stats for this combat only, and only the magpie's gain is permanent. I moved the golden magpie's special rule into a variation.

Open questions
  • Can the steal reduce a golden enemy's Attack to 0 (so it stops attacking)? Proposed: yes, it is an ordinary combat debuff.
  • Should golden chasing (archetypes.md 1.3) get more answers like this, or is one magpie line enough?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: edge

Rules. Works: the steal is permanent only on its own unit and combat-only on the snapshot, which fixes R9's impossible write. Add R6's fallback for when every golden is protected (a Shelter in front of it), and say a steal that takes Health can kill. The Hoarder's gold reads a combat result back into the Recruit Phase, which needs R9's combat report; readable.

Budget. Shiny is near budget; its value rises as goldens get commoner, so sim it late.

  • Jackdaw Lookout: in band. T1 1/3: body 4 + '+2 Attack to your Shiny units if the enemy has a golden': about 0.15 chance of an enemy golden on turns 1-3 (C08) x about 1 Shiny unit x 2 x 0.8 = 0.25, about 1.2 if held to mid-game [guess] = 4.25 vs V(1) 5: -15% (floor +-20%).
  • Fence Magpie: edge. T3 3/3: body 6 + permanent steal (about 0.5 hits on goldens a fight x 2 SP = 1 SP a fight, under the cap; c_combat 2.5 x 1 = 2.5) + the golden's loss 1.1 + golden-seeking reach 0.4 [guess] = 10 vs V(3) 8: +25% (+-25%, scaling). Same as the old line; it rises late as enemy goldens get commoner. Change: If the sim agrees: 3/2 (about 9, +12%).
  • Magpie Hoarder: in band. T4 2/5: body 7 + about 1 gold next turn in a build with two Shiny units, conditional recurring gold (2.2: +1 a turn at Tier 4 = 4.2) [guess] = 11.2 vs V(4) 10: +12% (+-25%, economy). For information: it can pay up to 2 gold a turn, over power-budget 4.1's proposed Tier-4 cap of 1 (not approved).

Bends. 9.5.1: back-row protection is ignored, but only for golden targets. 7.3: being golden becomes something the enemy can aim at. The victim's loss lasts the combat only, because the enemy is a recorded snapshot (2.1.4, 9.1.1).

From creatures 4.. Creatures: Magpie and jackdaw. Magpies can't resist shiny things; the Folio's Shiny Syndicate “can't resist anything shiny, including evidence”..

No. 19

Flush

NEW
SCAM

Before the fight, this drags an enemy back-row unit out into their front row, where it can be attacked.

Rules textFlush has two shapes. Flush (swap): Start of Combat: the enemy back-row unit with the highest Attack swaps places with the unit in front of it that has the lowest Health (ties: the left one). If no unit is in front of it, nothing happens. Tongue (pull): Start of Combat: the enemy back-row unit with the lowest Health moves into the nearest empty slot in their front row (nearest by the 8.4.2 order from its slot). If their front row is full, nothing happens. Ties for highest Attack or lowest Health are random. Later Start of Combat effects see the new board.
Natterjack ToadTier 1
Bog folk
After an enemy unit is moved into their front row, deal 1 damage to it.
1 Attack3 Health
Bullfrog TenorTier 3
Bog folk
Tongue.
3 Attack4 Health
Burrow WeaselTier 3
Hunters
Flush. This unit's first attack this combat targets the unit it flushed, if that unit is still alive.
3 Attack3 Health
How they play together

The Bullfrog pulls the enemy's flimsiest back unit into the open, and the Natterjack nicks it for 1 on arrival, so it reaches the fight already hurt. The Weasel works the other way: it drags their biggest back-row carry forward by swapping it with a weak front unit, then attacks it first. Both break the enemy's position text, because a buffer that was “behind” its tank is now beside it.

Why run it

The back row is where players keep their engines and carries. Flush lets your front row reach them on the first attack, without needing a whole tribe of back-row reach.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, your opponent's board is a wall of three front units and a 2/2 Nest Robin and a 7/4 back-row carry. You buy a Bullfrog Tenor and a Natterjack. At Start of Combat the Tenor's tongue yanks the Robin into the empty F4 and the toad hits it to 2/1. Your first attacker kills it. Next turn you add a Burrow Weasel, which swaps their 7/4 carry with their weakest front unit and goes for it on its first attack.

Good against

Boards that hide a carry or a fragile engine in the back row.

Weak against

Against Tongue, a full enemy front row; against Flush, no units in front of the carry. The two shapes have opposite defences.

Similar to

Like the hook or pull champions in MOBAs (Blitzcrank in League of Legends, Pudge in Dota 2): drag the target out of safety.

Variations
  • Ferret Out
    Start of Combat: swap the enemy back-row unit with the highest Attack with the enemy unit across from this, if both exist.
  • Long Tongue
    Tongue, and the pulled unit can't be the target of its own side's buffs this combat.
  • Beat the Bushes
    Start of Combat: every enemy back-row unit with 2 Health or less moves into an empty enemy front slot, if there is one.
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I kept both shapes under one entry and added tie rules and the nearest-slot rule from the review. The two shapes give opposite counterplay, which is the interesting part.

Open questions
  • Should Start of Combat effects that already resolved (the enemy's auras) re-read the board after a Flush? Proposed: no; only effects that resolve later see the new board (9.3.4 needs a line).
  • Is one entry with two shapes right, or should Tongue be its own mechanic?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: in band

Rules. Both shapes are precise moves that 8.3.5 allows ('effects that say so'). Needs a ruling in 9.3.4 that each Start of Combat effect reads the board as it stands when it resolves, which the designer proposes, so random side order (9.3.2) decides some outcomes (R8). Two Flush units can swap a pair back; that is finite but should be shown clearly, and Tongue may read better as its own keyword.

Budget. Flush is in band as written; the Tier-1 toad is blank until a mover joins it.

  • Natterjack Toad: in band. T1 1/3: body 4 + 1 damage to enemies moved into their front row, about 0.2 (it needs a Flush or Tongue unit, Tier 3) [guess] = 4.2 vs V(1) 5: -16% (floor +-20%). Blank without a mover.
  • Bullfrog Tenor: in band. T3 3/4 Tongue: body 7 + pull about 2.5 (E94) x about 0.4 chance of an empty enemy front slot = 1 [guess] = 8 vs V(3) 8: 0%. Same as the old line; blank against full boards.
  • Burrow Weasel: in band. T3 3/3 Flush: body 6 + bringing their back carry forward about 2.5 (E94) + first attack on the flushed unit about 0.5 (E83) [guess] = 9 vs V(3) 8: +12.5% (+-15%).

Bends. 8.3.5: an effect moves an enemy unit. 9.5.1: back-row protection is beaten by moving the target, not by reaching over the front row (11.5.3). 8.2.4: the moved unit's “in front of” and “behind” text now points at new slots.

From rows 10, creatures 17.. Creatures: Weasels, stoats and ferrets hunt inside burrows (ferreting drives rabbits out of the warren). Frogs and toads snatch prey from a distance with a sticky tongue..

Group F

Protection and dodging

No. 20

Shelter

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Taunt
STICKYTALL

A big front unit that keeps the back-row units behind it safe from anything that reaches over the front row.

Rules textShelter. While this lives, the units in the slots behind it can't be attacked, even by attacks that may reach the back row (such as Swoop, Nocturnal or Shiny). Effects that pick a random enemy or hit a group still reach them. “Behind” is 8.2.1: one slot for F1 and F4, two for F2 and F3.
Bison YearlingTier 1
Great beasts
While a unit with Shelter is in front of this, this has +2 Attack.
1 Attack3 Health
Red Deer HindTier 2
Great beasts
Shelter.
2 Attack5 Health
Białowieża BisonTier 4
Great beasts
Shelter. When an effect would damage a unit behind this, this takes the damage instead.
3 Attack9 Health
How they play together

Put the Bison in F2 or F3 and it covers two back slots. The Calves and your back carry sit there, safe from Swoop birds and owls, and the Bison even soaks random pings aimed at them. The Hind is the early version for F1 or F4, covering one slot. The Calf rewards you for building the column: it hits harder while it is sheltered.

Why run it

Reach mechanics (Swoop, Nocturnal, Shiny, Flush) are how other players get at your back-row carry. Shelter is the answer, and it gives Great beasts a gameplay identity that matches the flavour: big animals guarding the young.

A game where it shines

Turn 7, two players in your lobby run Birds whose Swoop units dive at your back-row 10/10 Ooze. You buy a Białowieża Bison and put it in F2, in front of the Ooze in B1. In the fight their Swoopers have to hit your front row instead, and a Start of Combat ping aimed at the Ooze hits the Bison's 9 Health. The Ooze lives through the fight and wins it.

Good against

Back-row reach: Swoop, Nocturnal, Shiny and the targeted effects that pick a back-row unit.

Weak against

Plain front-row pressure that kills the Shelter unit first; once it falls, the column opens. Flush can still drag a sheltered unit out.

Similar to

The bodyguard role in many tactics games: a big unit that covers the fragile one standing behind it.

Variations
  • Herd
    Shelter, and adjacent units with Herd also shelter the slots behind them while this lives.
  • Brace
    Shelter until this first takes damage; then it loses Shelter. (Cheaper, breakable cover.)
  • Stand Over
    While this lives, the units behind it take half damage (rounded down) from every source.
Your direction

On Great beasts as a tribe (02h): “I don't really see how it connects to the gameplay ideas and how they're fleshed out here with the great beasts.”

Designer's response

Not a reaction to Shelter itself, but Shelter is one answer to it: the herd guarding its calves is a gameplay idea the flavour explains. I cut the review's redundant clause (“even after the rest of your front row has fallen”) and settled the Bison's damage text: an effect can still pick the calf, but the Bison takes the damage.

Open questions
  • Does Shelter protect against Flush and Tongue moving the sheltered unit? Proposed: no; moving isn't attacking, and Flush is one counter to Shelter.
  • Does the Bison's redirect apply to damage from group effects (“all enemies”)? Proposed: yes, so it can take two hits at once.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as protection that beats reach (R6), and it can't stall a fight because the Shelter unit itself stands in the front row and is attackable. The Białowieża Bison's redirect of effect damage should say it takes each redirected hit separately (two group hits means two hits). Moving isn't attacking, so Flush beats it, which is a good counter; readable.

Budget. Shelter matters only against reach, so it is cheap; only the big bison is over, on body size.

  • Bison Yearling: in band. T1 1/3, back: body 3.75 + '+2 Attack while a Shelter unit is in front' (2 x 0.75 x about 0.25 chance on its turns) [guess] = 4.15 vs V(1) 5: -17% (floor +-20%).
  • Red Deer Hind: in band. T2 2/5: body 7 + Shelter about 0.5: while it lives the front row isn't empty, so it only adds protection against reach attacks (Nocturnal, Shiny, Grudge, Rival) [guess] = 7.5 vs V(2) 7: +7% (+-15%).
  • Białowieża Bison: flag over. T4 3/9: body 12 (+20% before text) + Shelter about 2 (it also soaks effect damage meant for the units behind; E91) [guess] = 14 vs V(4) 10: +40% (+-15%). Same as the old line. Change: 3/6 (about 11, +10%).

Bends. 9.5.1: back-row protection works by column as well as by row. 11.5.3: a counter to back-row reach, so reach doesn't become a must-have.

From rows 13 (marked variant by the designer).. Creatures: Bison, the “immovable tank”. A herd turns to face a threat with the calves behind it. Red deer stags do the same..

No. 21

Thicket

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Taunt
STICKYTALL

A back-row unit that grows a bramble hedge in every empty front slot in front of it, so the enemy has to cut through the hedges first.

Rules textThicket. Start of Combat: each empty front slot in front of this grows a Thicket with 3 Health. A Thicket is a hedge, not a unit. It can be attacked, and it counts as a front-row unit for back-row protection (9.5.1), but it never attacks. It doesn't count for winning or losing (a side left with only Thickets has lost), attack order, who attacks first, hero damage, random enemy effects, Cleave, or “adjacent” text. A summon into a Thicket's slot clears the Thicket; for summon placement (8.4) the slot counts as empty. Thickets have no death effects.
Bramble WardenTier 2
Greenwood
Thicket. Units that attack your Thickets take 1 damage.
1 Attack4 Health
Briar SpriteTier 3
Fairies
While a Thicket is in front of this, this has +2 Attack.
4 Attack2 Health
Blackthorn KeeperTier 5
Greenwood
Thicket. Your Thickets have +3 Health. Whenever one of your Thickets is cut down, your back-row units gain +1/+1 for this combat.
3 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Put the Warden in B2 and leave F2 and F3 empty. At Start of Combat two 3-Health hedges appear in those gaps. The enemy must attack your front row, which is now mostly hedge, so their first few attacks are spent cutting brambles (and taking 1 damage each from the Warden) while your back-row units attack freely. The Sprite hits harder while a hedge stands in front of it, and the Keeper makes the hedges tougher and pumps your back row each time one is cut.

Why run it

It turns empty front slots, which are normally a weakness, into a wall. It suits a board with a few strong back-row units (a TALL carry or a buffing engine) that doesn't want to spend gold on front-row bodies.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you have a strong 6/6 carry in B1 and only two front units in F1 and F4. Gaps in F2 and F3 normally mean little. You buy a Bramble Warden and put it in B2, behind F2 and F3. In the fight two hedges grow in F2 and F3. The enemy's 3/3 cuts the first hedge and takes 1 damage; their next attacker hits the second hedge. Meanwhile your carry and the Warden have attacked twice each. By the time the hedges are gone, the enemy has two units fewer than you.

Good against

Many small attackers and Tongue (which needs empty front slots: hedges fill them).

Weak against

Cleave, Trample and splash cut through brush, and Swarm spends one joint attack to clear a hedge. Hedges do nothing for a board that fills its front row.

Similar to

Like the Wall-nut in Plants vs. Zombies: a wall that only soaks hits, placed in the lanes your attackers leave open.

Variations
  • Hedge (Recruit Phase)
    Spell: plant a Hedge on an empty front slot (a slot effect, 8.5). At Start of Combat, if that slot is still empty, a 3-Health Thicket grows there. The player sees the hedge on the board while recruiting.
  • Lure (Lights)
    Start of Combat: each empty front slot in front of this fills with a Wisp (0 Attack, 1 Health). Enemies must attack Wisps before other front-row units. (The decoy-lights version.)
  • Overgrowth
    Thicket. At the start of each of your cycles, each of your Thickets regrows +1 Health, up to its maximum.
Your direction

“I'm not really sure about Thicket. I'd have to see more on that. I don't necessarily love it yet, but I still don't understand it well.” And on Lights (02h): “Decoy lights that fill empty slots and draw attacks: I'm interested to see what that would look like.”

Designer's response

I kept Thicket as written and added a concrete board and turn so it can be judged. In short: hedges are walls in your front-row gaps that the enemy must cut through, and they never count as units. I also give a Recruit-Phase version (a hedge you plant and can see while recruiting) and the Lights decoy version the owner asked to see.

Open questions
  • Should hedges be visible during the Recruit Phase (the planted variation) for readability?
  • Do Thickets count for Swarm's joint attack as a target? Proposed: yes, they are attack targets like any front-row unit.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag under

Rules. The text answers R7 for hedges almost fully: front-row cover yes, attack order, win, hero damage, random effects and adjacency no. Needs rulings: cutting a Thicket should also not count as a kill ('after this kills', Gorge, Grudge marks), two Wardens behind one empty slot should grow one hedge, and the Warden's 1 damage to attackers should carry the thorn tag (R2). The play scene (Warden in B2, F2 and F3 left empty) makes it understandable; the planted 'Hedge' variation would make it visible while recruiting.

Budget. Thicket is priced for the turns with gaps; the Tier-3 sprite is under because its bonus rarely applies.

  • Bramble Warden: edge. T2 1/4, back: body 4.75 + about 1.2 Thickets a fight on turns 2-4 x (3-Health decoy 2.1 + 1 prick 0.85) = 3.5 (E97) [guess] = 8.25 vs V(2) 7: +18% at its turns (+-15%). Same as the old line; blank on a full board. Change: If the sim agrees: 1/3 (about 7.25, +4%).
  • Briar Sprite: flag under. T3 4/2, back: body 5 + '+2 Attack while a Thicket is in front' (2 x 0.75 x about 0.4 chance on turns 4-6) = 0.6 [guess] = 5.6 vs V(3) 8: -30% (+-15%). Change: 5/3 (3.75 + 3 + 0.6 = 7.35, -8%).
  • Blackthorn Keeper: edge. T5 3/6, back: body 8.25 + about 0.5 Thickets a fight on its turns, 6 Health each as decoys (0.7 x 6 = 4.2) = 2.1, + '+1/+1 to your back row' per Thicket cut (about 3 units x 1.75 x 0.9 x 1.4 = 6.6) x 0.5 = 3.3 [guess] = 13.65 vs V(5) 12: +14% (+-15%). More with other Thicket units; blank on full boards. Change: If the sim says it is over in a Thicket build, 3/5.

Bends. 9.5.1: back-row protection can come from terrain, not only units. 9.9.1 and 9.2.1: Thickets don't count as units. Killing a Thicket isn't a death, so it feeds no death triggers.

From rows 9 (marked variant by the designer).. Creatures: Brambles themselves, the game's name. Greenwood folk grow hedges over their burrows; hedgehogs and dormice live in them..

No. 22

Jink

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Divine Shield
STICKY

The first time this is attacked, it darts sideways into an empty slot and the attack misses.

Rules textJink. The first time each combat this is attacked, if a slot adjacent to it in its row is empty, it darts into that slot (the right one if both are empty) and the attack misses: no damage either way. Darting doesn't use up its attack: it keeps whether or not it has attacked this cycle. Random effects, group effects and splash can't be dodged.
Jack HareTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Jink.
2 Attack2 Health
Brown Hare CourierTier 3
Hedgerow folk
Jink. After this darts, it attacks the unit that missed it.
3 Attack4 Health
Warren MatronTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Your Jink units can dart once more each combat.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

A hare needs an empty slot beside it, so you build the board with gaps: hares in F1 and F3 with F2 and F4 open, for example. Each hare dodges one attack, and the Courier punishes the miss with a free counterattack. The Matron gives every hare a second dodge, but each dart uses up a gap, so placement decides how many dodges you get.

Why run it

It makes gaps worth having and rewards careful placement. A row of hares wastes the enemy's attacks.

A game where it shines

Turn 2, you have a Jack Hare in F2 and a unit in F4, leaving F1 and F3 empty. In the fight the enemy's 3/2 attacks the hare; it darts right into F3 and the attack misses. Your hare survives the first exchange and kills their next attacker. On turn 6 you replace it with a Brown Hare Courier, which dodges their big front unit and then bites it.

Good against

Big single hitters and boards that rely on a few strong attacks.

Weak against

Random pings and splash, and full boards (yours or a summon that fills your gaps). Its moves can break your own adjacency text.

Similar to

Like a dodge or evade chance in many RPGs, but certain and visible from the board instead of random.

Variations
  • Zigzag
    Jink, but it can dart into any empty slot in its row, not only an adjacent one.
  • Scrape
    The first time each combat this is attacked, if the slot behind it is empty, it drops into that back slot and the attack misses.
  • Swerve
    The first time each combat this is attacked, if a slot next to it is empty, the attack misses (it doesn't move).
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I settled the review's open points: right if both slots are empty, and a dart doesn't cost the hare its attack.

Open questions
  • If a redirect (Shoulder) makes the hare the defender, does it still dart? Proposed: yes; it is attacked either way.
  • Does a dart onto a slot with a slot effect (8.5) pick that effect up? Proposed: yes, slot effects apply to whoever stands there.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: the dart happens in 9.6.1 step 1 and the 'defender gone' line ends the attack, and keeping the flag is a stated exception to 9.4.9. Brown Hare Courier's counterattack is an out-of-turn attack that should queue after the missed attack ends (R5). Picking up a slot effect on landing follows 8.5.1; readable, and placement decides how many dodges you get.

Budget. Jink is cheap because it needs a gap; the Tier-3 hare is just over and fits at 3/3.

  • Jack Hare: in band. T1 2/2: body 4 + one Divine-Shield-like block 1.8 x about 0.6 chance of an empty slot beside it on turns 1-4 = 1.1 (E20, E32) [guess] = 5.1 vs V(1) 5: +2%. Same as the old line.
  • Brown Hare Courier: flag over. T3 3/4: body 7 + Jink 3.0 x about 0.5 chance of an empty neighbour slot = 1.5 + attacking the attacker after a dart (about 3 x 0.8 x 0.5) = 1.2 [guess] = 9.7 vs V(3) 8: +21% (+-15%). Change: 3/3 (6 + 1.5 + 1.2 = 8.7, +9%).
  • Warren Matron: in band. T5 4/6: body 10 + a second dart for about 2 Jink units (4.4 x about 0.3 chance of an empty slot late x 0.7 chance the first dart happened) = 1.8 [guess] = 11.8 vs V(5) 12: -2% (+-15%). Blank on full boards.

Bends. 9.6.1: an attack whose defender is gone ends without damage; here the defender leaves on purpose. 8.3.5: units change slots in combat. 9.4.9: a dart is an exception to flagging on moving to a lower slot.

From creatures 15 (marked variant by the designer).. Creatures: Hare. A hare doesn't fight; it jinks, turning sharply sideways at full speed..

Group G

Attack order and timing

No. 23

Headstart

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Heroic Broodmother
BURST

This attacks once before the fight proper, but it tires and loses 1 Attack every time it attacks.

Rules textHeadstart. After Start of Combat and before either side's first attack, this makes one attack. Then it attacks in normal order. Each time it attacks, it loses 1 Attack for this combat (after the attack). Headstart attacks don't move the attack pointer (as 9.4.12). If both sides have Headstart units, the side that attacks first goes first; within a side, reading order.
March HareTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Headstart.
3 Attack1 Health
Starter's RookTier 2
Birds
Your Headstart units have +2 Attack for their Headstart attack.
2 Attack3 Health
Racing HareTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Headstart. If this kills a unit with its Headstart attack, it doesn't lose Attack for that attack.
5 Attack4 Health
How they play together

The hares open the fight by picking off a token or a weak front unit before anyone else moves. The Rook makes that first strike much harder, so a March Hare can take out a 5-Health unit on turn 2. The Racing Hare doesn't tire if its opening shot is a kill, so it rewards picking fights it can win.

Why run it

It strips a layer before the fight starts: a shield, a decoy, a token or a buffer. That changes who wins the trades that follow.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, your opponent's board leans on a 2/4 front unit that buffs its neighbours. You have a March Hare and buy a Starter's Rook. In the fight, before anyone attacks, the hare hits the buffer for 5 and kills it. The hare dies to the return blow, but their board loses its buffs before the first trade.

Good against

Boards that rely on one key front unit, tokens or a protective layer.

Weak against

A hedgehog with Bristle in front (the fable: the hare dies on the spikes), and long fights where it has faded.

Similar to

The hare in Aesop's “The Tortoise and the Hare”; mechanically like a first-turn ambush.

Variations
  • Tortoise
    This starts each combat with 0 Attack and gains +1 Attack each time an enemy attacks. (The mirror: slow, then strong.)
  • False Start
    Headstart, but its Headstart attack targets the enemy's leftmost front-row unit.
  • Sprint (time-bound)
    Headstart. Each turn you don't move this, it gains +1 Attack permanently, up to 3 turns; moving it resets the count.
Designer's response

No owner reaction. Kept as written, with the ordering rule from the review. The Sprint variation is a time-bound version in the spirit of pillar 13.

Open questions
  • Does a Headstart attack count toward who attacks first afterwards? Proposed: no; the normal first attacker still goes first after the Headstart attacks.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as an out-of-turn attack that leaves the pointer alone (9.4.12). Needs a ruling when both sides have Headstart units: 'the side that attacks first goes first' doesn't say whether that side makes all its Headstart attacks before the other side starts, or whether the sides alternate; proposed alternate, as in 9.4.1. The Attack loss after each attack is clear; readable.

Budget. Headstart fits at low tiers; the Tier-4 hare needs a smaller body.

  • March Hare: in band. T1 3/1: body 4 + an extra attack before the fight about A(1) 2 (E83) - the fade about 0.5 (E15) [guess] = 5.5 vs V(1) 5: +10%. Same as the old line.
  • Starter's Rook: in band. T2 2/3: body 5 + '+2 Attack on the Headstart attack' for about 1.5 Headstart units x 2 x 0.85 x 0.6 chance you have one = 1.5 [guess] = 6.5 vs V(2) 7: -7% (+-15%).
  • Racing Hare: flag over. T4 5/4: body 9 + Headstart attack about A(4) 4.0 - fade about 0.6 (the kill clause saves some) = 3.4 [guess] = 12.4 vs V(4) 10: +24% (+-15%). Change: 4/3 (7 + 3.4 = 10.4, +4%).

Bends. 9.2.1 (who attacks first) and 9.4.2 (attack order).

From combat 15.. Creatures: Hare. The fastest runner who tires and loses the race (Folio: “Losing racers, forever beaten by hedgehogs”)..

No. 24

Quick

NEW
TALLSCAM

In a fight between two units, this strikes first; if that kills the other unit, it takes no damage back.

Rules textQuick. In an exchange (attacking or defending), this deals its damage first. If that kills the other unit, this takes no damage from it. Splash from this unit lands with its strike. Bristle on a unit this kills still fires. If both units are Quick, the exchange is simultaneous. Limits are set per card, not in the keyword: the high-tier version is Quick while this is undamaged, and the low-tier version is First Bite (Quick in this unit's first exchange each combat).
Stoat War-DancerTier 2
Hunters
First Bite (this is Quick in its first exchange each combat).
3 Attack2 Health
Weasel SecondTier 3
Hunters
Start of Combat: the unit in front of this gains First Bite for this combat.
2 Attack4 Health
Ermine DuellistTier 5
Hunters
Quick while this is undamaged.
7 Attack5 Health
How they play together

The War-Dancer wins its first trade against anything with 3 Health or less and walks away unhurt, then fights normally. The Weasel Second hands a single First Bite to whatever stands in front of it, often your own front carry. The Duellist kills cleanly as long as nothing has scratched it, so it is strong on a protected board and weak against pings: one point of damage and it trades normally.

Why run it

A Quick carry wins exchanges against anything it can kill in one hit, so it survives much longer. The limits keep it from being free: First Bite works once, and the Duellist loses Quick to the first scratch.

A game where it shines

Turn 9, your opponent's front row is three 6/6 units. Your Ermine Duellist, buffed to 8/6, attacks one and kills it without taking a hit, then kills a second. Their board has a Start of Combat pinger, though, and in the next fight it deals 1 damage to the Duellist first, so it loses Quick and trades normally with the third unit.

Good against

Boards of medium units the Quick unit can kill in one hit.

Weak against

High-Health units, Bristle (it still fires), hit-blocking layers such as Shell or Hunker, and any ping that damages it first (for the undamaged version).

Similar to

First strike in Magic: The Gathering.

Variations
  • First Bite
    This is Quick in its first exchange each combat. (Low-tier limit.)
  • Quick (attacking only)
    When this attacks, it deals its damage first; when it is attacked, damage is simultaneous.
  • Outpace
    This is Quick against units with less Attack than it. (The faster unit strikes first.)
Your direction

“Quick seems incredibly strong. That would be a very, very hard effect to balance. It would be on high-tier units; it absolutely could be, but it would need to go on high-tier units. … we'd need some sort of limits attached to it.”

Designer's response

I followed this: the full keyword sits on a Tier 5 unit and only while it is undamaged, and the low tiers get First Bite, which works once per combat. I'd push back gently on “almost impossible”: Bramble already has natural answers in Bristle, Shell, Hunker, pings and high Health, so Quick has counters without extra rules.

Open questions
  • Which limit reads best: undamaged, first exchange, attacking only, or against lower Attack?
  • Does Peck plus Quick deal all its pecks first? Proposed: yes, as the review said.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a first-strike handler in the exchange (E90), with both-Quick, splash and Bristle cases stated, and the high-tier limits follow the owner's ask. Order against Ambush: Ambush strikes in 9.6.1 step 1, before any exchange, so it still beats Quick. 'Quick while undamaged' reads well; First Bite is clear.

Budget. The owner's limits (first exchange only, or while undamaged) keep Quick near budget; the Tier-5 duellist still needs a smaller body.

  • Stoat War-Dancer: in band. T2 3/2 First Bite: body 5 + first strike in its first exchange: Divine Shield's 2.25 x about 0.5 chance 3 damage kills a Tier-2 unit = 1.1 (E90) [guess] = 6.1 vs V(2) 7: -13% (+-15%).
  • Weasel Second: in band. T3 2/4, back: body 5.5 + First Bite for the unit in front (3.0 x about 0.5 x 0.9 fill) = 1.35 [guess] = 6.85 vs V(3) 8: -14% (+-15%). A back slot has two slots in front; if both get First Bite it is about 8.2 (+3%).
  • Ermine Duellist: flag over. T5 7/5 Quick while undamaged: body 12 + first strike 4.4 x about 0.6 one-shot chance x about 1.4 (it stays undamaged after a clean kill and strikes first again) = 3.7 [guess] = 15.7 vs V(5) 12: +31% (+-15%). Change: 6/4 (10 + 4.4 x 0.5 x 1.3 = 2.9 = 12.9, +7.5%).

Bends. 9.6.1 step 3 and 9.6.2: damage in an exchange is no longer simultaneous.

From combat 16.. Creatures: Stoat. It kills with one fast bite; the Folio's stoat “war-dance troupe”..

No. 25

Swarm

NEW
WIDE

When one Swarm unit attacks, every other Swarm unit in its row joins in on the same target, and only the leader takes the return blow.

Rules textSwarm. When a Swarm unit attacks, every other Swarm unit in its row that can attack and hasn't attacked this cycle joins in, hitting the same target at the same time. The target hits back only the unit that started the attack. Every joiner counts as having attacked this cycle. Units that are stopped (Mired, entranced) don't join. Bristle and Curl prick each joiner one by one.
Worker BeeTier 1
Hive
Swarm.
1 Attack1 Health
Bumblebee NurseTier 2
Hive
Swarm. When a friendly Swarm unit dies, your Swarm units gain +1 Health for this combat.
2 Attack3 Health
Drone CaptainTier 4
Hive
Swarm. Your other Swarm units have +1 Attack while they join this one's attacks.
3 Attack3 Health
How they play together

Fill a row with bees. When the first bee attacks, the whole row piles on the same target, so four 1-Attack bees deal 4 at once and only one bee takes the hit back. The Nurse makes the swarm tougher as bees die, and the Captain adds +1 per joiner when it leads. Because joiners are flagged, the row makes one big attack per cycle instead of many small ones.

Why run it

It lets a wide board of small units kill a big one, which they normally can't. It makes wide against tall a choice of shape, not only of numbers.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, your opponent has built one 4/9 carry in the front row. You have three Worker Bees and a Bumblebee Nurse in F1 to F4. In the fight the first bee attacks the carry and the whole row joins: 5 damage, and only one bee dies. The Nurse makes the others tougher. Next cycle the three survivors hit again for 4 and the carry falls. A few 1/1 bees took down their best unit.

Good against

TALL boards with one or two huge units.

Weak against

Cleave, splash and Curl, and Bristle, which pricks every joiner. Many small enemy units waste the swarm's focus.

Similar to

Like mob tactics in strategy games, or gang-up attacks in some tactics RPGs (Fire Emblem's adjacent support attacks).

Variations
  • Pack (Hunters)
    When this attacks, adjacent units with Pack join in (adjacent only, so gaps break the pack).
  • Sting
    Swarm, and each joiner dies after the attack. Joiners deal double damage.
  • Cloud
    Swarm, and the return blow is split evenly among all the bees that attacked (rounded down, the rest to the leader).
Your direction

“Swarm's really interesting. I like that idea a lot. That's really, really interesting to me.”

Designer's response

Kept as written, with the review's rules made explicit: only bees that haven't attacked join, stopped units don't join, and pricks hit joiners one by one. I added a Pack version for the Hunters' wolves, which uses adjacency so slot placement matters.

Open questions
  • Does a Swarm unit summoned mid-cycle join a later leader? Proposed: only if it isn't flagged (9.4.8).
  • Should the back row's Swarm units join a front-row leader? Proposed: no, row only.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag under

Rules. Works: the leader sets the pointer, joiners are later unflagged units in its row and get flagged (9.4.5), and the return blow hits only the leader. Each joiner's damage is its own hit, so Hunker halves each and Bristle pricks each; the simultaneous-hit question belongs to Shell's ruling. A Thicket is a valid target, as the designer proposes; readable.

Budget. Swarm is in band except the 1/1 bee, which is too small for Tier 1.

  • Worker Bee: flag under. T1 1/1: body 2 + Swarm about 0.6 (joiners skip retaliation; needs a partner in its row; E90) [guess] = 2.6 vs V(1) 5: -48%. Same as the old line. Change: 2/2 (4 + 0.8 = 4.8, -4%).
  • Bumblebee Nurse: in band. T2 2/3: body 5 + Swarm 0.6 + '+1 Health this combat' to about 2 Swarm units on each of about 2 Swarm deaths x 0.9 x 0.7, x 0.6 chance of other bees = 1.5 [guess] = 7.1 vs V(2) 7: +1% (+-15%).
  • Drone Captain: in band. T4 3/3: body 6 + Swarm 0.6 + '+1 Attack' to about 3 joiners x about 1.25 attacks it leads x 0.85 = 3.2 [guess] = 9.8 vs V(4) 10: -2%. Same as the old line.

Bends. 9.4.1 and 9.4.5: one attack per side per turn, from one unit. The pointer is set by the leader and every joiner is flagged. 9.6.1: the exchange is no longer one against one.

From creatures 5.. Creatures: Honeybee and bumblebee. Bees mob an intruder together. Wolves' Pack is the same idea for Hunters..

Group H

Kills, overkill and survival

No. 26

Gorge

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Overkill and Wildfire Elemental
TALL

When this kills a unit with more damage than it needed, it gains the extra as Health.

Rules textGorge. When this kills a unit by attacking it, this gains Health equal to the excess damage for this combat. Excess is the damage that actually landed (after Shell, Hunker and other reductions) minus the target's Health before the hit.
Pine Marten GluttonTier 2
Hunters
Gorge.
4 Attack2 Health
Glutton WolverineTier 4
Hunters
Gorge. After this gorges three times in one combat, it gains +2/+2 permanently.
6 Attack4 Health
Acorn SlingerTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Start of Combat: deal 1 damage to two random enemies.
2 Attack2 Health
How they play together

The gluttons attack small units and grow from the overflow: a 6-Attack Wolverine that kills a 1/1 token gains 5 Health. The Slinger softens targets first, so more of each hit is excess. The Wolverine gets permanent stats from a big feast, so it scales from fighting wide boards.

Why run it

It is a front carry that heals and grows as it fights, so it lasts longer the more it kills.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your opponent goes wide with 1/1 and 2/2 tokens. You put a Glutton Wolverine in F1. In the fight it eats a 1/1 (+5 Health), a 2/2 (+4) and another 1/1 (+5). After the return blows it ends at 6/14, wins the fight and gains +2/+2 for good.

Good against

WIDE boards of small tokens.

Weak against

Sturdy medium units that die to exactly its hit, and hedgehogs that prick it each meal.

Similar to

Lifesteal in many games, but paid only from the overkill.

Variations
  • Gorge (Attack)
    When this kills a unit by attacking it, it gains Attack equal to the excess damage for this combat.
  • Stash
    When this kills a unit by attacking it, gain Acorns equal to the excess damage, at most 2 per combat. (Hedgerow economy tied to fighting.)
  • Trample
    When this kills a unit by attacking it, the excess damage hits the enemy unit behind it. (Great beasts' version.)
Your direction

On Hunters' Devour (02h): “I don't necessarily think you need something that's capped per combat.”

Designer's response

That remark was about Devour, but it fits Gorge too: I put no cap on Gorge. The Wolverine's permanent gain needs three meals in one combat, which is a trigger, not a cap.

Open questions
  • With Peck, only the last peck has excess. Is that the right reading?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: edge

Rules. Works with excess defined as landed damage minus the target's Health. Say that against a Knotted target the landed damage is the part that stayed on the target after the split, and that with Peck only the killing strike has excess. The Wolverine's 'three times' is a counter and stays 3 on a golden (7.3.5); readable.

Budget. Gorge is in band; the pinging helper is a little under.

  • Pine Marten Glutton: in band. T2 4/2: body 6 + about 0.6 kills x about 1.5 excess as Health this combat x 0.7 = 0.6 (E03 on R23) [guess] = 6.6 vs V(2) 7: -6% (+-15%).
  • Glutton Wolverine: in band. T4 6/4: body 10 + Gorge about 1.5 + '+2/+2 permanently after three gorges in one combat' (about 0.1 of fights x 4 SP = 0.4 SP a fight; c_combat(4) 2.1 x 0.4 = 0.8) [guess] = 12.3 vs V(4) 10: +23% (+-25%, permanent growth). Near the line.
  • Acorn Slinger: edge. T2 2/2: body 4 + Start of Combat 1 damage to two random enemies (E72: 2 x 0.85 = 1.7) [guess] = 5.7 vs V(2) 7: -19% (+-15%). Change: If the sim agrees: 2/3 (6.7, -4%).

Bends. 9.6.3: excess damage below 0 is normally lost. Here it is a resource.

No. 28

Play Dead

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Reborn
SCAMSTICKY

The first time this would die, it fakes its death, lies still, then springs up and ambushes the next enemy that attacks next to it.

Rules textPlay Dead. The first time each combat this would die, it lies limp at 1 Health instead. While limp it can't be attacked and doesn't attack. The enemy's “after this kills” effects fire as if it had died; your own death effects and death counters don't (it didn't die). After the next enemy attack on a unit adjacent to it (and that attack's deaths), it springs up and attacks that attacker immediately. If no other friendly unit can attack, it springs up at once. Random and group effects can still hit it while limp, and damage while limp kills it for real.

Hunters
Play Dead.
2 Attack1 Health

Hunters

Hunters

4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Place the foxes beside the units the enemy will attack next. When a fox goes limp, the enemy's on-kill effects fire for nothing, and the fox waits. When the enemy attacks its neighbour, Roadside Reynard springs up and hits that attacker for 7. The Old Fox protects limp foxes from the random pings that would otherwise finish them.

Why run it

It is a second life that ends in an ambush, and it wastes the enemy's kill triggers (Hunt, Gorge).

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your opponent runs a Glutton Wolverine. In the fight it kills your Roadside Reynard, which lies limp; the Wolverine's Gorge fires, but the fox isn't dead. The Wolverine then attacks the unit beside the fox: Reynard springs up and hits it for 7, and it dies.

Good against

On-kill payoffs and big single attackers.

Weak against

Random damage and splash that finish limp foxes, and boards with gaps (no adjacent unit to wait beside).

Similar to

Feign Death in World of Warcraft; Reynard the Fox fables.

Variations
  • Feign
    The first time each combat this would die, it stays at 1 Health and can't be attacked until your next cycle starts.
  • Possum Trap
    Play Dead, but instead of springing up, the next enemy that attacks next to it takes damage equal to this unit's Attack.
  • Regrow (Bog folk newt)

Needs a ruling on back-row protection: a limp front-row unit 'can't be attacked', so either it still counts as a front-row unit and, as the only one, makes your back row unattackable until it springs, or 9.5.3 turns it back into a candidate; proposed: limp and asleep units don't count for 9.5.1 (R7). Rules textLast Word (once per combat). If a death resolution would leave both boards empty and this is among the dying, it survives with 1 Health instead. If combat would end because neither side can attack, and +1 Attack would let this attack, it gains +1 Attack and combat goes on.

How they play together

The wren does nothing until the last moment. If the fight would end with both sides wiped out, it survives at 1 Health and you win. The Crowned Wren wins by more. With the Eagle Elder and Ride, the wren sits on the eagle and saves the fight when the eagle falls.

Why run it

Late fights between sticky boards often end empty on both sides. A win instead of a tie means hero damage for you.

A game where it shines

Turn 11, you and your opponent both have strong sticky boards. In the fight your last unit and theirs trade; your Jenny Wren is among the dying, but Last Word saves it at 1 Health. You win the fight instead of tying it.

Good against

Even fights, Curl or Hunker walls, and Whistle-only boards that stall.

Weak against

Opponents who kill the wren before the last trade, and fights that aren't close.

Variations
  • Tie Breaker
    If combat ends in a tie and this is on your board or died this combat, you deal hero damage as if you had won with this unit alone.
  • Final Say
    While this is the last unit on your side, it has +3/+3.
  • Encore
    If this is among the last units to die in combat, summon a 1/1 Wren in its slot.
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I took the review's fix: once per combat, and the +1 Attack only if it lets a unit attack, so it can't loop.

The second clause (+1 Attack when neither side can attack) works and stops 9.9.1's tie; both sides having Last Word is finite because each is once per combat. Hard to notice when it fires; it needs a clear moment in the fight.

Budget. Last Word almost never fires, so both wrens are far under and need normal bodies.

  • Slime Mould Sage: flag over. Change: 3/5, and only the new copy gains +1/+1 (8 + 3 x 2 x 0.6 = 3.6 = 11.6, -3%).

Bends. 9.6.3: damage normally only lowers Health; here it also makes bodies. 8.4.2: where the bud lands.

From creatures 7.. Creatures: Ooze. Oozes are unstable magic slime; real slime moulds split and rejoin..

Group I

Extra bodies, off-slot zones and switching sides

No. 31

Ride

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Magnetic plus a Deathrattle summon
STICKYWIDE

A small unit rides on a bigger one; when the mount dies, the rider leaps off and attacks.

Rules textRide. Play this onto a friendly unit with more Attack instead of into a slot. It rides that unit: it uses no slot, can't be targeted or hit by any effect, and can be played onto a full board. One rider per mount. When the mount dies, the rider leaps into the mount's slot before the mount's own death effects summon (those go to the nearest empty slot), then attacks immediately. Stowaway is the same with no Attack limit and no attack on leaping.
Field Mouse SquireTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Stowaway. While this rides a unit, that unit has +1 Health.
1 Attack2 Health
Wren KingTier 2
Birds
Ride. When this leaps, it has +2 Attack for that attack.
2 Attack1 Health
Brown Bear PorterTier 4
Great beasts
This can carry two riders.
5 Attack7 Health
How they play together

Riders go on your big units: the mouse adds Health while it rides, and the Wren King bursts out when the mount dies and hits for 4. The Bear Porter carries two, so each of its deaths gives you two more bodies. Every mount takes two kills.

Why run it

It gets more bodies than slots onto the board, and each mount becomes a two-part threat.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, your board is full. You buy a Wren King and play it onto your 6/6 bear. In the fight the enemy kills the bear; the wren leaps into its slot and pecks a damaged enemy to death.

Good against

Boards that win by trading one for one.

Weak against

Cleave and splash, if they hit riders too; mounts that never die.

Similar to

Magnetic in Battlegrounds, but the rider stays itself and separates on death.

Variations
  • Stowaway
    Play this onto any friendly unit. When it dies, this takes its slot (no attack on leaping).
  • Pouch
    This carries one friendly unit with less Attack, which leaps out when this dies.
  • Swap Mount
    Ride. In the Recruit Phase you may move a rider to another mount for free.
Designer's response

No owner reaction. I kept both shapes (Ride and Stowaway) and settled the leaping order from the review.

Open questions
  • Does a rider count for who attacks first, hero damage and triples? Proposed: not for who attacks first; yes for hero damage and triples.
  • What happens when the mount is sold? Proposed: the rider goes to hand if there is room; otherwise it is sold too.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as an off-slot zone (R7) with its leap timing stated, but several of R7's questions are open: whether riders count for who attacks first (proposed no), hero damage and triples (proposed yes), and whether 'can't be targeted or hit by any effect' blocks your own buffs too. Also rule what happens to the rider when the mount is sold, tripled or transformed (proposed: to hand, else sold). The leap's 'attacks immediately' fires inside death resolution and should queue after it (R5).

Budget. Riders fit at their tiers; the bear mount is over on body size, and the saved-slot price needs a sim.

  • Field Mouse Squire: in band. T1 1/2 Stowaway: body 3 x 0.9 (it enters when the mount dies) = 2.7 + no slot used about 1 (E23) + the mount's +1 Health about 0.9 [guess] = 4.6 vs V(1) 5: -8%.
  • Wren King: in band. T2 2/1: body 2.7 + attacks at once with +2 Attack about 2.4 (E83) + no slot about 1 [guess] = 6.1 vs V(2) 7: -13% (+-15%). Same as the old creatures version.
  • Brown Bear Porter: flag over. T4 5/7: body 12 (+20% before text) + a second rider seat (another saved slot about 1 x about 0.4 chance of two riders) = 0.4 [guess] = 12.4 vs V(4) 10: +24% (+-15%). Change: 5/5 (10 + 0.4 = 10.4, +4%). For information: E23's +1 for a saved slot likely under-prices an eighth or ninth body on a full late board; sim riders before printing them above Tier 2.

Bends. 8.1.1 and 7.2.2: the board can hold more than 7 bodies. 8.4.1: the rider jumps ahead of the mount's own summons. 9.4.12: it attacks out of turn.

From creatures 10, rows 6 (both marked variant by their designers).. Creatures: Wren, from the fable of the wren on the eagle's back (Folio hook “Rider”). The Folio's mice “ride larger allies into battle”..

No. 32

Burrow

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Deity
WIDESTICKY

This digs underground at the start of combat, safe from everything, and pops up to attack when a friendly unit dies.

Rules textBurrow. Start of Combat: this goes underground. It leaves its slot and can't attack, be attacked or be hit by any effect. A molehill stays in its slot and blocks summons there. After the first friendly death, it surfaces in that unit's slot (or the nearest empty one) and attacks immediately. If your side would be left with no units, it surfaces first.
Mole DiggerTier 1
Hedgerow folk

Hedgerow folk

4 Attack3 Health

5 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The moles are safe from Start of Combat damage and pop up as the first friendly unit falls. The Sapper hits for 7 on surfacing. The Conspirator brings every mole up at once, so a board with three moles ambushes with all of them after the first death.

Why run it

It refills your board safely and hits back the moment you lose a unit.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your opponent's board opens with Start of Combat pings. Your Velvet Sapper digs in and takes none. When their first attack kills your front unit, the Sapper surfaces in that slot and hits the attacker for 7.

Start of Combat damage and boards that kill one unit quickly.

Boards that kill nothing for a long time, and opponents who see the molehill and plan the first kill.

Variations
  • Tunnel
    Once per combat, after a friendly unit dies, this moves into its slot.
  • Go to Ground
    Start of Combat: this goes underground. At the start of your second cycle it surfaces with +3/+3.
  • Molehill
    When this surfaces, the enemy unit across from it takes 2 damage.

  • If no friendly unit dies and you win, does the mole count for hero damage? Proposed: yes, it survived.

No. 33

Changeling

NEW
SCAM

At the start of the fight, this trades places with the enemy unit facing it, and each one fights for its new side.

Rules textStart of Combat: this swaps places with the enemy unit across from it. For this combat, each of the two fights for the side it now stands on: it attacks for that side, counts as that side's unit for every effect, and its death counts for that side. Its owner keeps the card, so a 'permanently' effect on it still goes to its owner's recruit board. A unit can be swapped at most once per combat. If the slot across is empty, nothing happens.
Cradle SwapperTier 2
Fairies
Changeling.
1 Attack3 Health
Changeling BabeTier 4
Fairies
Changeling. While this fights for the enemy, enemy units adjacent to it have -2 Attack.
1 Attack1 Health
Hob of the Hollow HillTier 5
Fairies
After a friendly Changeling swaps, give the unit it brought over +3/+3 this combat.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Cradle Swapper is the cheap version: it trades a 1/3 for whatever the enemy put across from it. Changeling Babe goes over as a saboteur, weakening the two enemies next to it in their line. Hob sits in your back row and turns each stolen unit into a bigger threat against its own board. Stolen units still use their own text for you, so a stolen death-summoner summons on your side.

Why run it

It takes the enemy's front unit for one fight and hands them a weak body in exchange. Inside any comp it is a SCAM piece that answers a big front carry without needing to kill it.

A game where it shines

Turn 8. You are on Tier 4 and the last fight showed that most boards in the lobby put their carry in the middle of the front row. You buy Changeling Babe and play it in F2. In the fight, the enemy's 14/14 boar stands in their F2. Start of Combat: the Babe and the boar swap. The boar now fights for you from your F2, and your Babe sits in their F2, giving their F1 and F3 units -2 Attack. Their board has to kill its own carry to win.

Good against

TALL boards that keep one big carry in a predictable front slot.

Weak against

WIDE boards of tokens (you steal a 1/1), boards with a gap across from it, and a cheap decoy placed in the mirrored slot.

Similar to

Like Hearthstone's Mind Control or Sylvanas Windrunner, but only for one fight, and paid for by sending your own unit over.

Variations
  • Glamour
    Start of Combat: this and the enemy unit across from it swap Attack and Health for this combat. Nobody changes sides. A simpler SCAM with no ownership rules.
  • Borrowed Child
    Start of Combat: swap places with the enemy across from this, as Changeling. After the stolen unit attacks once, the two swap back to their own sides and slots (or the nearest empty ones). A time-bound theft.
  • Front-row Changeling
    As Changeling, but only from the front row: in the back row it does nothing. Keeps back-row carries safe and the rule easier to read.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • Name clash: archetypes.md uses 'Changeling' for a Fairy that copies the unit behind it. One of the two needs another name.
  • Should a back-row Changeling be able to swap with a back-row carry, or only the front row?
  • Hero damage: does a stolen unit that survives count its tier for the side it ended on (proposed in 10.1.1)?
  • Against Rival or Flush, the result depends on the random Start of Combat order (9.3.2). Is that acceptable?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Needs R10's combat-side attribute for every 'friendly' and 'enemy' selector, which the text implies. Needs a ruling on 'a permanently effect on it still goes to its owner's recruit board': when the stolen unit belongs to the recorded opponent, that write is impossible (2.1.4, R9), so say permanent effects on a unit fighting for the other side are lost. Hero damage for a stolen survivor (10.1.1) and the clash with archetypes.md's Changeling name are open.

Budget. Stealing a unit is worth about twice that unit, so cheap Changelings get stronger as enemy boards grow; a limit on what they can steal keeps them in band all game.

  • Cradle Swapper: flag over. body 4 + swap (you gain the unit across and they lose it, less both bodies: 2 × X − 8, × 0.6 the slot is filled) ≈ 1.2–2.4 at turns 2–4 (X ≈ 5–6), ≈ 20 late (X ≈ 15) (proposed E94) [guess] ≈ 5.2–6.4 vs V(2) 7 early (−9% to −26%), ≈ 24 late (+240%), ±25% Change: 2/3, and swap only with an enemy that has 4 Attack or less: ≈ 5.5 at turns 2–4 (−21%), ≈ 7.5 from turn 6 (+7%). It still steals mid-size units; big carries are safe.
  • Changeling Babe: flag over. body 2 + swap (2 × X − 4) × 0.9 ≈ 9.4 at turn 6 (X ≈ 7.2), ≈ 23 late + −2 Attack to 2 enemy neighbours while it lives (E15 4.4 × 0.4 survival ≈ 1.8) [guess] ≈ 13.2 vs V(4) 10 at turn 6 (+32%), ≈ 27 late (+170%), ±25% Change: The old review's change still fits: 2/2, and only if the enemy across has 6 Attack or less: ≈ 10.2 at turn 6 (+2%), ≈ 10.5 late (+5%).
  • Hob of the Hollow Hill: in band. body 10 + +3/+3 this combat on the stolen unit (6 × 0.9 swaps × 0.8 realised × 0.6 a Changeling is on board ≈ 2.6, E03) [guess] ≈ 12.6 vs V(5) 12: +5%, ±15%. With two Changelings on board ≈ 18.6 (+55%)

Bends. 9.1.1: each side fights with its own copied board; here a unit fights for the other side. 8.2.6: across from. 10.1.1 (proposal): a swapped unit counts for the side it ends the fight on.

From creatures 8.. Creatures: The folklore changeling: fairies take a child and leave one of their own in its place..

No. 34

Brood

NEW
SCAMWIDE

At the start of the fight, this lays an egg in an empty slot on the enemy board, blocking it, and the egg later hatches into a chick for you.

Rules textStart of Combat: lay an Egg in the enemy slot across from this if it is empty; otherwise in the empty enemy slot nearest it (8.4.2 order). If the enemy board has no empty slot, nothing happens. The Egg is not a unit: it can't attack, be attacked or be hit by effects, and it doesn't count for protecting the back row, attack order, winning or hero damage. It only fills the slot: no summon, move or return can enter it. After the enemy side has made 3 attacks (extra attacks count), the Egg hatches: remove it and summon a 3/3 Cuckoo Chick for you into your empty slot nearest this unit's slot (its last slot if it has died). If you have no empty slot, the hatch fails.
CuckooTier 2
Birds
Brood.
2 Attack2 Health
Cuckoo MotherTier 4
Birds
Brood. Your Cuckoo Chicks hatch after 2 enemy attacks instead of 3 and have +2/+2.
3 Attack4 Health
Reed Warbler DupeTier 3
Birds
Whenever one of your Cuckoo Chicks is summoned, give it +1/+1 for each enemy unit that has died this combat.
2 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Cuckoo and Cuckoo Mother each lay an Egg, so two Eggs can take two of the enemy's gaps. The Mother makes the chicks hatch faster and bigger, so they arrive while the fight is still open. The Warbler, fooled into feeding the chick, makes late hatchlings huge. A Delivery stork (44) steers the chicks into your safest slot.

Why run it

It answers WIDE boards that leave gaps for their summons: a summon with nowhere to land is lost (8.4.5). And after a few enemy attacks you get a free 3/3.

A game where it shines

Turn 5. Your opponent runs rabbits that bolt and leave Kits behind, and they keep one front slot open for them. You have a Cuckoo in F3. Start of Combat: their F3 is the gap, so the Egg goes there. Later their death-summoner in F1 dies: its first token takes F1, and its second token has nowhere to go, because the Egg holds their only gap, so it is lost. After their third attack, the Egg hatches and a 3/3 Chick joins your side.

Good against

WIDE boards with one or two gaps kept for summons.

Weak against

Full 7-unit boards (no Egg at all) and short fights that end before the enemy has made 3 attacks.

Variations
  • Laid in Their Nest
    Start of Combat: lay an Egg under the enemy across from this. When that unit dies, the Egg takes its slot before that unit's own summons can, and you summon a 2/2 Chick. Works against full boards too.
  • Clutch
    Start of Combat: lay an Egg in every empty enemy front slot. These Eggs never hatch. Pure summon denial, readable at a glance.
  • Early Hatch
    As Brood, but the Egg hatches after the first enemy death in its row, not after 3 attacks.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • Can an Egg be the target of an effect that 'destroys' or 'clears' things on a slot, such as a Thicket clearer?
  • Should the Egg count as an empty slot for Solitary (41) and Caravan (35)? Proposed: Caravan can't step into it; Solitary ignores it (only units are neighbours).
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: edge

Rules. The Egg's occupant rules are stated in full, so it fits R7's table. Needs a ruling on what counts as one of the 'enemy attacks' that hatch it: whistles, dances, Swarm joiners (one attack or several) and Peck strikes; proposed only attack declarations count, one per Swarm. A side that never attacks never hatches it, which is fine; readable.

Budget. Brood is in band on the turns when enemy boards have gaps and fades on full boards, as the old review found.

  • Cuckoo: in band. body 4 + 3/3 chick (E30 6 × 0.8) × your empty slot by the hatch 0.85 × an empty enemy slot at Start of Combat 0.9 × it hatches 0.9 ≈ 3.3 + blocked enemy summon space ≈ 0.3 (proposed E97) [guess] ≈ 7.6 vs V(2) 7: +9% at turns 2–5; ≈ 5 late (−28%), weak late by design, ±15%

Bends. 8.4: summons land only on their own side; Brood puts an object on the enemy board and takes their summon space. 9.9.1: the Egg is not a unit, so it never keeps a board alive.

From rows 7.. Creatures: Cuckoo, the brood parasite (Folio hook 'Sabotage')..

Group J

Moving your own units in combat

No. 35

Caravan

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' board packing
WIDEMEDIUM

When a gap opens to its left in combat, this steps into it, so a line of Caravan units closes up after deaths.

Rules textIn combat, after each death resolution ends, every Caravan unit whose left-hand slot in its row is empty steps into that slot, going left to right; repeat until no Caravan unit can step. Summons made during that death resolution land first, so a dying unit's own summon still takes its slot (8.4.1). A unit that steps keeps its attack flag; stepping into a slot below the attack pointer flags it (9.4.9).
Shrewlet TrainTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Caravan. After this steps, gain +1 Attack this combat.
2 Attack1 Health
Tail-holder ShrewTier 3
Hedgerow folk
Caravan. Adjacent Caravan units have +2 Attack.
3 Attack4 Health
Shrew MatriarchTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Caravan. Whenever a friendly Caravan unit steps, give your Caravan units +1/+1 this combat.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Shrewlet Trains grow every time they step, so the line gets meaner as it loses members. The Tail-holder's aura only works on neighbours, and Caravan keeps them neighbours after deaths. The Matriarch turns every step into a buff for the whole line, so each death powers the survivors.

Why run it

Adjacency boards normally fall apart when a unit in the middle dies. Caravan keeps the chain whole, so adjacency auras and 'units next to this' text keep working all fight.

A game where it shines

Turn 6. Your front row is Shrewlet Train in F1, Tail-holder in F2, Shrewlet Train in F3 and the Matriarch in F4. The enemy kills your F1 shrew first. The Tail-holder steps into F1, the F3 shrew steps into F2, and the Matriarch steps into F3: three steps, so every Caravan unit gets +3/+3, and the shrew that stepped gets +1 Attack. The line is still touching, so the Tail-holder's +2 Attack aura still reaches both neighbours.

Good against

SCAM and kill-order boards that snipe one middle unit to break up an adjacency chain.

Weak against

Cleave and splash, which love a packed line, and Mark (40), which punishes units that step into its slot.

Similar to

Battlegrounds packs every board after a death; Caravan is that rule as a keyword, leftward within a row only.

Variations
  • Step Up
    When the front slot in front of this empties in combat, this steps forward into it. A back-row reserve that refills the front.
  • Follow the Leader
    When the unit to the left of this dies, this steps into its slot and gains that unit's Attack this combat.
  • Shuffle Right
    As Caravan, but steps right. Keeps the F4 flank filled, for boards that build on the right.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • Should Caravan units also close gaps at Start of Combat, or only after deaths? Proposed: only after deaths, so recruit placement stays the player's choice.
  • Rows compete for 'units that move in combat': Flush (19), Jink (22), Tunnel for a Digger calling. Should one group own movement?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag under

Rules. Works: it runs after each death resolution, after that pass's summons, and its flag handling follows 9.4.9, so a step into the slot that just attacked lets it attack next. Say whether a Thicket's slot counts as empty for a step (Thickets count as empty only for summons); Eggs block steps as their own text says. Finite and readable.

Budget. The keyword is cheap; the Matriarch's team buff on every step snowballs inside one fight, so aim it at the unit that moves.

  • Shrewlet Train: flag under. Same text as the old review: body 3 + stepping ≈ 0.3 (E32) + +1 Attack after a step ≈ 0.5 [guess] ≈ 3.8 vs V(1) 5: −24%, outside ±15% and the ±1 SP floor Change: 2/2 (≈ 4.8, −4%).
  • Tail-holder Shrew: in band. body 7 + stepping 0.3 + +2 Attack to adjacent Caravan units (≈ 0.8 Caravan neighbours in a Caravan row × 2 × 0.6 realised, a little more as the line closes up ≈ 1.3; E13 adjacent) [guess] ≈ 8.6 vs V(3) 8: +8%, ±15%
  • Shrew Matriarch: flag over. body 10 + stepping 0.3 + every step gives each Caravan unit +1/+1 this combat; one front death can cause 3 steps: ≈ 3 steps a fight × 2.5 Caravan units × 2 SP × 0.6 realised × 0.6 Caravan build ≈ 5.4 (E03) [guess] ≈ 15.7 vs V(5) 12: +31%, ±15% Change: Give the +1/+1 only to the unit that stepped (≈ 3 × 2 × 0.6 × 0.6 ≈ 2.2): ≈ 12.5 (+4%) on the same 4/6.

Bends. 8.3.4 (no packing): packing comes back as a keyword, for one tribe. A moved unit keeps its attack flag, and stepping into a slot below the pointer flags it (9.4.9).

From rows 3.. Creatures: Shrews. Young shrews travel in a line, each holding the next one's tail (Folio hook 'Formation')..

No. 36

Rotate

NEW
STICKYMEDIUM

After this attacks, it swaps with the healthiest unit behind it, so a fresh unit comes forward and the tired one rests in the back.

Rules textAfter this attacks, if it is alive and in the front row, it swaps places with the friendly unit behind it that has the most Health (a tie goes to the lower reading order). If no unit is behind it, nothing happens. Each keeps its own attack flag; a unit brought forward into the slot that just attacked is unflagged, so it attacks next (9.4.8).
Skein GoslingTier 1
Birds
Rotate. When this swaps into the back row, it heals to full Health.
2 Attack2 Health
Lead GooseTier 3
Birds
Rotate. A unit that swaps forward with this gains +2 Attack this combat.
4 Attack4 Health
Barnacle Goose MatriarchTier 5
Birds
Rotate. Whenever two friendly units swap places, both gain +1/+1 this combat.
5 Attack7 Health
How they play together

Two geese, one in F1 and one in B1, take turns at the point: each attacks, swaps back, and the Gosling heals while it rests. The Lead Goose brings a back-row unit forward with +2 Attack, so your back carry gets an early swing. The Matriarch makes every swap a buff. Stance units (43) flip mode as they rotate.

Why run it

Your front unit gets to hit and then hide before the enemy's reply kills it. The back row stops being a waiting room: its best unit joins the fight early.

A game where it shines

Turn 5. You have a Lead Goose in F2 and a 3/9 Hedgehog in B1. The Lead Goose attacks an enemy 3/3, kills it and drops to 4/1. It swaps with the Hedgehog: the goose rests safe in B1, and the Hedgehog stands in F2 as a 5/9 with no flag, so it attacks next. The enemy now faces a fresh wall, and the wounded goose can still fight later if the front falls.

Good against

Boards with one big front hitter that would kill your front units one at a time.

Weak against

Back-row reach and random damage (9.5.6), which ignore rows and find the resting goose.

Similar to

Like switching out a hurt fighter in Pokemon or a tag-team fighting game.

Variations
  • Relieve
    When the unit in front of this survives damage, this swaps with it. A back-row unit that steps in to take over.
  • V Formation
    After this attacks, swap with the unit behind it that has the most Attack, not Health. An attacking version.
  • Wheel
    Once per combat: after this attacks, rotate instead with the friendly unit across the whole board with the most Health, in either row.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • Is healing on retreat (Skein Gosling) too sticky? A Gosling pair can cycle all fight.
  • Should a back-row Rotate unit be able to swap forward with a wounded front unit?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works with 9.4.9: both units keep their flags. Drop 'a unit brought forward into the slot that just attacked is unflagged', which can contradict 'each keeps its own attack flag' when the back unit has already attacked this cycle. Two Skein Goslings healing on retreat can survive weak attackers until the 200-attack tie (9.10.1); R3's no-progress rule would end it sooner.

Budget. Rotation itself is cheap, but both bigger geese print a full body before their text.

  • Skein Gosling: in band. body 4 + rotation ≈ 1 (E32) × a unit behind it on turns 1–3 ≈ 0.4 + heals to full when it rests (≈ 2 Health × 0.4 × 0.5 used ≈ 0.4) [guess] ≈ 4.8 vs V(1) 5: −4%, ±15%
  • Lead Goose: flag over. Same text as the old review: body 8 + rotation ≈ 1 + +2 Attack to the unit that comes forward ≈ 0.9 [guess] ≈ 10 vs V(3) 8: +25%, ±15% Change: 3/4 (≈ 9, +12%).

Warren LookoutTier 1
Hedgerow folk

1 Attack3 Health
ThumperTier 3
Hedgerow folk

Old Buck of the DownTier 5
Hedgerow folk

How they play together

The Lookout runs and leaves a Kit in its hole, so the front stays full. Thumper's warning thump toughens everyone left in front. With the Old Buck, every Kit is a 3/3 that can bolt too, as long as you left back slots open as bolt holes. A rabbit board plans its gaps: every bolter needs an empty slot behind it.

Why run it

Each bolter takes a hit, then gets out of the way and leaves something new in front. The enemy spends attacks on bodies that don't die.

A game where it shines

Turn 2. You play Warren Lookout in F2 and leave B1 empty. In the fight an enemy 2/2 hits it: the Lookout is 1/1 and kills nothing, but it survives, so it bolts to B1 and a 1/1 Kit pops into F2. The enemy now has to chew through the Kit, and the Lookout still waits in the back to fight last.

Good against

Boards that win with many medium hits, and Peck (5).

Weak against

One big hit that kills outright (it never survives to bolt), random damage, and back-row reach.

Variations
  • Go to Ground
    The first time this survives damage, it can't be attacked until your front row has no other units. It doesn't move.
  • Spring
    When the front slot in front of this empties, this springs forward into it and attacks immediately. The bolt in reverse.
  • Bolt Hole
    After this bolts, the slot it left gets +2/+2 for this combat (a slot effect), so whatever fills it next is bigger.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • If both back slots behind F2 or F3 are empty, should the player choose, or is 'left one' fine?
  • Does a unit that bolts into a Bury or Fallow slot (39) take the pickup?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: it checks after the death check, picks the left slot on a tie, and keeps its flag (9.4.9). The Lookout's Kit goes into a named slot (8.4.6). Bolting onto a Nut needs Bury's 'enters a slot' event; readable.

Budget. Bolt prices fine on small boards; Thumper's team buff is the one over.

  • Warren Lookout: in band. Same as the old review: body 4 + (bolt ≈ 0.5 + a 1/1 Kit ≈ 1.6) × survives a hit 0.7 × an empty back slot 0.8 ≈ 1.2 [guess] ≈ 5.2 vs V(1) 5: +4%, ±15%. Blank on a full board
  • Thumper: flag over. body 7 + bolt ≈ 0.2 + +1/+2 this combat to the front row after it bolts (≈ 3 front units × 3 SP × 0.7 realised × it bolts in ≈ 0.45 of fights at turns 4–6 ≈ 2.8; E03) [guess] ≈ 10 vs V(3) 8: +25%, ±15%. Blank on a full board Change: 3/3 (≈ 9, +12%).
  • Old Buck of the Down: in band. body 11 + Kits get +2/+2 and Bolt (≈ 0.2 Kits a fight late, from Warren Lookouts that find an empty back slot, × 3.2 ≈ 0.7) [guess] ≈ 11.7 vs V(5) 12: −2%, ±15%. Nearly a plain unit while only one card makes Kits

No. 38

Sett

RESKIN of Galaxy's slot buffs
MEDIUMTALLWIDE

At the end of each turn, this badger's slot gets +1/+1, and the slot keeps it for whoever stands there later.

Rules textEnd of Turn: this unit's slot gains +1/+1 Sett. A Sett is a slot effect (8.5): whatever unit stands in that slot has its stats, now or later, including units summoned into it in combat. The Sett stays when this unit moves, is sold or dies. Golden: +2/+2.
Badger CubTier 1
Hunters
Sett.
1 Attack2 Health
Sett GrandsireTier 2
Hunters
Sett. When you sell this, its slot's Sett gains +2/+2.
2 Attack4 Health
Sett MatriarchTier 4
Hunters
End of Turn: each of your slots that has a Sett gains +1/+1 more Sett.
3 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The Cub and Brock build slots, not units. You can keep a badger in one slot to dig a mansion, or move it each turn to spread Setts across the board, since free moves let you choose. Sell Sett Grandsire to cash out into its slot, then play your carry there. The Matriarch grows every Sett you have, wherever the badgers went.

Why run it

Stats on a slot survive the unit dying. A death summon that takes a dead unit's slot (8.4.1) moves into the mansion and comes out big.

A game where it shines

Turns 1 to 4: your Badger Cub sits in F2, and the F2 Sett reaches +4/+4. Turn 5: you sell the Cub and play your best unit in F2; it fights with +4/+4. In combat it dies, and its own death summon lands in F2, so that token has +4/+4 too.

Good against

SCAM boards that kill one big unit: the next unit in the slot is just as big.

Weak against

Early tempo, because it gives nothing the turn you buy it, and hostile slot effects like Mark (40).

Similar to

Galaxy's slot buffs, such as Candy Claws: 'Hunt - Give my slot +1/+1'.

Variations
  • Ancestral Sett
    When this dies in combat, its slot gains +1/+1 permanently. Grows from dying, for STICKY and WIDE boards.
  • Warren
    End of Turn: this unit's slot gains 'When the unit here dies, summon a 1/1 Kit into this slot.' (Stacks.) A slot that carries a death effect, not stats.
  • Tenancy
    If this has stood in the same slot at the end of 3 of your turns in a row, its slot gains +3/+3 and this loses Tenancy. A time-bound version, as pillar 13 asks for.
Your direction

02e: "We have set slots, so we can experiment with slot buff mechanics, and we should have the ability to do that, I think."

Designer's response

Sett is the plainest slot buff: one per turn, on the badger's slot. The variations try a slot that carries a death effect and a time-bound version.

Open questions
  • Slots need to show their buffs on screen; every slot mechanic needs that UI.

Name clash: this Sett Grandsire (T2) and Hunker's Sett Grandsire (T5) are different cards.

Budget. Unconditional +1/+1 a turn puts the Setts +72% to +164% over their tiers (band ±25%); Health-only Setts or a higher tier keep the slot idea.

  • Badger Cub: flag over. body 3 + slot +1/+1 every turn: s = 2, 2.5× the Tier-1 growth cap of 0.8; c(1) 3.9 × 2 × 1.1 (E16, the slot keeps it) ≈ 8.6 [guess] ≈ 11.6 vs V(1) 5: +132%, ±25% Change: 1/1, and its Sett gives +1 Health a turn (golden +2): 2 + 3.9 × 0.9 × 1.1 ≈ 5.9 (+18%).
