No. 39

Bury

VARIANT of Galaxy's slot buffs (merged with Fallow)
WIDEECO

This hides a Nut in an empty slot; the first unit you put there eats it for +1/+1, and a Nut left alone for 3 turns grows into a sapling.

Rules textEnd of Turn: bury a Nut in your empty slot nearest this (8.4.2 order). You can have at most 3 Nuts on your board. In the Recruit Phase, the first unit that enters a Nut's slot (played, moved or summoned) eats it: +1/+1 permanently, and the Nut is gone. A unit eats at most one Nut per turn. In combat, a unit summoned into a Nut's slot eats it for that combat only, and the Nut is still there after combat. A Nut no unit has eaten by the end of 3 of your turns sprouts: summon a 1/2 Oakling into its slot (Created).
Hoarder SquirrelTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Bury.
1 Attack2 Health
Jay ForesterTier 3
Birds
Start of Combat: summon a 1/1 Oakling into each of your empty slots that holds a Nut.
3 Attack3 Health
Cache KeeperTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Bury. Your Nuts give +2/+2 instead, and your Oaklings sprout with +2/+2.
2 Attack5 Health
How they play together

The Squirrel plants Nuts in your gaps, and you choose which gaps by where you put it. You can spend a Nut by playing a new unit there, or leave it for three turns for a free Oakling. The Jay turns every Nut into a combat Oakling that eats it, every fight, without using it up. The Cache Keeper doubles both payoffs.

Why run it

It turns the turns when your board isn't full into value: a stat buff for your next unit, or a free body later. The owner's 'take a little bit of stuff up front now, or get more later' choice, slot by slot.

A game where it shines

Turn 2. You have four units, with the Hoarder Squirrel in F1 and F2 empty. End of Turn: a Nut goes into F2. Turn 3: you can play your new buy into F2 for +1/+1, or play it elsewhere and wait. You wait. On Turn 4 the Jay Forester arrives: in each fight it summons a 1/1 Oakling into F2, which eats the Nut and fights as a 2/2. At the end of Turn 4 the Nut sprouts, and a real 1/2 Oakling joins your board.

Good against

Slow lobbies where a few gaps early don't cost you fights.

Weak against

Early tempo boards, since every Nut is a slot you didn't fill.

Similar to

A Galaxy slot buff on an empty slot that is used up by the first unit to enter.

Variations
  • Fallow
    While this is on your board, at End of Turn each empty slot gains +1/+1 Fallow (up to +3/+3). The first unit to enter a fallow slot harvests it: in the Recruit Phase it keeps the Fallow permanently and the slot resets; in combat, a summon takes it for that combat only. A unit harvests at most once per turn.
  • Scatter
    When this dies in combat, bury a Nut in its slot on your recruit board.
  • Acorn Stash
    If you have 3 Nuts on your board, they combine into one Acorn Stash in the slot of the oldest: the first unit to enter it gains +5/+5 permanently. Hoard, then cash in big.
Your direction

02h on the Hedgerow Acorns: "Maybe you get large acorns and bigger acorns ... to encourage you to hoard for a bit ... it's always nice to have an idea of 'take a little bit of stuff up front now, or get more later'."

Designer's response

Bury is that choice on the board: eat the Nut now, or let it sprout. The Acorn Stash variation is the owner's 'three small make a big one' idea as a slot pickup. Nuts land in the empty slot nearest the squirrel, not a random one, so the player picks the spot.

Open questions
  • Overlaps Hedgerow Acorns (cards in hand) and the Fairy ring (a slot enchantment). Should Nuts and Acorns be one system?
  • Needs a 'unit enters slot' event that covers playing, moving, summoning and returning.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: Nuts are pickups (R7) that don't block summons or play, with a cap of 3 and a once-per-turn eating rule that stops move-in-move-out farming. It needs one 'unit enters a slot' event covering play, move, summon and return, as the designer says. Readable with a slot badge.

Budget. Free permanent stats from gaps put two units +56% and +94% over their tiers (band ±25%); one tier up and smaller bodies fix it without touching the Nut.

  • Hoarder Squirrel: flag over. As the old review: a Nut a turn, eaten by the next unit to enter (played or moved): s ≈ 1.6, twice the Tier-1 cap; 3.9 × 1.6 ≈ 6.2 + Oakling ≈ 0.5 [guess] ≈ 9.7 vs V(1) 5: +94%, ±25% Change: Tier 2 at 1/1, same text (s ≈ 1.4, near the Tier-2 cap: ≈ 7.7, +10%).
  • Jay Forester: in band. body 6 + Start of Combat Oaklings in Nut slots (≈ 1.2 Nut gaps in a Bury build × a 2/2 after eating, 3.2 × 0.6 a Bury unit on board ≈ 2.3; E30) [guess] ≈ 8.3 vs V(3) 8: +4%, ±15%. It fires every fight, since a Nut eaten in combat stays
  • Cache Keeper: flag over. body 7 + own Nuts at +2/+2 (s ≈ 4 × 0.5 a Nut eaten at turn 6 ≈ 2.0: 3.1 × 2 ≈ 6.2) + other squirrels' Nuts doubled (s ≈ 0.6: ≈ 1.9) + bigger Oaklings ≈ 0.5 [guess] ≈ 15.6 vs V(4) 10: +56%, ±25%; total s ≈ 2.6, at the Tier-4 cap Change: 1/2, same text (≈ 11.6, +16%).

Bends. 8.1 and 8.3.1: a slot holds something that isn't a unit, so a gap becomes a resource. 9.1.1: because combat runs on a copy, a Nut eaten in combat is still there next fight.

No. 40

Mark

NEW
SCAMWIDESTICKY

At the start of the fight, this marks the enemy slot facing it, and any enemy unit that steps or is summoned into that slot later takes damage.

Rules textMark (X). Start of Combat: scent-mark the enemy slot across from this. For the rest of this combat, each enemy unit that enters a marked slot (summoned there, returned there after dying, or moved there) takes X damage. The unit already standing there when the mark is made is not hurt. The mark is yours, lasts the combat, and stays after this unit dies.
Fox CubTier 1
Hunters
Mark (2).
2 Attack1 Health
Gubbio WolfTier 2
Hunters
Mark (3). After an enemy dies in a marked slot, this gains +1/+1 this combat.
3 Attack2 Health
Pack AlphaTier 4
Hunters
Mark (3). Your units attack enemies standing in marked slots first, if they can be attacked.
4 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Mark does nothing to the unit facing it at first. It waits for what comes next: the token the dead unit leaves, the rabbit that comes back, the shrew that steps in. Because refills go into the dead unit's own slot, the slot across from your wolf is exactly where the enemy's next body lands, with no aiming needed. The Gubbio Wolf grows from each kill there, and the Pack Alpha sends your attacks at the marked slot, so you kill the first unit, then the refill steps onto the scent.

Why run it

WIDE and STICKY boards get their value from bodies that come back into the same slot. Mark taxes every one of those arrivals, and kills tokens before they can attack.

A game where it shines

Turn 6. Your opponent's last board had rabbits that leave Kits and a unit that comes back after dying. You play the Fox Cub in F1, the Gubbio Wolf in F2 and the Pack Alpha in F3. Start of Combat: they mark the enemy's F1, F2 and F3. Your Fox Cub attacks first and hits their Warren Lookout (1/3) in F2: it survives at 1 Health and bolts to the back row, and the Kit it leaves pops into F2, steps on the scent, takes 3 and dies at once. The Wolf is now 4/3. Later you kill their F3 unit; it comes back into F3 with 1 Health, takes 3 and dies again. Two of their 'free' bodies never got to attack.

Good against

Boards that refill the same slot: death summons, returns, Bolt Kits, Caravan lines stepping left.

Weak against

Boards with no summons or returns, where Mark is just a body, and Delivery (44), which sends summons elsewhere.

Similar to

Like a trap square in a tactics game: you place it where you know the enemy's next unit will stand.

Variations
  • Mark, hits now
    Start of Combat: deal X damage to the enemy across from this and mark its slot; every enemy that later enters it takes X too. Always does something, so it is easier to read.
  • Scent
    Start of Combat: the enemy slot across from this is Scented for this combat. Whatever unit stands in it has -2 Attack, now and after it is replaced. A hostile slot debuff, like a curse on the ground.
  • Territory
    Mark your own ground instead. See the new mechanic Territory: your slot punishes enemies that attack the unit standing in it.
Your direction

"Mark doesn't make sense to me at all. I don't really see how that could be a good mechanic. Maybe there's iteration to it, but I don't see it right now." (02h). Direction: example cards; look for the version that works.

Designer's response

Push back with the case where it works: Bramble's summon rule sends refills into the dead unit's own slot, so the slot across from the wolf is a known landing spot. The scene shows two refills dying there. The weak point is that Mark does nothing against a board with no refills, so the cards may read as blank. Three iterations: 'Mark, hits now' (always does something), 'Scent' (a hostile slot debuff, close to the owner's suggestion of curses that debuff enemies), and Territory (marks your own slot, written in full as a new mechanic). My pick to show the owner first is 'Mark, hits now'.

Open questions
  • 8.5 needs an owner for slot effects made in combat on the enemy board (8.5.2 only copies each board's own).
  • Should the base Mark hurt the unit already in the slot, as 'Mark, hits now' does?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag under

Rules. Needs a ruling in 8.5: slot effects made in combat on the enemy board have an owner (you), last that combat, and are not copied back to either recruit board. Entering damage should be effect damage: Shrug and Shell apply, Hunker doesn't. 'Mark, hits now' makes the card readable at Start of Combat, which answers the owner's 'doesn't make sense to me'.

Budget. Mark itself is cheap, so the units that carry it want full bodies.

  • Fox Cub: flag under. body 3 + Mark (2): ≈ 0.3 refills a fight outside summon builds × 2 × 0.85 ≈ 0.5 (proposed E97, hostile mark) [guess] ≈ 3.5 vs V(1) 5: −30%, outside ±15% and the ±1 SP floor Change: 2/2 (≈ 4.5, −10%).
  • Gubbio Wolf: in band. Close to the old review: body 5 + Mark (3) on refills ≈ 0.8 + +1/+1 this combat after a death in the mark ≈ 0.9 [guess] ≈ 6.7 vs V(2) 7: −4%, ±15%
  • Pack Alpha: edge. body 9 + Mark (3) ≈ 1.0 (more summons by turn 6) + your units attack the marked slot first (kills that unit sooner, then hits each refill ≈ 1.5; E83, E90) [guess] ≈ 11.5 vs V(4) 10: +15%, on the ±15% line Change: If the sim agrees, 4/4 (≈ 10.5, +5%).

Bends. 8.2.4: slot relations turned against the enemy, a hostile slot effect that stays when the unit there changes. It punishes 8.4.1 (a death summon takes the dead unit's slot first) and 9.8.3 (a returning unit comes back to its own slot).

From rows 8.. Creatures: Wolves and foxes scent-mark territory. In the Folio, Hunters 'follow wolves to kills'..

No. 41

Solitary

NEW
TALL

While no friendly unit is next to it, in front of it or behind it, this has double Attack.

Rules textWhile this has no friendly neighbours (8.2.3: the slots adjacent in its row, in front of it and behind it; only units count, so Eggs, Thickets and riders don't), its Attack is doubled. This is checked whenever the board changes, so it can wake mid-combat when its neighbours die, and switch off when a summon lands next to it. Doubling applies after other Attack changes.
Pine MartenTier 2
Hunters
Solitary.
2 Attack3 Health
Scottish WildcatTier 4
Hunters
Solitary. Start of Combat: if this has no friendly neighbours, gain +3 Health.
4 Attack6 Health
Lone LynxTier 6
Hunters
Solitary. After this kills an enemy while it has no friendly neighbours, gain +2/+2 permanently.
6 Attack8 Health
How they play together

A Solitary unit wants a clearing: in F1 it needs F2 and B1 empty; in F4, F3 and B3. The rest of the board still fills the other slots, so a board of five can keep one cat alone. The Wildcat gets tougher when it starts alone, and the Lynx grows permanently from kills made alone, so it is a TALL carry.

Why run it

One carry with doubled Attack trades up into almost anything. The cost is two or three empty slots, which is a real choice on a 7-slot board.

A game where it shines

Turn 8. Your Wildcat stands in F1. You leave F2 and B1 empty and put four units in F3, F4, B2 and B3. Start of Combat: the cat is alone, so it is 8/9 (doubled Attack, +3 Health). It kills an enemy 7/7 in one hit. In a later fight one of your own death summons lands in F2 next to it, and the cat drops to 4 Attack until that token dies; you learn to keep summoners away from the clearing.

Good against

Boards with one big carry it can trade into, and SCAM pieces that need two hits to land.

Weak against

Your own summons landing next to it, and WIDE boards that out-number its attacks.

Similar to

Like Magic's 'exalted', which rewards a creature for attacking alone, but here it is about the board around it.

Variations
  • Clearing
    This has +2 Attack for each empty slot among its neighbours. A graded version of the Galaxy note's idea 'If the slot beside me is empty, +2 Attack'.
  • Wake
    The first time this has no friendly neighbours in a combat, it gains +4/+4 this combat. Kill-order drama: the enemy kills its friends and wakes it up.
  • Recluse
    End of Turn: if this has no friendly neighbours, gain +1/+2 permanently. A Recruit Phase version; the gap is the price.
Your direction

No reaction to this mechanic.

Open questions
  • Doubling also doubles later buffs. Should it be a flat bonus instead (see Clearing)?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: edge

Rules. Works: 'neighbour' is 8.2.3, only units count, and the doubling applies in the multiplier layer after additions (architecture-notes.md 4), recalculated when the board changes like an aura (12.2.1). No loop; readable.

Budget. Solitary is about on budget; the two big cats print full bodies and land just over.

  • Pine Marten: in band. body 5 + doubled Attack (+2) when alone, easy on turns 2–4 when gaps are free (≈ 0.6 at Start of Combat, plus late wakes) × 0.7 realised ≈ 1.5 (E06 with C02) [guess] ≈ 6.5 vs V(2) 7: −7%, ±15%; ≈ 5.8 on full boards
  • Scottish Wildcat: edge. Same text as the old review: body 10 + doubled Attack once its neighbours die ≈ 1.7 + +3 Health if alone ≈ 0.3 [guess] ≈ 12 vs V(4) 10: +20%, ±15% Change: If the sim agrees, 4/5 (≈ 11, +10%).
  • Lone Lynx: edge. body 14 (on V(6)) + doubled Attack when its neighbours die (+6 × 0.5 of fights × 0.6 ≈ 1.8) + +2/+2 permanently per lonely kill (≈ 0.3 a fight: s ≈ 1.2, under the cap; c_combat 1.6 × 1.2 ≈ 1.9) [guess] ≈ 17.7 vs V(6) 14: +26%, ±25% Change: 6/7 (≈ 16.7, +19%).

Bends. 8.2.3 and 8.3.1: almost all position text rewards clustering; Solitary rewards gaps.

From rows 15.. Creatures: Wildcats, solitary and untameable (Folio: Clan Chattan's 'touch not the cat'). Lynxes and pine martens fit too..

No. 42

Migrate

NEW
MEDIUM

At the end of each turn, this moves itself to the next empty slot and grows a little.

Rules textEnd of Turn: this moves to the next empty slot after its own in reading order (F1, F2, F3, F4, B1, B2, B3, then back to F1) and gains +1/+1 permanently. If there is no empty slot, it stays and gains nothing. All End of Turn triggers are listed before any unit moves, so each migrator moves once. A unit 'migrates past B3' when its next empty slot can only be reached by wrapping round.
Lemming PupTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Migrate.
1 Attack2 Health
Cliff-cult LemmingTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Migrate. If this would migrate past B3, it leaps instead: remove it and give your other units +1/+1 permanently.
1 Attack1 Health
Lemming ProphetTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Whenever one of your units leaps, gain +2/+2 permanently and add a Cliff-cult Lemming to your hand.
3 Attack4 Health
How they play together

You steer a lemming by filling slots: it skips every full slot, so filling the slots in front of it makes it jump further and reach the cliff sooner. The Cliff-cult Lemming gives the board a permanent buff when it leaps off past B3. The Prophet grows from each leap and hands you a new lemming, so the march starts again.

Why run it

It is slow, steady permanent growth, and the leap is a timed payoff the player can see coming and plan for.

A game where it shines

Turn 3. You play a Cliff-cult Lemming in F3. End of Turn: F4 is empty, so it moves there as a 2/2. Turn 4: you fill B1 and B2 with new buys, so its next empty slot is B3; it jumps there as a 3/3. Turn 5: every slot after it is behind the wrap, so it leaps off the cliff, and your six other units each gain +1/+1 permanently. With a Prophet on board, a new lemming comes to your hand to start again.

Good against

Long games, where a steady permanent buff adds up.

Weak against

Full boards (no room to move) and fast tempo early, since a 1/1 that moves itself is a weak fighter.

Similar to

Like the Lemmings video game: they walk on their own, and you shape where they go.

Variations
  • Stampede
    End of Turn: all your Migrate units move together, and each gains +1/+1 for each other Migrate unit that moved.
  • Pilgrimage
    When this reaches B3, it gains +3/+3 permanently and loses Migrate. A one-trip journey with a payoff at the end.
  • Ebb
    As Migrate, but it moves backwards in reading order, from the back row to the front.
Your direction

02h on Recruit Phase position: "it is our choice where to put things and which units to put where. It can be an interesting puzzle to figure those things out."

Designer's response

Migrate keeps that puzzle: the unit moves itself, but the player decides which slots are full and so where it lands.

Open questions
  • Should it wrap from B3 to F1, or simply stop at B3 when no 'leap' text exists?
  • Does a migrator eat Bury Nuts and harvest Fallow (39) as it passes? Proposed: yes, only the slot it ends in.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: listing End of Turn triggers before any move fits 3.4.1, and wrapping past B3 is defined. Removing a Cliff-cult Lemming returns a Bought pool copy (5.3.7). Under Bury's text a migrator does eat the Nut in the slot it moves into; readable with an arrow.

Budget. Migrate's free +1/+1 a turn puts two units +42% and +100% over their tiers (band ±25%), and the Prophet closes a lemming loop that needs a sim.

  • Lemming Pup: flag over. body 3 + +1/+1 a turn while a slot ahead is empty (≈ 0.9 on turns 1–4): s ≈ 1.8, over twice the Tier-1 cap of 0.8; 3.9 × 1.8 ≈ 7.0 (E02, E32) [guess] ≈ 10 vs V(1) 5: +100%, ±25% Change: Tier 2 at 1/1, like Cliff-cult Lemming (s ≈ 1.6: 2 + 3.7 × 1.6 ≈ 7.9, +13%).
  • Cliff-cult Lemming: edge. Same as the old review: 3.7 × 1.4 ≈ 5.2 + the leap ≈ 1.6 + body 2 ≈ 8.8 vs V(2) 7: +26%, ±25% Change: None yet; sim the leap turn.
  • Lemming Prophet: flag over. body 7 + +2/+2 permanently per leap (≈ 0.35 leaps a turn in a lemming build: s ≈ 1.4; 3.1 × 1.4 ≈ 4.3) + a Cliff-cult Lemming per leap (≈ 0.7 gold-equivalent a turn ≈ 2.9; E40) [guess] ≈ 14.2 vs V(4) 10: +42%, ±25%. With it, Cliff-cult Lemmings come back forever, so their leap's +1/+1 to your units repeats too Change: +1/+1 per leap instead of +2/+2, keeping the new lemming (≈ 12.1, +21%).

Bends. 8.3.3: the player places units freely; a Migrate unit places itself, and the player steers it only by choosing which slots to fill. It reads position at End of Turn.

From rows 16. The recruit list's stork 'Migrate' is a different mechanic, renamed Fly South (60).. Creatures: Lemmings and their 'cheerful, endless tide' (Folio take 'The Great Migration'); their doom cult, 'believers in the cliff'..

No. 43

Stance

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Choose One
MEDIUM

This card has a front-row side and a back-row side, and it uses whichever matches the row it is in.

Rules textThis card has a front text and a back text, each with its own stats, rules or both. It uses the one for the row it is in. If it changes row in combat, it switches at once. Buffs apply on top of either side, and damage taken stays when it switches: if the new side leaves it at 0 Health or less, it dies.
PipistrelleTier 1
Neutral
Front: 3/1. Back: 1/3, and Start of Combat: deal 1 damage to a random enemy.
3 Attack1 Health
Double-agent BatTier 3
Neutral
Front: 5/2. Back: 2/5, and the units in front of this have +1 Attack.
5 Attack2 Health
Noctule CaptainTier 5
Neutral
Front: 7/4. Back: 3/7, and after a friendly unit changes row, give it +2/+2 this combat.
7 Attack4 Health
How they play together

Each bat is two cards in one, so one buy fills whichever role the board lacks. Early, the Pipistrelle is a 3/1 attacker in front or a pinger in back. The Double-agent Bat in the back row is a sturdy aura for the two units in front of it. The Noctule Captain rewards Rotate (36), Bolt (37) and Caravan-style moves, which change units' rows mid-fight.

Why run it

Flexibility. A Stance unit never sits in the wrong row, and boards that move units in combat get a second use out of every move.

A game where it shines

Turn 5. Your Double-agent Bat sits in B1 as a 2/5 and gives F1 and F2 +1 Attack. In the fight, your Lead Goose (36) in F2 attacks and rotates with the healthiest unit behind it: the bat. The bat comes forward into F2 as a 5/2, unflagged, and attacks next. If the bat had taken 3 damage while in the back, coming forward would have killed it, so you plan the rotation.

Good against

Lobbies that change fast, where one card that can play two roles keeps the board useful.

Weak against

Flush (19) and other enemy moves that drag a back-row bat forward into its fragile side.

Similar to

Like Hearthstone's Choose One, or a double-faced card in Magic, but the row picks the face and it can flip mid-fight.

Variations
  • Perch
    Stats only: Front: +3 Attack. Back: +3 Health. The simplest Stance, for any tribe.
  • Turncoat
    Text only: Front: 'Whenever this attacks, gain +1 Attack.' Back: 'Start of Combat: give the units in front of this +2 Health.'
  • Choose by placement
    When played, it locks to the side for the row it was played into, and keeps it even if it moves. Battlegrounds' Choose One, chosen by the slot.
Your direction

02h on Recruit Phase position: "if I have two different fairies, one that buffs things behind it and one that buffs things in front of it, it is our choice where to put things ... It can be an interesting puzzle to figure those things out."

Designer's response

Stance is that puzzle inside one card: where you put it decides what it is.

Open questions
  • Two stat lines on one card are busy. The card frame needs a clear front and back half.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: in band

Rules. Works as a base-stat switch in the stat layers, with death on switching stated. Say which side applies when it is in hand or off the board (proposed: front), which hand-buff cards such as Rehearse need. Busy as a card, as the designer says; the Perch variation is the readable form.

Budget. Stance prices cleanly as a body with a row condition, and all three are in band.

  • Pipistrelle: in band. Front 3/1 = 4 (−20%); back 0.75 + 3 + Start of Combat 1 damage ≈ 0.85 = 4.6 (−8%). The player picks the better side: ≈ 4.6 + 0.3 for the choice ≈ 4.9 vs V(1) 5: −2%, ±15%
  • Double-agent Bat: in band. Same as the old review: front 5/2 = 7 (−12%); back 2/5 = 6.5 + +1 Attack to the units in front (1.9 measured on Lantern Moth) = 8.4 (+5%), ±15%
  • Noctule Captain: in band. Front 7/4 = 11 (−8%); back 2.25 + 7 + +2/+2 this combat to a unit that changes row (≈ 1.5 row changes a fight in a mover build × 4 × 0.7 × 0.6 ≈ 2.5) ≈ 11.75 (−2%) [guess], ±15%. Priced on combat row changes only

Bends. 8.2.7 and 7.2.1: a row becomes a mode switch. 12.2.1's 'recalculated whenever the board changes' now covers a unit's own stats, so moves in combat (Rotate, Bolt, Flush) flip it mid-fight.

From rows 17.. Creatures: Bats. Aesop's bat belongs to neither birds nor beasts and changes sides to suit itself (Folio take 'Double agents')..

How they play together

Where you put the stork decides where your summons go. A stork in the back row sends tokens into protected back slots; in the front row it keeps them in the line. Bundle of Joy is a cheap summon to deliver, and the Sorter makes each later summon bigger. Delivery also sends tokens into a Sett (38) or onto a Nut (39), and away from enemy Marks (40) and Eggs (34).

Why run it

Summons get +1/+1 and land where you want them, which matters on a board where slot position is everything.

A game where it shines

Turn 7. Your opponent's wolves mark your F2 every fight. You play a Rooftop Stork in B2 and your Bundle of Joy in F2. In the fight the Bundle dies in F2. Good against

Mark, Brood, Cleave and any enemy text that punishes where your summons land.

Weak against

Anything that kills the stork first; then placement snaps back to normal.

Variations
  • Doorstep
    While this lives, your summons go into the slot in front of this (or the nearest empty one) and gain +1/+1.
  • Special Delivery
    The first unit you summon each combat is delivered next to this and gains +3/+3. A one-off version, easier to read.
  • Wrong Address
    While this lives, enemy summons go into their empty slot farthest from their source. A tech card against slot-based WIDE boards.

No. 45

Both Ends

NEW
MEDIUMSTICKY

Put one hedgehog at each end of a row: they get tougher, and whichever one the enemy attacks, the other one pricks the attacker first.

Rules textBoth Ends works in pairs. While you have a Both Ends unit in F1 and another in F4 (or one in B1 and one in B3), each of the two has +1/+2. Whenever an enemy attacks one of the pair, the other first deals 2 damage to the attacker. If that kills the attacker, the attack doesn't happen. Any two Both Ends units make a pair; they don't need to be the same card. The empty or full slots between them don't matter.
Hedgehog HogletTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Both Ends.
1 Attack2 Health
Hedgehog TwinTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Both Ends.
2 Attack3 Health
Furrow RacerTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Both Ends. Whenever the other end of your pair pricks an attacker, give your units in this row +1 Attack this combat.
4 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Picture the front row as a furrow with a hedgehog at each end, F1 and F4, and your other units in F2 and F3. Every enemy attack on either hedgehog costs the attacker 2 Health before the blow lands. Small attackers die before they swing. The Furrow Racer turns each prick into +1 Attack for the whole row. Kill one hedgehog and the other loses its bonus, so the enemy's kill order matters.

Why run it

Enemy attacks pick front units at random, so with two of four front slots held by hedgehogs, about half of the enemy's attacks get pricked. And the corner slots, which normally do nothing special, get a job.

A game where it shines

Turn 4. You play Hedgehog Hoglet in F1 and Hedgehog Twin in F4, with a 3/3 in F2 and a 4/4 in F3. The pair is set: the Hoglet is a 2/4 and the Twin a 3/5. In the fight an enemy 3/2 attacks the Hoglet in F1. Before the blow lands, the Twin at the far end throws its quills: 2 damage, and the 3/2 dies without hitting. Next an enemy 4/5 attacks the Twin in F4. The Hoglet pricks it to 4/3, the Twin hits back for 3 and kills it, and the Twin survives at 3/1.

Good against

WIDE boards of small attackers, many of which die to the prick before they swing.

Weak against

Random damage and Start of Combat pings that kill one hedgehog, which breaks the pair, and big attackers that shrug off 2.

Similar to

Like the fable The Hare and the Hedgehog: wherever the enemy goes, a hedgehog is already at the other end.

Variations
  • Far End
    One card, no pair: whenever an enemy attacks the unit at the other end of this row, this deals damage equal to its Attack to the attacker first.
  • Twin Strike
    Whenever an enemy attacks one of the pair, the other attacks that enemy immediately. The other hedgehog jumps in with a full attack, and takes the hit back.
  • Already Here
    When one of the pair dies, the other gains its Attack and Health this combat. The race is lost, but the hedgehog at the far end is waiting.
Your direction

"I don't understand Both Ends. I still don't get the proposal." (02h). Direction: example cards and a play scene.

Designer's response

The rules now name the slots (F1 and F4, or B1 and B3) instead of 'the other end of this row', and the scene walks through two attacks step by step. 'Far End' is the simplest version to show if the pair is the confusing part: one card that guards the opposite corner.

Open questions
  • Should a pair in F1 and F4 still count if one of them is moved in combat (Rotate, Bolt)? Proposed: the pair is checked all the time, like an aura.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a fixed-slot pair (F1 with F4, B1 with B3), checked all the time like an aura. Needs the 'attacker gone' line in 9.6.1, shared with Ambush and Territory, and the pre-strike should count as effect damage (the attacker's Bristle pricks the far hedgehog, which is finite because thorns never trigger thorns, R2). The F1-and-F4 picture makes it understandable; the card should show the partner slot.

Budget. Both Ends is cheap because it needs a partner; the Tier-4 racer has a full body plus text.

  • Hedgehog Hoglet: edge. body 3 + (+1/+2 ≈ 3 + a 2-damage prick ≈ 1.5) × a partner at the other end ≈ 0.25 on turns 1–3 ≈ 1.1 (E13, E72 on proposed R33) [guess] ≈ 4.1 vs V(1) 5: −18%, just inside the ±1 SP floor Change: If the sim agrees, 1/3 (≈ 5.1, +2%).
  • Hedgehog Twin: in band. Same as the old review: body 5 + (3 + 1.5) × 0.4 partner ≈ 1.8 ≈ 6.8 vs V(2) 7: −3%, ±15%
  • Furrow Racer: flag over. body 9 + Both Ends (≈ 5 × 0.5 partner at turn 6 ≈ 2.5) + +1 Attack this combat to its row per prick (≈ 1.2 pricks × 0.5 × 3 units × 0.6 ≈ 1.1) [guess] ≈ 12.6 vs V(4) 10: +26%, ±15% Change: 3/4 (≈ 10.6, +6%).

Bends. 8.2.2: adjacency never skips a gap; this relation spans a whole row, gaps and all. It gives the flank slots (F1 and F4) a job the centre can't do. 9.6.1 needs an 'attacker gone' line to match its 'defender gone' line.

From rows 14.. Creatures: Hedgehogs. In Grimm's The Hare and the Hedgehog, the hedgehog's wife waits at the far end of the furrow, so whichever end the hare reaches, a hedgehog is already there (Folio take 'Twin tricksters'). The owner's thorn idea, struck from the far end..

The water stays up after this unit dies.

How they play together

Beavers raise the water from the back row; swimmers in your front row get stronger while the enemy's front row gets weaker. Swim is a keyword any tribe can carry, so voles, otters and frogs all join. The Lodge Builder adds a second plan: a strong, dry back row.

Why run it

It shrinks the enemy's front carry and grows your swimmers in one move, and both players can see it coming.

A game where it shines

Turn 7. Your opponent runs a 14/14 boar in front. You have two Dam-Warden Beavers in the back and two Ditch Otters plus a Water Vole in front. Start of Combat: the water rises to 2. Good against

Front-row TALL carries.

Weak against

Boards whose carries sit in the back row, and other boards with Swim.

Similar to

Like weather in Pokemon: Rain helps Water moves for both sides.

Variations
  • Rain
    Start of Combat: the enemy front row has -1 Attack this combat, and your Swim units +1. One-sided and simpler, no shared level.
  • Rising Tide
    After every 5 attacks in this combat, raise the water by 1. The water rises as the fight goes on.
  • Marsh Slot
    End of Turn: this unit's slot becomes Marsh (a slot effect). A Swim unit in a Marsh slot has +2/+2; any other unit there has -1 Attack.

No reaction to this mechanic.

Gold over turns

No. 47

Cache

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' anomaly Prudence of Amitus
ECOSTICKY

Hide some gold in this squirrel; next turn you get it back plus 1, unless the squirrel died in the fight.

Rules textCache (X). Once per turn, you may bury up to X gold in this unit. It has +1 Health for each buried gold. At the start of your next turn, dig it all up plus 1 gold. If this died in the last combat, dig up only half (rounded down) and get a 1/2 Oak Seedling in your hand instead of the +1 (lost if your hand is full). Selling this returns its buried gold, with no +1. In a triple, buried gold carries over to the golden unit. Golden: X stays the same; the bonus is +2.
Harvest MouseTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Cache (2).
1 Attack1 Health
Nutkin TellerTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Cache (3).
1 Attack2 Health
Acorn Exchange ClerkTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Your Cache units dig up 1 more gold each, and have +1 Attack for each gold buried in them.
3 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Each Cache unit is a small bank that also gets tougher while it holds gold. Keep it alive and it pays interest; let it die and you lose half. So the bank wants the back row and STICKY friends. The Clerk raises the interest and turns the buried gold into Attack too, so a full bank fights.

Why run it

Gold that sits unspent earns nothing in Bramble. Cache gives it a return, tied to an action (burying) and to keeping the squirrel alive.

A game where it shines

Turn 3. You have 6 gold. You buy one unit for 3, then bury the other 3 in your Nutkin Teller in B1, which fights as a 1/5. Your front row holds, the squirrel lives, and on Turn 4 you dig up 4 gold on top of your income: enough to upgrade and buy in the same turn.

Good against

Slow lobbies where you can protect the back row.

Weak against

Back-row reach and random damage, which find the fat squirrel and rob the bank.

Similar to

Battlegrounds' anomaly Prudence of Amitus: 'Unspent Gold carries over to your next turn. If you saved at least 5, gain 1 extra.' Cache puts that interest on a unit that can die.

Variations
  • Long Cache
    Bury gold for two turns instead of one; dig up all of it plus 3. 'Take a little now or more later.'
  • Seed Cache
    Once per turn, put a unit from your hand under this. At the start of your next turn it comes back to your hand with +2/+2.
  • Shared Hoard
    Start of turn: if this survived the last combat, gain 1 gold for every 5 gold you carried over (up to 2).
Your direction

02h on economy: "I like a lot of the economy ideas of tying economy effects to specific actions that kind of reward you for being in that type of strategy." And on Acorns: "take a little bit of stuff up front now, or get more later".

Designer's response

Cache ties its interest to an action (burying) and to the board (the squirrel must survive), so it pays best in a back-row, STICKY build. Long Cache is the 'more later' version.

Open questions
  • Combat deaths happen on a copy (9.1.1), so the game must remember whether this unit died in the last combat.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: edge

Rules. Works as an activated ability (3.3.1) with digging at the start-of-turn step (3.2.1 step 5), and keeping X on golden fits 7.3.5. It needs R9's combat report for 'died in the last combat'. Say it can only hold gold while on the board; readable.

Budget. Cache prices as conditional income and stays in band; the Clerk makes every Cache unit pay even when it dies.

  • Harvest Mouse: in band. body 2 + bury 1–2 gold a turn: +1 gold if it survives (≈ 0.35 early), lose up to 1 and get a 1/2 Seedling if it dies: ≈ +0.35 gold a turn × ≈ 5 (Tier-1 recurring gold beats a plain unit at a sink of 2.0) ≈ 1.8 + Health while buried ≈ 1.5 (E50) [guess] ≈ 5.3 vs V(1) 5: +6%, ±25%

No. 48

Thrift / Revel

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Tavern Tipper
ECOMEDIUM

Thrift units reward you for saving some gold this turn; Revel units reward you for spending every last coin.

Rules textThrift: End of Turn: if you have more gold than you carried into this turn (before this turn's income), do the effect. Revel: End of Turn: if you have 0 gold, do the effect.
Worker AntTier 1
Hive
Thrift: gain +1/+1 permanently.
1 Attack2 Health
Cicada BandleaderTier 2
Neutral
Revel: give this and the units behind it +2/+1 permanently.
3 Attack2 Health
Granary AntTier 3
Hive
Thrift: give your back-row units +1/+2 permanently.
2 Attack4 Health
Grasshopper FiddlerTier 5
Neutral
Thrift: give your back-row units +2/+2 permanently. Revel: give your front-row units +2/+2 permanently.
5 Attack4 Health
How they play together

Thrift units pay when you bank part of this turn's income; Revel units pay when you end at 0. Since refreshes cost 1, a Revel board can always dump its last coins into the shop. The Fiddler pays either way, so each turn the question becomes which row to grow.

Why run it

Gold carry-over makes saving and spending equal; these units make your choice each turn grow the board. Thrift suits a board saving up for a big upgrade turn; Revel suits a board rolling for pieces.

A game where it shines

Turn 5. You have 9 gold, 2 of it carried over. You are saving for Tier 4 next turn, so you buy one unit and end with 6: more than the 2 you carried in, so the Granary Ant gives your back row +1/+2. On Turn 6 you upgrade, roll and spend everything: the Cicada Bandleader triggers instead.

Good against

Thrift: lobbies slow enough for a delayed spike. Revel: fast lobbies, where it hits hard now.

Weak against

Thrift: early pressure while you bank. Revel: turns where you need to save for an upgrade.

Similar to

Battlegrounds' retired Tavern Tipper: 'If you have any unspent Gold at the end of your turn, gain +1/+2.' Revel mirrors it.

Variations
  • Profit
    End of Turn: if you have more gold than you had after this turn's income came in, do the effect. Only selling and gold effects reach it, so it rewards ECO actions.
  • Feast
    End of Turn: if you spent 10 or more gold this turn, do the effect. Revel for big turns, no need to hit exactly 0.
  • Lean Years
    End of Turn: if you have banked gold for 3 turns in a row, do the effect, then the count restarts. A time-bound Thrift, as pillar 13 suggests.
Your direction

02h on economy: "tying economy effects to specific actions that kind of reward you for being in that type of strategy." Pillar 13: a design that rewards not acting is fine if isolated or time-bound.

Designer's response

Thrift rewards a choice, saving part of your income, while every other action stays open, so it is isolated in the sense of pillar 13. I read 'started the turn with' as the gold carried in before income, so that Thrift means 'you saved some'. The rules review's reading (after income) is kept as the Profit variation, since it rewards selling and gold effects instead.

Open questions
  • Which reading of 'started the turn with' does the owner want: before income (save some) or after income (make a profit)?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: both read gold at End of Turn (3.4.1 step 1), and 'carried into this turn' is a stored value from 3.2.1 step 1. Say whether gold dug up from Cache counts as carried in or gained this turn (proposed: gained). Readable.

Budget. Whole-row permanent buffs every turn put all four units +75% to +235% over their tiers (band ±25%); the save-or-spend choice survives if each trigger buffs one unit.

  • Worker Ant: flag over. Thrift now fires whenever you save part of this turn's income, ≈ 0.8 of turns in a Thrift build: s ≈ 2 × 0.8 = 1.6, twice the Tier-1 cap; 3.9 × 1.6 ≈ 6.2 − banking cost ≈ 0.5 [guess] ≈ 8.7 vs V(1) 5: +75%, ±25% Change: Thrift: gain +1 Health permanently (s ≈ 0.7, under the cap): ≈ 5.3 (+6%).
  • Cicada Bandleader: flag over. Same text as the old review: Revel on ≈ 0.7 of turns, +2/+1 to this and ≈ 1.5 units behind: s ≈ 4.7, over three times the Tier-2 cap; ≈ 22 vs V(2) 7: +214%, ±25% Change: 1/1, 'Revel: give this and the units behind it +1 Attack permanently' (s ≈ 1.5, at the cap): ≈ 7.5 (+7%).
  • Granary Ant: flag over. +1/+2 to ≈ 2.5 back units (≈ 6.9 SP) a fire × 0.8 of turns (the new wording fires far more often than the old review's 0.3): s ≈ 5.5, over three times the Tier-3 cap; 3.5 × 5.5 ≈ 19 [guess] ≈ 25 vs V(3) 8: +215%, ±25% Change: 1/2, 'Thrift: give a random back-row unit +1/+1 permanently' (s ≈ 1.4): ≈ 7.4 (−8%).

Bends. 4.1.2 to 4.1.3. Carry-over makes banking neutral; these put a board reward on each side, so 'save or spend' becomes a build identity.

From recruit 2.. Creatures: Aesop's ant and grasshopper; the Folio's 'The Hive and the Carnival': thrifty ants and bees against a cicada brass band..

No. 49

Upkeep (X)

NEW (merged with Hungry)
TALLECO

A cheap unit that hits far above its tier, but you must feed it gold every turn or it fights at half Attack.

Rules textUpkeep (X): At the start of your turn, after your gold is set and before start-of-turn triggers, choose for each Upkeep unit: pay X gold, or it Starves until your next turn starts. A Starved unit has half its Attack, rounded down (applied after all other Attack changes). A golden Upkeep unit keeps the same X. Hungry (the creature version): In each attack cycle, this attacks before your other units; then your side continues in reading order, and this doesn't move the attack pointer. At the start of your turn it eats 1 gold; if you have none, it loses 1/1 permanently.
Pygmy Shrew BerserkerTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Upkeep (1).
4 Attack3 Health
Shrew QuartermasterTier 2
Hedgerow folk
After you pay Upkeep for a unit, give it +1/+1 permanently.
2 Attack3 Health
Shrew Warband ChiefTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Upkeep (2). Start of Combat: if you paid Upkeep for every unit this turn, give your Upkeep units +2 Attack this combat.
5 Attack5 Health
How they play together

The Berserker is a 4/3 on turn 1, when everything else is a 2/3 or a 3/2. The Quartermaster turns every payment into a permanent +1/+1, so a shrew you keep feeding becomes a slow scaler instead of a tax. The Chief pays out only on turns you fed everyone, so starving one shrew to afford an upgrade has a real cost. Any card that makes gold now also makes shrew stats.

Why run it

The most fight power per gold on turns 1 to 4, when fights decide early health. Later, the bill is small next to your income, and with the Quartermaster the bill is buying stats.

A game where it shines

Turn 1, you buy Pygmy Shrew Berserker and win a fight nobody else can win. Turn 2, you have 4 gold: pay 1 upkeep, buy Shrew Quartermaster with the rest, and the Berserker grows to 5/4. Turn 3 you want to upgrade, so you let the Berserker starve (2/4 this fight) and accept a close loss. Turn 6 you add the Chief: you pay 3 gold at the start of each turn, and in the fight your shrews have +2 Attack.

Good against

Economy boards and slow scalers in the first five turns, which take damage while they build.

Weak against

Long games against tall boards: shrews stop scaling once you stop paying, and a short turn means starving them.

Similar to

Magic: The Gathering's upkeep costs, where you pay each turn or lose the creature.

Variations
  • Hungry
    Pygmy Shrew, Tier 1, 3/1: "Hungry." Attacks first in each cycle; eats 1 gold at the start of each turn, or loses 1/1 permanently.
  • Tier Upkeep
    "Upkeep: pay gold equal to your tier, or this Starves." The cost grows as you level, so it stays a real choice on turn 12.
  • Lean Times
    "Upkeep (1). While Starved, this has +4 Health." Starving becomes a choice: a cheap wall this fight, or a full hitter.
Open questions
  • A Hungry unit in the back row attacks first but can't be attacked while the front row lives. Should Hungry work only from the front row?
  • Fixed X or tier-scaled X? With uncapped carry-over, 1 gold is a lot on turn 2 and almost nothing on turn 12.
  • Does a Starved unit show its halved Attack in the Recruit Phase, so the opponent's snapshot reads it correctly?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Upkeep works as a new step in 3.2.1 between gold and start-of-turn triggers, and the Starved half Attack sits in the last stat layer. Needs rulings for Hungry: it attacks out of turn at the start of each cycle (R5), so say it is then flagged and skips its own slot's turn; and a 1-Health Hungry unit at 0 gold drops to 0 Health and dies in the Recruit Phase (5.3.7), which the text should say. The snapshot copies the Starved state into the fight (9.1.1).

Budget. Upkeep is a fair option on its own; the Quartermaster turns every payment into permanent stats and needs a once-per-turn limit.

  • Pygmy Shrew Berserker: in band. Same as the old review: starved 2/3 = 5; paying 1 gold for +2 Attack is a fair option ≈ +0.5 → ≈ 5.5 vs V(1) 5: +10%, ±25% (proposed E98)
  • Shrew Quartermaster: flag over. body 5 + +1/+1 permanently per Upkeep payment (≈ 1.2 Upkeep units × 0.6 build ≈ 0.7 payments a turn: s ≈ 1.4, over the Tier-2 cap of 1.3; 3.7 × 1.4 ≈ 5.2) [guess] ≈ 10.2 vs V(2) 7: +46%, ±25%; with three Upkeep units s ≈ 6 Change: 1/2, once per turn (s ≈ 1.2): ≈ 7.4 (+6%).
  • Shrew Warband Chief: in band. starved 2/5 = 7; paying 2 gold buys +3 Attack plus Start of Combat +2 Attack to ≈ 1.5 Upkeep units (≈ 2.4 × 0.8), ≈ 5 SP for gold worth ≈ 4: ≈ +1.4 + option ≈ 0.3 [guess] ≈ 8.7 vs V(4) 10: −13%, ±25%

Bends. 3.2.1 (the start of a turn gives gold and never takes it) and 4.1: a recurring cost on a body. Hungry also bends 9.4.2 and 9.4.5: it jumps the reading order.

From recruit 3, creatures 11.. Creatures: Shrews: they must eat every few hours and are the fastest things in the hedge..

No. 50

Wager (X)

NEW
ECO

Bet some of your gold on winning your next fight: win and you get double back, lose and it's gone.

Rules textWager (X): At End of Turn you may stake up to X gold on your next fight. The stake leaves your gold at once. If you win, you gain double the stake at the start of your next turn. If you tie, you get the stake back. If you lose, it is gone. You make at most one wager a turn, up to your highest Wager value. A fight against a ghost or a recorded board settles the same way; the result is known before your next turn starts.
Otter BookieTier 2
Hunters
Wager (2).
2 Attack3 Health
Ransom OtterTier 3
Hunters
Wager (3). After you win a wager, this gains +1/+1 permanently.
3 Attack3 Health
High-Stakes OtterTier 5
Hunters
Wager (5). Start of Combat: give your front-row units +1 Attack for each gold you staked this turn (this combat).
5 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Wager values don't add: a second otter only raises your cap, so you run several for the payoffs, not the cap. The Ransom Otter grows with every bet you win, and the High-Stakes Otter turns the stake itself into fight power, so a bigger bet makes the win more likely.

Why run it

It turns a board that is already ahead into gold, so a winning streak pays for your upgrades without slowing your board.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you have won three fights in a row and your next opponent's board looks weaker than yours. You end the turn with 3 gold and stake all of it with Ransom Otter. You win: turn 5 starts with 6 extra gold, the otter is 4/4, and you upgrade and buy in the same turn.

Good against

Slow starters and economy boards whose early boards are thin.

Weak against

Close, swingy fights and losing streaks, where each lost bet sets you back further.

Similar to

Teamfight Tactics' Fortune trait, where you bank on a streak and cash out; poker-style betting.

Variations
  • Stake a Unit
    "Wager: at End of Turn you may stake a unit. Lose the fight and it is destroyed; win and it gains +3/+3 permanently."
  • Insured
    "Wager (4). On a loss, get half the stake back, rounded down." A safer bet that pays the same on a win.
  • Side Bet
    "End of Turn: you may stake up to 3 gold that the enemy unit across from this dies in the next fight. If it does, gain double."
Open questions
  • In async play the stake is a bet on your own board against a recorded one. Is that still fun, or should the bet be on something you can read better?
  • Does a golden double X (a stake cap is a number, but it works like a threshold under 7.3.5)?
  • Does Wager need a late-game ceiling on the payout, given uncapped gold?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works against a recorded board: the stake leaves at End of Turn and settles at the next start of turn. Needs a ruling on golden X (a stake cap reads like a threshold under 7.3.5, R12) and depends on async play showing the next opponent (2.3.3 under 2.1.5's open question). Readable.

Budget. Wager is fair if players can't read their next fight; the High-Stakes payoff turns a 5-gold stake into a 20-Attack front row.

  • Otter Bookie: in band. body 5 + staking 2 when you expect to win (≈ 0.65 win rate, on ≈ 0.6 of turns): ≈ +0.36 gold a turn × 6.5 (Tier-2 recurring price) ≈ 2.3 (E50 on R28) [guess] ≈ 7.3 vs V(2) 7: +4%, ±25%; ≈ 5 (−29%) if players can't pick their fights
  • Ransom Otter: edge. Same as the old review: ≈ 11.3 (+41%) if players pick their fights, ≈ 9.5 (+19%) if not, ±25% Change: If the bot shows players pick their fights, 2/2 (≈ 9.3, +16%).
  • High-Stakes Otter: flag over. body 10 + Wager (5) ≈ 0.9 gold a turn × 4.1 ≈ 3.7 + up to +5 Attack to each front unit this combat (≈ 20 Attack × 0.9 of turns × 0.8 ≈ 14; E03) [guess] ≈ 28 vs V(5) 12: +130%, ±25% Change: 3/3, and the Attack goes to one random front-row unit (+1 per gold staked ≈ 3.6): ≈ 13.3 (+11%).

Bends. 10.1: a fight pays out only in hero damage. Wager pays out in gold too. It uses the next-opponent information you already have (2.3.3).

No. 51

Bargain

NEW
ECOTALL

A fairy gives you something big now, and you pay its price a few turns later.

Rules textBargain: When you play this, you get its gift now. Its price is due at the start of your turn a set number of turns later; the due turn shows on the card and on your hero. The debt is yours, not the unit's: selling, tripling or losing the unit doesn't clear it. If you sell the unit before the price is due, you pay the price at once, doubled. A price in gold that you can't pay in full takes what you have, then its fallback happens. A golden Bargain unit doubles both its gift and its price.
Hob of the CrossroadsTier 2
Fairies
Bargain: give a unit +4/+4 permanently. Price, in 2 turns: your shop has one fewer unit that turn.
2 Attack2 Health
Toll BrownieTier 3
Fairies
After you pay a Bargain price, give your Fairies +1/+1 permanently.
3 Attack4 Health
Thrice-Kissed SpriteTier 4
Fairies
Bargain: gain 4 gold. Price, in 3 turns: pay 6 gold. Fallback: your highest-Attack unit loses 4 Attack permanently.
3 Attack3 Health
How they play together

The Hob puts a big buff on your carry two turns early; the Sprite is a loan for the turn you need to upgrade and buy at once. The Toll Brownie turns every repaid debt into a buff for your Fairies, so a fairy player who plans for the due days comes out ahead. The price sometimes lands on the board (a lost 4 Attack), so opponents can see it in fights.

Why run it

Tempo when you need it most: a spike turn now, paid for on a turn when you can afford it.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you have 7 gold and need 8 to upgrade and buy the unit you want. You play Thrice-Kissed Sprite: +4 gold, upgrade to tier 4, buy. Turn 7 the 6 gold falls due. You kept gold back on turns 5 and 6, and with Toll Brownie on board, paying it gives your three Fairies +1/+1.

Good against

Opponents you need to beat on one key turn, such as a lobby leader on a streak.

Weak against

Bad due days: a loss streak or a dry shop on the turn the price comes due.

Similar to

Magic: The Gathering's Pact cards: free now, pay on your next turn or lose.

Variations
  • Fairy Gold
    "Bargain: gain 3 gold now. Price: none, but the gold vanishes at End of Turn if unspent." A same-turn loan with no debt.
  • Open Bargain
    "Bargain: gain 4 gold. Pay 4 back whenever you like; each turn unpaid, the price rises by 1." You choose the due day.
  • Changeling's Price
    "Bargain: get a Created copy of a unit in your shop. Price, in 2 turns: a random unit on your board is replaced by a Created 1/1."
Open questions
  • Should prices more often land on the board, so the debt is visible in fights, rather than in gold?
  • Does a Bargain price count as a start-of-turn trigger (reading order) or a separate debt step before them?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. The player-level debt is well specified, including selling and fallbacks. Needs a ruling on where debts are paid in 3.2.1, and their order against Upkeep (proposed: debts after gold is set, then Upkeep, then start-of-turn triggers). Say that Pair's 'triggers twice' doubles both gift and price; the due turn shown on the hero keeps it readable.

Budget. Bargain prices well when the price is real; the Hob's +4/+4 gift is the one still over.

  • Hob of the Crossroads: flag over. Same as the old review: body 4 + +4/+4 now (0.9 × 8 ≈ 7.2) − one fewer shop unit once ≈ 0.5 ≈ 10.7 vs V(2) 7: +53%, ±15% Change: 1/1 and +3/+3 (≈ 6.9, −1%).
  • Toll Brownie: edge. body 7 + +1/+1 permanently to ≈ 2.5 Fairies per Bargain price paid (≈ 0.2 a turn: s ≈ 0.9, under the cap; 3.5 × 0.9 ≈ 3.2) [guess] ≈ 10.2 vs V(3) 8: +28%, ±25% Change: If the sim agrees, 2/4 (≈ 9.2, +15%).
  • Thrice-Kissed Sprite: in band. body 6 + 4 gold now (≈ 8.4 at about 2.1 a gold) − the cheaper price: spend to 0 on debt day and the carry loses 4 Attack (≈ −5) rather than pay 6 gold (≈ −12) [guess] ≈ 9.4 vs V(4) 10: −6%, ±25%. Group M

The shop: buying, selling, refreshing, tiers

No. 52

Pawn

NEW
ECOTALL

Sell a unit for more than usual, with a chance to buy it back next turn for a little extra.

Rules textPawn: Once per turn, instead of selling a Bought unit with Pawn, you may pawn it: gain 3 gold, and it goes into your Pawn slot at the end of your shop, frozen, keeping its buffs. Until the end of your next turn you may buy it back for 4 gold; it returns to your hand, Bought, and triggers "when played" as normal when you play it. Pawning isn't selling, so "when sold" effects don't trigger. If you don't buy it back in time, it is forfeit and returns to the pool (a golden returns copies under 5.3.5). Created units can't be pawned. You have one Pawn slot.
Carrion Crow FenceTier 1
Birds
Pawn. When this is forfeit, gain 1 gold.
2 Attack1 Health
Jackdaw PawnbrokerTier 2
Birds
Your other units have Pawn.
2 Attack3 Health
Jackdaw UncleTier 4
Birds
After you buy back a pawned unit, give it +2/+2 permanently.
4 Attack4 Health
How they play together

The Pawnbroker gives your whole board the option, so any unit can become a loan of 3 gold for one fight. The Uncle makes buying back a buff, so a pawn cycle loses 1 gold but gains +2/+2. The Crow Fence is a body you are happy to let go: pawning it and letting it lapse earns 4 gold in total.

Why run it

It lands an upgrade or a triple a turn early, and you get the unit back after the spike.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, you have 8 gold. You need 9 to upgrade, plus 3 for the third Mole that makes a triple. You pawn your Hedgehog wall for 3, upgrade, buy the Mole and triple. You fight one body short. Turn 6 you buy the Hedgehog back for 4, and with Jackdaw Uncle it comes back +2/+2.

Good against

Opponents racing to the same triple or tier: you get there first.

Weak against

Aggressive boards that punish the fight you play a body short.

Similar to

A real pawnshop loan.

Variations
  • Long Loan
    "Pawn: gain 3 gold. Buy back for 4, plus 1 for each turn it has been pawned. It is never forfeit, but it takes up the Pawn slot."
  • Quick Hock
    "Pawn for 2 gold; buy back for 2, this turn only." A free loan inside one turn, for landing a triple.
Open questions
  • Should buying back go to hand (and retrigger "when played") or straight back to its slot (no retrigger)? Hand is cleaner; slot is safer for free-refresh triggers.
  • Can a pawned unit be refreshed away, or is the Pawn slot outside refreshes entirely? Proposed: outside.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a sell alternative with its own slot outside refreshes. Needs a ruling on 'once per turn': per player or per Pawn unit, which matters once the Pawnbroker gives every unit Pawn. Buying back to hand re-fires 'when played', so under G1 any 'when played: refresh the shop' unit becomes a refresh for about 1 gold; once per turn passes G1, but it must be reviewed against every retrigger (R11).

Budget. Pawning is a better sale, which is gold, and gold at Tiers 1–2 is worth a lot, so the gold-makers sit better a tier or two higher.

  • Carrion Crow Fence: flag over. body 3 + pawning instead of selling gives 3 gold and +1 more on forfeit: +3 gold over a normal sale, paid around turns 3–5 (≈ 6; E54) [guess] ≈ 9 vs V(1) 5: +80%, ±25%; at the Tier-1 table ≈ 6.5 (+30%), still over Change: Tier 2, same text (the gold arrives around turns 5–6, ≈ 5.4): ≈ 8.4 (+20%).

No. 53

Barter

NEW
MEDIUMWIDE

Once a turn, swap a unit on your board for a unit in the shop without paying.

Rules textBarter: Once per turn for each unit with Barter, instead of buying, you may swap one of your board units with a unit in your shop. The shop unit goes straight into the board unit's slot; it isn't played, so "when played" effects don't trigger, and it takes the old unit's origin (Bought or Created). The old unit goes into that shop slot with its buffs and costs the normal 3 to buy back. If the shop refreshes, or the turn ends with the shop unfrozen, it returns to the pool and loses its buffs; a Created unit vanishes instead. If the new unit completes a triple, it triples at once (7.3.1).
Fox Cub PeddlerTier 1
Hunters
Barter.
2 Attack1 Health
Rag-and-Bone FoxTier 3
Hunters
Barter. After you barter, the new unit gains +1/+1 permanently.
3 Attack3 Health
Market VixenTier 5
Hunters
Barter. After you barter, the new unit gets the permanent buffs of the unit you gave away.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

The Peddler swaps a weak body for a shop unit for free, which is a buy without gold. The Rag-and-Bone Fox adds a small buff to every trade. The Market Vixen moves your buffs onto the better unit, so a tall carry can change into a higher-tier body without starting over. This is one concrete version of the Hunters' idea of foxes taking a shop card once a turn.

Why run it

Cheap pivots: you upgrade units without gold, and a tall board isn't stuck with its first carry.

A game where it shines

Turn 7, your carry is a tier 2 Mole with +10/+10 from earlier turns, and the shop shows the tier 5 Badger you want. With Market Vixen you barter the Mole for the Badger: it lands in F2 with the Mole's +10/+10 and +1/+1 from Rag-and-Bone Fox. You spent no gold and still have 10 to upgrade.

Good against

Players who read your last board: barter boards change shape between fights.

Weak against

Thin shops: if the shop shows nothing you want, the fox is a plain body.

Variations
  • Trade-In
    "Barter: pay 1 gold; the new unit goes to your hand instead and is played as normal." Slower, but keeps "when played" effects.
  • Hand Barter
    "Once per turn, swap a card in your hand with a unit in your shop."
  • Even Trade
    "Barter only for a unit of the same tier or lower." A safer, cheaper version for low tiers.
Open questions
  • Swapping a Created token in makes the shop unit Created (sells for 0). Is a token-for-real-unit swap too strong for WIDE, or the point?
  • Does the Market Vixen's buff transfer need a limit (for example, only buffs from this game's effects, not golden base stats)?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: the shop unit enters the slot without being played and takes the old unit's origin, consistent with 4.3.2. Needs a ruling for the Market Vixen: the given-away unit goes to the shop 'with its buffs' and the new unit also 'gets' them, so buffs are copied, not moved, and buying the old unit back duplicates a carry's buffs every turn; say the old unit loses them. Base stats versus buffs is answerable with the stat layers (architecture-notes.md 4).

Budget. A free swap saves 2 gold every turn, so Barter needs a gold cost or a higher tier.

  • Fox Cub Peddler: flag over. body 3 + a barter saves 2 gold against sell-and-buy (≈ 0.4 uses a turn on turns 1–5: ≈ 0.8 gold a turn × ≈ 5.5 early recurring price ≈ 4.5; E44) [guess] ≈ 7.5 vs V(1) 5: +50%, ±25% Change: Barter costs 1 gold on this card (it still saves 1): ≈ 5.6 (+12%).
  • Rag-and-Bone Fox: flag over. Same as the old review: ≈ recurring +2 gold a turn + +1/+1 per barter (s = 2, over the Tier-3 cap) ≈ 23 vs V(3) 8: +188%, ±25% Change: 'Barter (1 gold)' and drop the +1/+1, on 1/2 (≈ 8.1, +1%); the +1/+1 payoff can live on a Tier-5 fox.
  • Market Vixen: flag over. body 10 + Barter late (≈ 0.8 uses × 2 gold ≈ 1.6 gold a turn ≈ 5.8) + the new unit keeps the old one's permanent buffs (≈ 4 SP a use: s ≈ 3.2; 2.6 × 3.2 ≈ 8.3; E08) [guess] ≈ 24 vs V(5) 12: +100%, ±25% Change: Drop its own Barter so it pays off other Barter units, on 3/6: 9 + 2.6 × 1.9 ≈ 14 (+17%). The text should say the unit given away loses those buffs, or buying it back duplicates them.

Bends. 4.2.1 (units cost 3) and 7.2.3 (units enter through the hand and trigger "when played"). The new unit takes the old one's origin (4.3).

No. 54

Aisle

NEW
ECOMEDIUM

Each unit improves one card in your shop, depending on which slot it fought from last time.

Rules textAisle: Your shop's unit cards line up with your board slots in reading order: the first unit card is F1's aisle, the second F2's, and so on, with the fifth B1's and the sixth B2's. The spell card has no aisle. A unit's aisle is the slot it stood in when your last fight began. Aisle text acts on the unit card in that aisle each time the shop fills (at the start of your turn and after each refresh). A unit that hasn't fought since you got it has no aisle. A discount never takes a card below 1 gold.
Rent-Paying VoleTier 1
Hedgerow folk
Aisle: when you buy that card, give this +1/+1 permanently.
1 Attack3 Health
Sett LandlordTier 2
Hunters
Aisle: that card costs 1 less.
2 Attack5 Health
Market HedgehogTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Aisle: that unit has +2/+2.
3 Attack4 Health
How they play together

The Landlord makes one shop card cheaper every refill, and it wants the front row because front aisles exist from tier 1. The Hedgehog buffs the card in its aisle, so placing it next to the Landlord gives you two good cards side by side. The Vole grows when you buy from its aisle, which pays you for refreshing that slot. Back-row aisles open only at tier 4 (5 unit cards) and tier 6 (6), so back-row Aisle units are a late plan.

Why run it

Cheaper and stronger buys every turn, and the deciding question each fight is where your shopkeepers stand.

A game where it shines

Last fight your Sett Landlord stood in F1 and your Market Hedgehog in F2. Turn 6, the shop fills: card 1 costs 2 gold, and card 2 is a +2/+2 unit. You refresh twice and get the same deal each time. Before you end the turn you think about moving the Landlord to the back row, where it would survive longer, but its discount would move to card 5, which you won't see until tier 4.

Good against

Slow boards that let you shop cheaply for several turns.

Weak against

Boards that kill your front row early: Aisle units in the front row are your shopkeepers and your wall at once.

Variations
  • Window Shopping
    "Aisle reads the slot this stands in now, not where it fought." Simpler, but free moves then cost nothing.
  • Corner Shop
    "Aisle: if that card shares a tribe with this, it costs 1 less." Rewards tribe focus.
  • Shop Front
    "Aisle: that card is frozen each time the shop fills." A one-card freeze that follows the unit's position.
Open questions
  • Needs a clear link in the UI from each slot to its shop card. Is that worth the work?
  • A 1-gold buy and a 1-gold sell make buy and sell triggers nearly free; fine while no Aisle effect refreshes the shop?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works with a fixed mapping of shop unit cards to slots, using 4.2.3's empty bought slots. Needs rulings: does the Market Hedgehog's '+2/+2' stay on the unit after it is bought (proposed yes, as 'gains'); and where Pawn and Cold Store cards sit in the aisle order. Not a loop: a bought slot only refills through a paid refresh, so cheap buy-and-sell cycles stay priced; hard to read without a drawn link from slot to card.

Budget. Aisle text acts on every shop fill, so with no turn timer it needs once per turn and smaller bodies.

  • Rent-Paying Vole: flag over. body 4 + +1/+1 permanently per aisle card bought (≈ 0.4 a turn early, more on refresh-heavy turns: s ≈ 0.8, at the Tier-1 cap; 3.9 × 0.8 ≈ 3.1) [guess] ≈ 7.1 vs V(1) 5: +42%, ±25% Change: 1/2 (≈ 6.1, +22%).
  • Sett Landlord: flag over. Same as the old review: ≈ recurring +0.7 gold a turn from discounts (≈ 4.5) + body 7 ≈ 11.5 vs V(2) 7: +64%, ±25% Change: Tier 3, 2/2, once per turn (≈ 7.6, −5%).
  • Market Hedgehog: flag over. Same as the old review: +2/+2 on the aisle card when bought (s ≈ 2: 3.1 × 2 ≈ 6.2) + body 7 ≈ 13.2 vs V(4) 10: +32%, ±25% Change: 2/3 and once per turn (≈ 11.2, +12%).

Bends. 6.1: the shop ignores position. Now combat position is also shop position, so free moves (8.3.3) gain a cost.

No. 55

Cold Store

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' Freeze
TALL

Keep one shop card in the snow for as many turns as you like; it grows every turn until you buy it.

Rules textCold Store: Once per turn, if your Cold Store is empty, you may put one unit card from your shop into it. You have one Cold Store. The stored card stays through refreshes and turns and takes up one of your shop's slots until you buy it. At the start of each of your turns it gains +1/+1. You buy it at its normal price; it arrives Bought and keeps its growth.
Red-Backed ShrikeTier 2

How they play together

The Shrike lets you hold one card from tier 2 on; the Wolverine doubles its growth. The Stoat turns a stored card into two copies, which is two thirds of a triple. Each stored card costs you a shop slot every refresh, so the price is a smaller shop while it ripens.

Why run it

You ripen a carry before you buy it, or hold the copy you need for a triple without spending a hand slot or gold.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, the shop shows a tier 3 unit you love but can't use yet. You put it in Cold Store with the Shrike. Turns 4 to 6 you shop from four cards instead of five. Turn 6 you buy it with +3/+3 on top (+6/+6 with the Wolverine), and the Stoat gives you a second copy.

Good against

Boards that peak in the mid game: your stored unit lands as a surprise.

Weak against

Low-tier tempo: a smaller shop at tier 1 and 2 hurts most.

Similar to

Battlegrounds' Freeze, kept on one card across refreshes and turns.

Variations
  • Root Cellar
    "Cold Store, but the stored card sits in an extra slot and gains only +1 Health a turn." Costs no shop slot.
  • Snowbank
    "Your stored card gains +1/+1 each time you refresh, instead of each turn." A concrete version of the Birds' 'each refresh improves something in the shop'.
  • Spoils
    "Cold Store. A card stored for 3 turns costs 0."
Open questions
  • Taking a shop slot is a third of the units at tier 1. Should Cold Store start at tier 2 only?
  • Is growth unbounded (linear) a problem if a card sits for ten turns?

Needs a ruling on what happens to the stored card when no Cold Store unit is left on the board (proposed: it stays until bought, but can't be replaced). Two Wolverines shouldn't stack their 'instead'; readable.

Budget. A card that grows in the shop is per-turn scaling paid out on purchase; the three units are +34% to +130% over their tiers (band ±25%).

No. 56

Fast (action, N)

was Fast (action)NEW
TALLECO

Go without one action for a few turns in a row, and this unit gives you a feast: a big turn of that same action.

Rules textFast (action, N): This keeps a count. At End of Turn, if you didn't do the named action this turn, the count goes up by 1; if you did, it goes back to 0. When the count reaches N, the Feast happens and the count goes back to 0. Only your own paid actions count: a paid refresh, buying from the shop, or selling. Free refreshes and effects don't. The count shows on the card. Break Fast: at any time in your turn you may end the fast early to get half the Feast, rounded down, and the count goes back to 0.
Still-Water HeronTier 2
Birds
Fast (refresh, 2). Feast: gain +4/+4 permanently.
3 Attack2 Health
Lenten HeronTier 4
Birds
Fast (refresh, 2). Feast: next turn, your first 8 refreshes are free.
3 Attack5 Health
Olm SleeperTier 5
Bog folk
Fast (buy, 3). Feast: next turn, units in your shop cost 2 gold.
2 Attack8 Health
How they play together

The waiting turns aren't empty: a refresh fast still lets you buy, sell, upgrade, place and play from the shop you have, and a buy fast still lets you sell, upgrade, and play cards from hand. The Feasts are action-rich: the Lenten Heron turns two turns without refreshing into a long free reroll turn, and the Olm turns three turns of saved gold into a cut-price shopping spree. It works with other waiting cards: Rehearse units grow in hand and Cold Store cards ripen while you fast.

Why run it

It turns patience into one planned spike turn, timed for when you need it.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, you buy Olm Sleeper. Turns 7 to 9 you buy nothing: you upgrade to tier 5, sell two weak units, and play the Rehearse toads you held, while your gold piles up to 22. End of turn 9 the count reaches 3: Feast. Turn 10 you start with 32 gold and every shop unit costs 2. You buy eleven units across your refreshes, finish two triples and rebuild the board in one turn. Turns 7 to 9 you fought with a thin board and lost some health; that was the price.

Good against

Boards that peak in the mid game, which your Feast turn outgrows.

Weak against

Tempo boards and Wager players who punish your waiting turns.

Similar to

Magic: The Gathering's Suspend (wait a set number of turns for a big payoff); Hearthstone's Quests turned around (don't do X for N turns instead of doing X N times).

Variations
  • Streak (the original Fast)
    "End of Turn: if you didn't refresh this turn, this gains +1/+1 permanently, and +1 more for each turn in a row." The isolated form: you still buy and sell, but it rewards the same skip every turn.
  • Vigil (N)
    "If this doesn't move for 3 turns, it gains +6/+6 permanently and Vigil starts again." The owner's "Don't move for 3 turns, then X" on a single unit; it also fits the Greenwood's Rooted.
  • Lent
    "When you play this, choose refresh, buy or sell. For the next 3 turns you can't do it. Then get a Feast for that action." The decision moves to the start: which action you give up.
Your direction

"Nah, Fast is boring. It rewards you for not playing the game ... Being rewarded for not making choices is not fun" (02h). "Fast isn't cut ... Fast might be a dead end, but we should still explore it" (02i). On the principle: "'Don't move for 3 turns, then X': that's cool. 'Don't move to get stats every single turn': not so cool" (02i; pillar 13).

Designer's response

Followed the direction. The main rule is now time-bound: a count to N, then a Feast, then it starts again. Break Fast adds a choice on each waiting turn: cash in early for half, or hold on. I also push back a little, with an example. What Fast teaches is that with no timer and uncapped gold, not doing an action is saving for it. So the Feast should be a big turn of that same action (8 free refreshes, units at 2 gold), not stats for doing nothing. The Olm scene shows the waiting turns still have decisions: what to sell, when to upgrade, what to play from hand. The original per-turn streak is kept only as a variation, in its most isolated form (refresh), since pillar 13 calls that one a delicate balance.

Open questions
  • Does a Feast of free refreshes need a cap beyond N turns of waiting, given the guardrail on unpaid refreshes (G1)?
  • Two Fast units with different actions can lock you out of most of the shop. Allowed as a player's choice, or one Fast count per player?
  • Should the count reset on selling at all, or only on the action named?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works: only paid actions count, so free refreshes in a Feast don't reset the count. G1: the Lenten Heron's 8 free refreshes can't chain (free refreshes give no new count), so it passes as bounded, but Pair and golden double it to 16, so it belongs on the retrigger review list (R11). Needs rulings: Break Fast at count 0 should give nothing, and two Fast units with different actions need the owner's call.

Budget. With the count resetting after each Feast, Fast is a timed reward, not a runaway; the Tier-2 stat Feast is still too big.

  • Still-Water Heron: flag over. Feast +4/+4 every 2 turns without a paid refresh, common early (≈ 0.55 of the time): s ≈ 2.2, over the Tier-2 cap of 1.3; 3.7 × 2.2 ≈ 8.1 − refreshes given up ≈ 0.7 + body 5 [guess] ≈ 12.4 vs V(2) 7: +77%, ±25%. The count now resets after each Feast, so the old quadratic growth is gone. Priced with Break Fast needing a count of 1 or more; at 0 it would be +2/+2 every turn Change: Feast +2/+2 on 2/2 (≈ 7.4, +6%); Break Fast then gives +1/+1.
  • Lenten Heron: in band. 8 free refreshes after 2 turns without refreshing; about 4 are useful on a normal turn (E52: 4 × 1.1 ≈ 4.4 a Feast, ≈ 0.67 gold-equivalent a turn ≈ 2.8) + body 8 [guess] ≈ 10.8 vs V(4) 10: +8%, ±25%. For information: 8 in one turn is a banked-turn case for the deferred turn-length check
  • Olm Sleeper: in band. body 10 + a Feast turn of 2-gold units every 4 turns (≈ 6 gold saved on a banked turn ≈ +1.5 gold a turn ≈ 6) − 3 turns without buying units (≈ −4) [guess] ≈ 12 vs V(5) 12: 0%, ±25%; very uncertain, and 'half the Feast' has no clear meaning for a price change

Bends. 3.1.2 and 4.4. With no timer, the usual best play is to refresh until gold runs out; Fast puts a price on doing the same thing every turn. With uncapped carry-over (4.1.2), not doing an action is saving for it.

From recruit 10.. Creatures: The heron stands still, then stabs; the olm can go years without food..

No. 57

Dig Down

NEW
WIDETALL

Once a turn, a mole lets you shop among lower-tier units only, to find the copies and cheap units you still need late in the game.

Rules textDig Down: Once per turn for each unit with Dig Down, you may dig down: choose a tier lower than yours. For the rest of this turn your shop shows only units of that tier, and it refreshes now for free. Later refreshes this turn cost the normal price and also show only that tier. Your real tier doesn't change (upgrade cost and hero damage use it), but you can't upgrade while dug down. The spell card and the shop's size stay as they are. At End of Turn your shop returns to normal. Golden: twice per turn.
Mole ProspectorTier 2
Hedgerow folk
After you buy a unit while dug down, give it +1/+1 permanently.
2 Attack3 Health
Mole ForemanTier 4
Hedgerow folk
Dig Down.
3 Attack5 Health
Deep-Seam MoleTier 5
Hedgerow folk
Dig Down. While you're dug down, units in your shop cost 2.
4 Attack6 Health
How they play together

At tier 5, your shop draws from tiers 1 to 5, so the one tier 1 unit you need turns up rarely. The Foreman lets you say "show me only tier 1" for the rest of the turn. The Prospector buffs everything you buy down there, so old units come back up stronger. The Deep-Seam Mole makes the old seam cheap, so late in the game you can fill your board with low-tier token makers at 2 gold each.

Why run it

Golden low-tier units are the cheapest triples in the game, and every triple gives a Triple Reward from the tier above yours. Digging down lets a late board chase those triples on purpose.

A game where it shines

Turn 9, you're at tier 5 with two Pygmy Shrew Berserkers (tier 1) on your board. In a normal tier 5 shop a tier 1 unit is a rare sight. You dig down to tier 1 with the Mole Foreman: your shop refreshes for free, and every unit in it is now tier 1. Two paid refreshes later the third Berserker shows up. You buy it, it triples into a golden Berserker, and its Triple Reward discovers a tier 6 unit. You couldn't upgrade this turn, but you weren't going to.

Good against

Late lobbies where everyone's high-tier copies are taken, and boards that need a golden to keep up.

Weak against

Turns where you need to upgrade: a dug-down turn is a turn at your current tier.

Similar to

Teamfight Tactics' "slow rolling": players stay at a low level so the shop keeps offering low-cost copies they need for three-stars. Also like Battlegrounds' "Discover a lower-tier unit" effects, but for a whole shop.

Variations
  • Dig Down (the original)
    "Once per turn, lower your shop tier by 1 until the end of the turn and refresh for free." Smaller: it only removes your top tier from the draw, which slightly raises the odds of everything else.
  • Old Seam
    "Dig Down to tier 1 only; units found there have +1/+1 for each tier you have above 1." Keeps tier 1 units viable late.
  • Climb
    "Once per turn, refresh your shop for free with units of the tier above yours; you may buy at most one of them." A peek up instead of down.
Your direction

"I have no idea what Dig Down even means. I don't understand this" (02h). Direction: example cards and a play scene.

Designer's response

Followed the direction: three units and a turn-9 scene that show what it's for, finishing low-tier triples late and fetching early token makers. I also changed the rule, because I think the original was hard to picture for a real reason: your shop already includes every lower tier, so lowering your tier by 1 only removes your top tier and slightly shifts the odds. The new version names the tier and shows only that tier, which is a clear, visible effect. The original stays as a variation.

Open questions
  • Do several moles dig further, or does each just grant another dig?
  • Is a shop of only tier 1 units at tier 6 too reliable for triples? A cap of one dig per turn per player may be enough.
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Passes G1: one free refresh per dig, once per turn per unit (twice on a golden), and it can't chain. It needs the shop query to take a draw tier apart from the player's tier (shopOffer in architecture-notes.md), with 6.1.4 empty slots when that tier runs out. Pair's 'triggers twice' shouldn't double an activated ability; the play scene makes it readable.

Budget. Dig Down itself is fair; a discount or growth on every dug-down buy adds up fast without a turn timer.

  • Mole Prospector: flag over. body 5 + +1/+1 permanently per unit bought while dug down (≈ 1.5 buys × 0.6 a mole on board: s ≈ 1.8 once Tier-4 moles arrive; priced at turn 6, 3.1 × 1.8 ≈ 5.6) [guess] ≈ 10.6 vs V(2) 7: +51%, ±25%; ≈ 6.5 (−7%) before a mole is on board Change: Once per turn, on 2/2 (s ≈ 1.2): ≈ 7.7 (+10%).

  • Deep-Seam Mole: flag over. body 10 + Dig Down ≈ 2.3 + units cost 2 while dug down (≈ 2 buys × 0.6 of turns ≈ 1.2 gold a turn × 4.1 ≈ 4.9; E53) [guess] ≈ 17.2 vs V(5) 12: +43%, ±25% Change: 2/4 (≈ 13.2, +10%).

Bends. 4.6.1 (your tier only rises) and 6.1.2 (the shop draws from your tier or lower). Late boards can fish for low-tier copies and token makers.
