Group N

Waiting off the board: hand and sleep

No. 58

Hibernate (N)

VARIANT of Galaxy's Mysterious Egg and Battlegrounds' Lockbox
TALL

This unit sleeps in its slot through your next few upgrades, growing each time, then wakes up big.

Rules textHibernate (N): This sleeps through your next N upgrades. While asleep it stays in its slot, but in combat it can't attack, be attacked or be chosen by effects; it isn't anyone's neighbour; and it doesn't count as a unit for who attacks first (9.2.1), for when the fight ends (9.9.1) or for hero damage (10.1.1). Its slot still counts as full, so summons can't land there. In the Recruit Phase it can be buffed and moved as normal. Each time you upgrade, it gains +3/+3 permanently. After the Nth upgrade it wakes. A golden keeps the same N and gains +6/+6.
Clockwork DormouseTier 2
Hedgerow folk
Hibernate (2).
1 Attack1 Health
Fat DormouseTier 3
Hedgerow folk
Hibernate (1). While this is asleep, your upgrades cost 1 less.
3 Attack3 Health

Hedgerow folk
After a friendly unit wakes from Hibernate, give its adjacent units +2/+2 permanently.
2 Attack5 Health
How they play together

The Clockwork Dormouse is a 1/1 that becomes a 7/7 after two upgrades. The Fat Dormouse pays for itself by making the upgrades that wake it cheaper. The Clockmaker turns each waking into a buff for the units beside it, so you plan where the sleeper lies.

Why run it

Cheap, sure growth you can see coming, tied to something you were going to do anyway: upgrading.

A game where it shines

Turn 2, you buy Clockwork Dormouse and put it in B3. For a few fights your board fights a body short. Turn 3 you upgrade to tier 3: it is 4/4. Turn 5 you upgrade to tier 4: it is 7/7 and wakes. You had put the Dormouse Clockmaker next to it, in B2, so the waking gives the Clockmaker +2/+2 as well.

Good against

Opponents who can't punish a body-short board in the early game.

Weak against

Tempo boards on turns 2 to 5, and slow upgraders: no upgrade, no growth.

Similar to

Galaxy's Mysterious Egg and Battlegrounds' Lockbox: delayed payoffs that take a slot.

Variations
  • Hibernate (turns)
    "This sleeps for your next 3 turns, gaining +2/+2 at the start of each." Counts turns, not upgrades.
  • Torpor
    "This sleeps until you lose a fight. Then it wakes with +2/+2 for each turn it slept." A comeback card.
  • Light Sleeper
    "Hibernate (2). If your front row is empty in combat, this wakes for that fight."
Open questions
  • A Hibernate (3) bought at tier 5 can never wake (only one upgrade left). Should it wake at tier 6 regardless?
  • Do upgrades past tier 6 (to tier 7 by effect) count?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Its occupant rules are stated, but they leave out back-row protection (9.5.1): a sleeper in the front row 'can't be attacked', so either it counts as a front-row unit and a lone sleeper in F1 makes your whole back row unattackable, or 9.5.3 turns it back into a candidate. Proposed: a sleeper doesn't count for 9.5.1, as with Play Dead (R7). Upgrades by effect to tier 7 should count; a Hibernate (3) bought at tier 5 never waking is a design choice.

Budget. Sleeping units pay for their missing fights about right; a gold discount on the same upgrade tips the Tier-3 one over.

  • Clockwork Dormouse: edge. asleep ≈ 4 fights, then 7/7 from about turn 6; time-averaged over turns 2–12 ≈ 8.9 vs V(2) 7: +27% (old review: +20%), ±25% (E02 on proposed R34, E96) [guess] Change: None yet; check in the sim. Bought with fewer than 2 upgrades left it never wakes, as intended.
  • Fat Dormouse: flag over. asleep ≈ 2 fights, then 6/6 from about turn 6: time-averaged ≈ 9 (+12%) + 'upgrades cost 1 less' on the upgrade it sleeps through (one-off gold at Tier 3 ≈ 4.5) [guess] ≈ 13.5 vs V(3) 8: +69%, ±25% Change: Tier 4, same text: one-off gold is cheaper there (2.2) and it wakes about turn 8: ≈ 10.2 (+2%).
  • Dormouse Clockmaker: in band. body 7 + +2/+2 permanently to ≈ 1.43 neighbours per wake (≈ 5.1 a wake × ≈ 1.5 wakes after turn 6 × 0.6 Hibernate build ≈ 4.6, one-off) [guess] ≈ 11.6 vs V(4) 10: +16%, ±25%

Bends. 9.1.2 (each unit fights from its slot) and 9.2.1 (sleepers aren't counted for who attacks first). Upgrading becomes a trigger.

From recruit 11 (marked variant by the designer). Creatures-first used the same name for in-hand growth, merged into Rehearse (59).. Creatures: The dormouse sleeps over half the year; the Folio's Dormouse Clockmakers' guild hibernates by law..

Rehearse

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' hand-buff cards (merged with creatures' Hibernate)

Rules textRehearse: At End of Turn, if this is in your hand, it gains +1/+1 permanently, or +2/+2 if another card with Rehearse is in your hand too. Once played, it stops growing. End of Turn effects resolve on the board in reading order first, then in your hand from left to right.

How they play together

Two Rehearse cards in hand each grow +2/+2 a turn, so they want company. The Understudy Toad's opening night buffs your whole chorus, scaled by how long it waited. The Old Sleeper copies its growth onto the units beside it, so where you play it matters.

Why run it

Growth with a visible cost: hand slots and units not on the board. It fits waiting plans like Fast and gives a thin board a planned big turn.

A game where it shines

Turn 4, you buy two Understudy Toads and keep them in hand. At End of Turn on turns 4, 5 and 6 each gains +2/+2. Turn 7, opening night: you play the first (8/8) and it gives your other Bog folk +3/+3; you play the second, and it gives the first toad and the rest +3/+3 again.

Good against

Slow lobbies, where a few thin fights cost little.

Weak against

Tempo boards on the turns you hold cards back.

Similar to

Battlegrounds' Bream Counter and Futurefin, which grow cards in hand; Rehearse grows on its own each turn.

Variations
  • Understudy (capped)
    "Rehearse, up to +5/+5." A version with a ceiling.
  • Curtain Call
    "Rehearse. When you play this after 3 or more turns in hand, get a Created copy of it."
  • Dress Rehearsal
    "While this is in your hand, at End of Turn it gains +1/+1 for each unit you played this turn." Grows from your actions, not from waiting.
Open questions
  • Name clash: the old Hazel Dormouse was also Bide's Slumber example, so it is renamed Den Dormouse here.
  • Unbounded but linear growth: does it need a cap, given the 10-card hand?
Review notesRules: cleanBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as an End of Turn trigger from hand, with board-then-hand order stated. The rulebook has no hand order yet, and Offstage needs it too: add one ('cards in hand keep the order they arrived in, left to right'). Readable.

Budget. Growth in hand is cheaper than growth on the board, but payoffs that multiply it by turns rehearsed run over.

  • Den Dormouse: in band. body 2 + +1/+1 a turn in hand, +2/+2 with another Rehearse card (≈ 0.3): s ≈ 2.6 × 0.6 for growth that isn't fighting: 3.7 × 2.6 × 0.6 ≈ 5.8 (E17, E02) [guess] ≈ 7.8 vs V(2) 7: +11%, ±25%. The old review: in band if held up to about 4 turns
  • Understudy Toad: flag over. Same as the old review: in-hand growth ≈ 4.2 + +1/+1 to other Bog folk per turn rehearsed, up to 4 (≈ 8.6) + body 4 ≈ 16.8 vs V(3) 8: +110%, ±25% Change: 1/1, payoff '+1 Attack to your other Bog folk for each turn it rehearsed (up to 3)': ≈ 9.4 (+18%).
  • Old Sleeper: edge. Same as the old review: ≈ 15.6 vs V(5) 12: +30%, ±25%; more when the pair doubling applies Change: If the sim agrees, 3/3 (≈ 13.6, +13%).

Bends. 3.4.2 (cards in hand do nothing) and 7.1: the hand becomes a growth space that competes with board slots.

From recruit 13 (marked variant by the designer), creatures 12.. Creatures: The Folio's Frog Chorus and fox travelling theatre, rehearsing offstage; dormice sleeping through half the year, with the hand as a den..

No. 60

Fly South

NEW
ECOWIDE

Send this bird away for two turns; it comes back stronger and brings you a free unit.

Rules textFly South: At End of Turn you may send this away. It leaves the board, and its slot is empty. While away it isn't on your board or in your hand: it doesn't fight, doesn't count for triples or hero damage, and its pool copy stays out of the pool. At the start of your turn after next, after your gold is set, it returns to its slot, or the nearest empty one (8.4.2), with +2/+2 permanently and a bundle: a Created random unit of your tier added to your hand. If your board is full, it returns to your hand instead. Golden: two bundles.
SwallowTier 1
Birds
Fly South.
2 Attack1 Health
Stork CourierTier 3
Birds
Fly South.
3 Attack3 Health
Stork PostmasterTier 5
Birds
When a unit returns from flying south, give it and its bundle +2/+2 permanently.
4 Attack5 Health
How they play together

Sending a bird away frees its slot, so you can try a new unit there for two turns; when the bird comes back it takes the nearest empty slot. Each return brings a Created unit of your tier, which sells for 0 but fills the board. The Postmaster makes each return a bigger delivery.

Why run it

A free unit of your tier every two turns per bird, paid for with two fights a body short.

A game where it shines

Turn 7, you're healthy and the next two fights matter less. At End of Turn you send two Stork Couriers away and fight with five units. You use the free slots to test a new unit. Turn 9 the storks return with +2/+2 each and two Created tier 4 units in your hand; with the Postmaster, all four have +2/+2 more.

Good against

Lobbies where you can afford to lose a fight or two to build up.

Weak against

Tempo boards, and the turns you are low on health.

Similar to

Magic: The Gathering's phasing and 'exile, then return' effects; Battlegrounds' Free Travel Winner, which leaves for a reward but doesn't return.

Variations
  • Long Haul
    "Fly South: choose how many turns it's away (1 to 3). It returns with +2/+2 and one bundle for each turn."
  • Escort
    "End of Turn: you may send away the unit in front of this with it. Both return together."
  • Winter Visitor
    "Start of your turn: if you have an empty slot, a Created copy of a random Bird from the pool flies in for this turn and the next fight."
Open questions
  • Renamed from Migrate because rows' Migrate (42) is a different mechanic.
  • Does an away unit return to the pool if you are eliminated (5.3.8)? Proposed: yes.
  • A bundle into a full hand is lost (7.1.2). Should it wait instead?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as an 'away' zone (R7) with the return after gold is set. Needs rulings: is the return a summon (Delivery, Mark, Bury read summons), is the bundle drawn from the pool (4.3.2 then makes it Created and a pool copy), and what happens when it returns to a full hand (7.1.2 loses it). Pool copies of an away unit should return on elimination (5.3.8), as proposed.

Group O

Copies, triples, spells and the pool

No. 61

Mimic

VARIANT of Galaxy's Mirror Mirror and Battlegrounds' Karmic Chameleon
TALL

Each turn this becomes a copy of the enemy unit that faced it in your last fight.

Rules textMimic: At the start of your turn, this becomes a copy of the enemy unit that was across from it (8.2.6) when your last fight began, before Start of Combat effects. It takes that unit's base stats and text, not golden, and keeps Mimic, its own permanent buffs and its own origin (4.3.6). For triples it counts as a copy of the unit it copied. If no unit was across from it, it stays as it is.
Starling EchoTier 2
Birds
Mimic. After this copies a unit, it loses Mimic.
1 Attack2 Health
Jay SentinelTier 3
Birds
After one of your units copies a unit with Mimic, give it +1/+1 permanently.
3 Attack3 Health
Mocking JayTier 4
Birds
Mimic. Has +2/+2.
2 Attack2 Health
How they play together

The Starling copies once and keeps it, so a cheap card can land a higher-tier unit early. The Mocking Jay keeps copying every turn, and the Sentinel makes each copy grow. You aim it by where you place it before the fight, guessing where the enemy's best unit will stand. Since it counts as the copied unit, it can be the third copy of a unit you saw your opponent run.

Why run it

It borrows your opponent's best units, and it can finish a triple from a unit you don't have.

A game where it shines

Turn 6, your opponent's best unit is a tier 5 Badger in F2, as it was last fight. You place Mocking Jay in F2. Turn 7 the jay is a Badger with +2/+2 and its buffs from the Sentinel. You already hold two Badgers, so it triples at once.

Good against

Boards with one big carry in a predictable slot.

Weak against

Wide token boards and gaps: it copies a 1/1, or nothing.

Similar to

Galaxy's Mirror Mirror; Battlegrounds' Karmic Chameleon; Magic: The Gathering's Clone.

Variations
  • Echo
    "Mimic, but only for the next fight; then it turns back."
  • Borrowed Song
    "At the start of your turn, copy the text (not the stats) of the enemy unit across from this in your last fight." Closer to Mirror Mirror.
  • Changeling's Mimic
    "At the start of your turn, become a copy of the friendly unit behind this." Friendly and aimable, as in the Fairies' Changeling idea.
Open questions
  • Origin: the entry said the copy is Created, but 4.3.6 keeps origin through a transform. Proposed here: it keeps its own origin.
  • Async: the copied unit may come from a tribe outside your lobby (5.1.3, 2.1.5). Allowed?
  • Name clash: Galaxy has a card called Mimic.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag under

Rules. Works as a start-of-turn transform that keeps its own origin (4.3.6). Needs a pool ruling: a Bought Starling that became a copy of a tier 5 unit would return a copy of that unit to the pool when sold, a copy the pool never lost (5.3.4), so say it returns its original card's copy. The copied unit may come from a tribe outside your lobby (5.1.3) in async play; the name clashes with Galaxy's Mimic.

Budget. Mimic is worth whatever stands across, so it is weak early and strong late at every tier; price it by when it is bought.

  • Starling Echo: flag under. body 3, then one copy at turn 3 of a ≈ 6-SP enemy unit if the slot was filled (≈ 0.55): time-averaged over turns 2–6 ≈ 4.3 + triple help ≈ 0.2 (E31) [guess] ≈ 4.5 vs V(2) 7: −36%, ±25%. Bought late, it copies a much bigger unit Change: Tier 1, same text (≈ 4.5 vs V(1) 5, −10%); sim late buys, when it copies a Tier-4–5 unit.
  • Jay Sentinel: edge. body 6 + +1/+1 permanently per copy (≈ 1 a turn × 0.6 a Mimic on board: s ≈ 1.2; 3.5 × 1.2 ≈ 4.2) [guess] ≈ 10.2 vs V(3) 8: +28%, ±25% Change: If the sim agrees, 2/3 (≈ 9.2, +15%).

No. 63

Pair

VARIANT of Battlegrounds' First Mate Pip and the False Idols anomaly
TALLSTICKY

Two copies of a swan bond into one stronger unit whose effects fire twice, and a third copy still makes it golden.

Rules textPair: When you have two non-golden copies of the same Pair unit across board and hand, they bond at once into one Bonded unit with both copies' stats and buffs added together. It takes the board copy's slot, or goes to hand if neither was on the board. A Bonded unit's effects trigger twice. It isn't golden and gives no Triple Reward. It counts as two copies for a triple: a third copy makes it golden as normal, with the higher of the Bonded stats and the golden stats, triggering once with doubled numbers, and gives the Triple Reward. If three copies arrive together, they triple.
Bewick's SwanTier 1

How they play together

Bewick's Swan is a plain early 4/4 from two copies. The Paladin and the Maiden sit in the back row and work on the units in front of them; Bonded, they work twice. A Bonded Maiden is a fast scaler for the two front units it backs.

Why run it

A halfway step to golden: a big unit with doubled effects from only two copies.

Open questions
  • "Triggers twice" is a retrigger: any free-refresh or gold effect on a Pair unit doubles. Should Pair be kept off those effects?

Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a two-copy merge that counts as two for a triple. It is a retrigger source, so it belongs on G1's review list (R11).

Budget. Pair prices fine; the Maiden is the budget's own failing example, and its passing twin is the fix.

Fewer, stronger spell turns, and the Apothecary as a payoff.

How they play together

Each Endangered unit counts its own copies, and the Grouse Lek adds your other Endangered units' copies. Cold Store and Pawn keep copies out of the pool, so they raise the count too.

A real dilemma for opponents: buying a copy to deny you feeds yours, and leaving them lets you triple.

A game where it shines

Turn 9, two other players hold Capercaillies, and one is in your shop. You buy it: your Capercaillie counts 4 copies out and is 8/8 in the fight. Next turn you put a third in Cold Store, and it counts 5.

Good against

Lobbies that hate-buy or share your tribe.

Quiet lobbies where nobody else touches the unit.

Group +

New mechanics

No. 66

Ambush

NEWADDED THIS ROUND
MEDIUMSCAM

When an enemy back-row unit attacks this, this strikes first.

Rules textAmbush. Whenever an enemy back-row unit attacks this, this deals damage equal to its Attack to the attacker before the damage exchange. If the attacker dies, the attack ends and this takes no damage. Against front-row attackers it fights normally.
Reedbed LurkerTier 2
Bog folk
Ambush.
3 Attack3 Health
Fen NewtTier 3
Bog folk
Ambush.
2 Attack5 Health
Bog DrummerTier 5
Bog folk
Whenever an enemy back-row unit attacks, your Bog folk gain +1 Attack this combat.
3 Attack6 Health
How they play together

Back-row units attack in every cycle once the front row has had its turn (9.4.2), and Mire makes them attack more often by skipping the front. Ambush units in your front row hit those back-row attackers first, and many back-row carries and supports have low Health. The Bog Drummer grows your board each time the enemy's back row has to come forward.

Why run it

It punishes the enemy's back row for attacking, which is where their engines and carries live.

A game where it shines

Turn 5, you have Mudwallow Toad and Fen Newt in the front row. In the fight, the toad mires the enemy's front-row bear. The bear's turn passes to its B1 owl, 5/3, which attacks your Fen Newt. The newt strikes first for 2, the owl survives at 1, and in the exchange both die. Later Bog Drummer turns every such attack into Attack for your board.

Good against

Boards that attack with their back row, such as Grudge rooks, Swoop birds and back-row carries.

Weak against

Boards whose back row never attacks (0-Attack supports) and WIDE front rows.

Similar to

Like first strike in Magic, but only against one kind of attacker.

Variations
  • Ambush (any)
    The first enemy that attacks this each combat is hit first, whatever its row.
  • Snap
    Whenever an enemy back-row unit attacks this, deal double damage back.
  • Reed Cover
    Enemy back-row units can't attack this while another friendly front-row unit lives.
Your direction

Inspired by the owner on Mire (02h): 'you could have effects for when the enemy's backline attacks, so you have some counterattack damage or something; you could build into that.' The owner said no change was asked (02i); this is offered as a new idea, not a change to Mire.

Designer's response

Pitched as its own mechanic so it can pair with Mire without changing it.

Open questions
  • Does 'first' happen at 9.6.1 step 1? Then the rule 'if the defender is gone, the attack ends' needs an attacker-gone version too.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: in band

Rules. Works as a pre-strike in 9.6.1 step 1, but the rulebook only says what happens when the defender is gone after step 1 or 2; it needs the matching 'attacker gone' line, shared with Both Ends and Territory. Needs an order against Quick and Hunker: proposed Ambush resolves first (it is a 'when attacked' effect), Ambush damage is effect-like and not halved by the attacker's Antlers unless the window says so. Readable; 'enemy back-row unit' should mean the attacker's slot at the moment it attacks.

Budget. Ambush only fires against back-row attackers, so it is cheap and all three units are in band.

  • Reedbed Lurker: in band. T2 3/3: body 6 + about 0.6 enemy back-row attacks on it x first strike (Divine Shield's 2.25 x about 0.5 chance 3 damage kills a Tier-2 back unit) = 0.7 (E90) [guess] = 6.7 vs V(2) 7: -4% (+-15%).
  • Fen Newt: in band. T3 2/5: body 7 + about 1.0 (as under Mire) [guess] = 8.0 vs V(3) 8: 0% (+-15%).
  • Bog Drummer: in band. T5 3/6: body 9 + about 2 enemy back-row attacks while it lives x +1 Attack to about 3 Bog folk x 0.6 realised = 3.6 [guess] = 12.6 vs V(5) 12: +5% (+-15%).

From new, inspired by Mire (combat 10) and the owner's 02h note. Creatures: Newts and frogs that wait in the reeds. Bog folk..

How they play together

Scar wants to survive hits, so it pairs with this batch's damage reducers: a boar given Shell, standing next to a Shoulder bear, or behind a whistle survives more hits and grows from each. Enemy Peck and WIDE boards, normally good against walls, feed it with every small hit.

A game where it shines

Designer's response

A payoff for the batch's defensive keywords, so STICKY has something to scale with besides surviving.

Rules. Scar itself works: 'survives a hit' is checked after that hit's death check, a hit reduced to 0 doesn't count (R1), and the name clashes with Bloodied's Sounder Matriarch (batch 2), a different card.

How they play together

Designer's response

Ripen is a time-bound payoff in that spirit: the unit keeps fighting while it ripens, so waiting isn't doing nothing.

Why run it

An eighth piece in the fight that can't be killed, at the cost of a hand slot and a body off the board.

A game where it shines

Turn 2 you hold Prompter Mouse instead of playing it: your F1 hits for +2 each fight. Turn 6 your board is full, so it stays in hand all game.

Good against

Boards that kill the support units in your back row.

Weak against

Boards that need every body: an Offstage card isn't one.

Variations
  • Understudy
    "Offstage. When a friendly unit dies, this is summoned in its place."
Open questions
  • Is "leftmost Offstage card only" readable enough, or should Offstage need an empty board slot instead?
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag under

Rules. Works with 9.3.1's 'units that trigger from hand' phase. Needs rulings: the snapshot others fight must include your leftmost Offstage card (9.1.1 copies only the board), and the hand needs a fixed order (see Rehearse). The Understudy variation summons from hand in combat, so say the card stays in hand after combat; readable.

Budget. An Offstage card gives only its effect, so the effect must carry most of a unit's budget; both are written at aura size and come out under. Slot-free value is a guess until simulated.

  • Prompter Mouse: flag under. never takes a board slot: Start of Combat +2 Attack to F1 (≈ 2.2) + a slot saved ≈ 1 (E23, proposed E96) [guess] ≈ 3.2 vs V(1) 5: −36%, ±15% Change: '+2/+2' instead of '+2 Attack' (≈ 5.2, +4%).
  • Wings Toad: flag under. +1 Attack this combat to other Bog folk per Bog folk death (≈ 3 buffs a fight × 0.6 realised ≈ 1.8) + a slot saved ≈ 1 [guess] ≈ 2.8 vs V(3) 8: −65%, ±15%; played as a 2/3 it is 5 (−38%) Change: '+2/+2 this combat' instead of '+1 Attack' (≈ 8.2, +3%).
  • Stage Manager Fox: edge. body 8 + +2/+2 to the Offstage card in hand, which counts only if that card is later played (≈ 0.6 × 0.3 × 3.6 ≈ 0.6) [guess] ≈ 8.6 vs V(4) 10: −14%, ±15% Change: 4/5 (≈ 9.6, −4%).

Bends. 3.4.2 (units in hand don't fight and don't count). The hand gains a slot that acts in combat.

From Inspired by Rehearse (59) and Hibernate (58).. Creatures: The Folio's fox travelling theatre and Frog Chorus: the prompter offstage..

No. 75

Nightshade

was Venom (working name)NEWYOUR IDEA
WIDESCAM

An enemy with Nightshade loses half its Health every time it is attacked, so many small hits can bring down one huge unit.

Rules textNightshade is a debuff on an enemy unit for this combat. Whenever a unit with Nightshade is attacked, it first loses half its current Health, rounded down; then the attack's damage is dealt as normal. This is Health loss, not damage: it can't be prevented, and it doesn't count as the unit taking damage. Nightshade on its own never kills: a unit with 1 Health loses nothing. A unit has Nightshade or it doesn't; gaining it again does nothing. It lasts until the end of combat. Units give it through their own text, for example 'Start of Combat: give the enemy with the highest Health Nightshade'.
Belladonna SpriteTier 6
Fairies
Start of Combat: give the enemy with the highest Health Nightshade (a tie is broken at random). Golden: the two enemies with the highest Health.
5 Attack8 Health
Bitter ToadTier 5
Bog folk
Whenever this deals damage to an enemy, that enemy gains Nightshade. Golden: also when this is dealt damage by an enemy, that enemy gains Nightshade.
4 Attack9 Health
Hornet ScoutTier 2
Hive
This attacks an enemy with Nightshade whenever there is one it can attack. When this dies, summon a 1/1 Hornetling with the same text minus the summon.
3 Attack2 Health
How they play together

Belladonna Sprite marks the enemy's biggest unit before any attack lands, from the safety of the back row. Bitter Toad marks whatever it trades with, including a big carry that attacks it, so a second huge unit or a unit summoned mid-fight gets marked too. Hornet Scouts and their Hornetlings send every attack they have into the marked unit instead of a random target, and each attack halves it first: a 70-Health unit hit only by 1/1 tokens goes 70, 34, 16, 7, 3, 1, dead in six attacks. Any wide maker of small attackers (tokens, death summons, Swarm) feeds the same plan, and Peck X would multiply it if a pecked attack counts as several attacks.

Why run it

A wide board's usual late-game problem is one carry with more Health than the whole board has Attack: it eats an attacker every turn and never falls. Nightshade turns the number of attacks into the stat that matters, which is exactly what a wide board has. It is the wide player's answer to a tall lobby leader without leaving the wide plan.

A game where it shines

Turn 3, you buy two Hornet Scouts and go wide with Hive units. Turn 7, you reach Tier 5; the lobby leader has a 40/70 boar carry, so you buy Bitter Toad. Turn 9, you reach Tier 6, find Belladonna Sprite and put it in B1, where the boar can't reach it while your front row lives. Your board: Scouts 3/2 in F1 and F2, 6/6 Hive units in F3 and F4, Sprite in B1, Toad in B2, another 6/6 in B3. The enemy: the 40/70 boar in F2 and two 5/5 feeders in the back row. You have more units, so you attack first. Start of Combat: the Sprite gives the boar Nightshade. Attack 1: the F1 Scout hits it; the boar halves to 35, takes 3, sits at 32; the Scout dies and its Hornetling takes F1. The boar kills your F3 unit and takes 6 (26; attacking doesn't halve it). The Hornetling attacks next: 26 halves to 13, minus 1 is 12. The boar kills the F2 Scout (9), whose Hornetling takes F2 and hits: 9 loses 4 to 5, minus 1 is 4. The boar attacks your last front unit, the F4 6/6, and dies on its counter-damage. Your Sprite, Toad and a 6/6 mop up the two feeders. Without Nightshade the same board deals about 35 damage in total, the boar finishes at about 35 Health, and you lose the fight.

Good against

One or two huge units (TALL carries, big STICKY bodies) standing in the front row.

Weak against

MEDIUM boards of many mid-sized units, where halving 8 Health takes only 4, and second lives: a unit that returns after dying comes back clean, without Nightshade.

Similar to

Pokemon's Super Fang, which halves the target's current HP, except that here it fires on every attack the unit takes. Not Battlegrounds' Venomous, which destroys the first unit it damages.

Variations
  • Nettled (low tier, weaker)
    The first time a unit with Nettled is attacked this combat, it loses half its current Health, rounded down, then Nettled is removed. Example: Nettle Hob, Tier 2, 2/3, Neutral: 'Start of Combat: give the enemy with the highest Health Nettled.' One halving at the start of the fight, instead of one on every attack.
  • Creeping Nightshade
    Nightshade as written, plus: when a unit with Nightshade dies, the enemy units adjacent to it gain Nightshade. Aimed at boards with two or three big units side by side.
  • Three Drops
    Nightshade wears off after the unit has been attacked three times this combat. A 70-Health unit can lose at most 35, 17 and 9 to it; a limit if the full version proves too strong.
Your direction

A debuff "that goes on creatures. Whenever they're attacked, they lose half their HP, their current HP, rounded down. So it helps wide boards deal with really, really big boards. It would have to be a powerful effect that goes on high-tier stuff."

Designer's response

Name: I propose Nightshade. Deadly nightshade grows at woodland edges, is the classic poison of hedge witches and fairy tales, and fits the owner's wish for fairy 'curses that debuff enemy units' (02h). It also reads as a state ('the boar has Nightshade') and avoids confusion with Battlegrounds' Venomous. Alternatives: Adderbite (the adder, Britain's one venomous snake), Hemlock, Wither. Decisions, each with its reason. When it is applied: by a unit's own text at Start of Combat or when that unit deals damage, not by a Recruit Phase spell, because fights are against snapshots and you can't touch an enemy board before combat; Start of Combat is the main route, because the wide board needs the mark before its first attack. How long: this combat only, as debuffs on a snapshot can't carry over. Before or after the attack's damage: before, so each hit's damage counts in full on top of the halving, and the order is easy to read ('halve, then hit'); after would make it noticeably weaker. Stacking: no, because the halving already compounds with every attack; stacking would quarter per hit and be hard to read. A 1-Health unit loses nothing, since half of 1 rounded down is 0, so Nightshade never kills by itself; the hit does. That keeps it a multiplier for many attacks, the wide plan, rather than plain removal. It is Health loss, not damage, so shells and hides don't block it (they are what big units carry) and damage triggers such as thorns don't fire twice per attack. Shell still limits the hit's damage after the halving. Following the direction, the appliers are Tier 5 and 6; Hornet Scout at Tier 2 only points attacks at a marked unit and does nothing on its own. The weaker low-tier range is shown by the Nettled variation: one halving per combat on a Tier 2 unit. The numbers show it is powerful: six 1/1 attacks kill a 70-Health unit, so it may become an auto-include against any tall leader. Its limits are random targeting when the enemy has several front units, MEDIUM boards, and returns, which shed it.

Open questions
  • The rulebook has no rule for Health loss that isn't damage. Proposal: it lowers current Health as damage would and can be healed like damage, but it is not a damage event, can't be prevented, and the kill is credited to the attack that finished the unit.
  • Does Peck X (an attack that counts as X attacks) halve a Nightshaded unit X times? It is the natural payoff, but Peck 3 would take a unit to an eighth in one attack.
  • Does a Swarm joint attack count as one attack on the defender or one per attacker?
  • Extra attacks ('attacks immediately', repeat attacks) count as attacks for Nightshade as written. Confirm that is wanted.
  • Should a golden Bitter Toad's defensive trigger mark the attacker, or should every Bitter Toad do that? It decides whether the Toad wants the front row.
Review notesRules: needs a rulingBudget: flag over

Rules. Works as a state on the defender once 9.6.3 gains one line for Health loss: it is recorded with damage taken (current Health = maximum − damage − loss), so healing restores it and a lost Health aura still counts it, but it is not a damage event, so Shell doesn't break, Knot doesn't share it, Bloodied, Bud, Grudge and Bitter Toad don't fire, and because it never takes the last point there is no kill to credit. Needs two rulings: halve as the first part of the damage exchange (9.6.1 step 3) rather than in step 1, so a dodged (Jink), redirected (Shoulder) or cancelled attack halves only the unit actually hit; and halve once per attack turn, so Peck (X) halves once (as Peck's review already proposes for the target's 'when attacked' effects), a Swarm joint attack halves once, and repeat or immediate attacks (9.4.11, 9.4.12) halve again. Under that ruling Swarm works against Nightshade, since bees attacking one by one halve more often than one swarm, so 'how they play' should drop Swarm unless the owner picks per-joiner halving; otherwise there is no loop or stall, a returning unit comes back clean (9.8.2), and the units read well, though Hornet Scout should print 'summon a 1/1 Hornetling that also attacks enemies with Nightshade', and Bitter Toad's mark lands after the current attack's halving, so it bites from the next attack. Answers to the designer's questions: Health loss that isn't damage: take the designer's proposal and write it into 9.6.3 as above. The rulebook has no healing rule yet, so 'can be healed like damage' should become 'healing restores lost Health and damage alike, up to maximum' when healing is written. Halving can't take a unit from 1 to 0, so no death check is needed after it and the kill always belongs to the hit. Peck (X): halve once per Peck attack. Peck's 'each strike counts as an attack' is for its own 'whenever this attacks' effects; the rules review of Peck already proposes that the target's 'when attacked' effects fire once. X halvings would make Peck 3 plus one applier take seven eighths of a unit in one turn, which is removal rather than the wide plan. If the owner wants the combo, it should be a line on a specific card ('your Peck strikes each count for Nightshade'), not the default. Swarm: one joint attack is one attack on the defender, so it halves once, before all the joiners' hits land together. That is consistent with Peck and with 9.6.1 running once per attack, but it means Swarm and Nightshade pull against each other; the entry should say so instead of listing Swarm as feeding the plan. Extra attacks: yes as written. Each repeat attack (9.4.11) and each 'attacks immediately' (9.4.12) runs its own 9.6.1 and halves again; this is the wide payoff the owner described and needs no new rule. Golden Bitter Toad's defensive mark: no rules issue either way. It only adds anything when the Toad is attacked (when the Toad attacks, its own damage already marks the defender), so it is a front-row trigger; giving it to every Toad pulls the Toad into the front row, keeping it golden-only keeps the Toad a back-row unit. A design call for the owner.

Budget. On an average late board Nightshade is worth about 5.5 SP a fight [guess], so the Tier-6 Sprite (+32%) and Tier-5 Toad (+33%) are over their +-15% band and need smaller bodies (4/5 and 3/7), while the Hornet Scout and the Nettled Tier-2 version fit; against a tall leader it is worth far more, as the owner intended.

  • Belladonna Sprite: flag over. T6 5/8: body 13 + Nightshade on the highest-Health enemy. On an average board at turns 9-12 that unit has about 13 Health (mean unit 10.4-17 SP), so the first halving takes about 6; it is attacked at least once in about 0.75 of fights (front 0.8, back 0.5); later halvings add about 0.5 because the first hit leaves it near death. About 5 Health removed x 1.1 (E15; Health loss is never blocked or wasted as overkill) = 5.5 [guess] = 18.5 vs V(6) 14: +32% (+-15%). Golden adds the second-highest (about 9 Health): +3.6, so the golden is 26 + 9.1 = 35.1 vs 28: +25%. Information: the price grows with the target, and against a tall leader (70 Health) the first halving alone takes 35. That is the owner's 'powerful effect', so sim it against TALL boards as well as average ones. Change: 4/5, same text (9 + 5.5 = 14.5, +4%; golden 18 + 9.1 = 27.1 vs 28, -3%). It stays safe in the back row with a small body, and all its power is in the mark.
  • Bitter Toad: flag over. T5 4/9: body 13 + marks whatever it trades with. A front unit has about 2 damaging exchanges a fight (1-1.5 attacks + 0.6-1.15 hits taken, and retaliation counts as dealing damage under 9.6.1 step 3); the enemy survives its 4 damage about half the time, and survivors are the big units (about 8 Health left at turn 8); a survivor is attacked again about 0.6 times, so the halving takes about 4 x 0.6 = 2.4 each; 2 x 0.5 x 2.4 + 0.3 for later halvings = 2.7 Health x 1.1 = 3.0 [guess] = 16.0 vs V(5) 12: +33% (+-15%). The mark lands after that exchange, so it only pays on later attacks. Information: Nightshade doesn't stack and retaliation already marks attackers, so the golden line adds only marks from pings and splash, about +0.3. Change: 3/7, same text (10 + 3.3 = 13.3, +11%; with 3 Attack slightly more enemies survive its hit and carry the mark). Golden 20 + 3.6 = 23.6 vs 24, -2%.
  • Hornet Scout: in band. T2 3/2: body 5 + Deathrattle 1/1 Hornetling in its own slot, 2 x 0.8 (E30) x 0.98 front death = 1.6 + attacks a Nightshaded enemy when there is one: blank until you own a Tier 5-6 applier, about 0.3 at its tier-open turn (C05) [guess] = 6.9 vs V(2) 7: -1% (+-15%). Information: in the Sprite build each directed Scout or Hornetling attack is worth about one halving (3-7 Health late), so the build's power sits in the pair. Sim the build, not the cards one at a time.
  • Nettle Hob (variation: Nettled): in band. T2 2/3: body 5 + one halving of the highest-Health enemy. At turns 2-4 that unit has about 4-5 Health, so the halving takes about 2, and it is attacked in about 0.9 of fights on 1-3-unit boards: 1.8 x 1.1 = 2.0 [guess] = 7.0 vs V(2) 7: 0% (+-15%). Information: it grows with the game. Kept to turns 9-12 it takes about 6 from a 13-Health unit (about +43% for a Tier-2 card), and 35 from a 70-Health leader. Sim it late, and the owner may want to know that the 'weaker low-tier' version still answers a tall leader with a Tier-2 card.

Bends. Damage is normally fixed by the attacker's Attack (9.6.1 step 3, 9.6.3). Nightshade makes every attack on the unit worth a share of its own Health, so the bigger the defender, the more each hit takes. It runs at the 'when attacked' step (9.6.1 step 1), before the damage exchange.

From owner, 2 Nov (02k). Creatures: Deadly nightshade (belladonna) fairies, the poison of hedge witches; bitter-skinned toads; hornets that follow the scent of a poisoned enemy..

For later

Questions for you

  1. Rounding when damage is halved. One rule for the whole game, or a choice per keyword? Rounding up means a 1-damage hit always lands (Hunker, Antlers, Whistle). Curl rounds down on purpose, so 1-Attack tokens can't hurt it.
  2. Turns that aren't attacks. May a unit spend its place in the attack order on something other than an attack (Bide, Whistle, War Dance, Curl, Nocturnal)? If so, proposed: a skip costs only that unit's attack, not its side's turn (shared rule 2), and a fight ends as a tie when a full cycle of both sides deals no damage, instead of running to the 200-attack limit (R3). Still open: do non-attack turns count toward that limit?
  3. What the board holds besides one unit per slot. Do you want non-unit objects in slots (Thicket, Brood's Egg, Bury's Nut, Fallow, Mark, Flood's water), and units that are in combat on no slot (Ride's riders, Burrow, Play Dead; Hibernate and Fly South between fights)? Proposed: one shared off-slot state, and one table (R7) saying what each kind counts for. And is 7 bodies a line to keep? Proposed: if riders may pass it, they stay at Tier 1–2 until simulated, since E23's +1 SP probably under-prices an eighth body.
  4. Reaching into the enemy's board. Which of these may effects do: put objects in enemy slots (Brood), move enemy units (Flush), make a unit fight for the other side (Changeling), permanently change the opponent's board (Shiny's steal)? Proposed: no permanent change to the opponent, since fights are against snapshots, so Shiny's victim loses the stats for that combat only. Changeling alone needs units to fight for the other side (R10): hold it back from v0, or keep it with a condition such as "only if the enemy unit across has 6 Attack or less" so it stays on a unit budget. If enemy moves stay in, should Start of Combat keep its random side order (9.3.2), which decides results like Rival against Changeling, or use a fixed order?
  5. Bending fixed slots and free moves. Three ideas break a placement rule. Caravan brings packing back for one tribe (8.3.4), Migrate makes a unit place itself each turn (8.3.3), and Aisle makes the slot a unit fought from pick which shop card it affects, so moves get a cost. Which are good rule-breakers, and which undercut fixed slots or become a chore?
  6. Owners for two open ideas. Golden chasing has no tribe yet (archetypes.md 1.3). Foundling, Pair and Mimic serve it: should one tribe own it, or neutral units? And should shared damage go to rats (Knot, Hedgerow folk) or Oozes (Linked)?
  7. Smaller rulings, as a set. Accept these defaults, or name the ones to change:
    • Reflected damage never triggers reflected damage. It becomes a system rule, which stops the Bristle, Curl and Both Ends chain (R2). Bristle reflects damage from attacks only, not enemy pings; Curl reflects all damage.
    • Protection beats reach: Shelter wins over Grudge, Rival, Nocturnal, Shiny and War Dance, and a reach unit whose target is protected attacks normally (R6).
    • Golden thresholds don't double, as 7.3.5 reads now: a golden Shrug, Shell or Cache keeps its N, so a golden Shell isn't weakened (R12).
    • Combat text that says "gains" with no duration means "this combat" (Slumber, Gorge, Alibi).
    • Thrift's "more gold than you started the turn with" counts this turn's income in the start. That fires on about 0.3 of turns; the "banked some gold" reading fires on about 0.7 and costs about 3 times as much.
    • Bargain's "if you can't pay" takes the gold you have as well as the Attack, so holding under 6 gold doesn't dodge the debt.
    • Pawn applies to Bought units only, so Created units still sell for 0, and pawning is limited to once per turn.
    • A Strong refresh spell from Brew is reviewed under G1 (R11), like any other unpaid refresh.
    • Sett goes to Tier 4 with the printed +1/+1 a turn, rather than Tier 2 with +1 Health a turn.
    • A third copy makes a Bonded pair golden at the normal golden size, not double the pair (four times one body). This is the reading that keeps it in budget; the review left it open.

Not asked here: Mimic, Endangered and Wager can't be judged until the async model (rulebook 2.1.4, 2.1.5) is settled.
